# Sources: Strength and Fitness

<!-- unread: McArdle, Hardcastle, Ekelund, LeSuer, Stricker -->

*Stage 1 research, 2026-09-24. Written before any lesson is drafted, by five researchers working in
parallel, each in a fresh context, each on one part of the subject (R1 to R5 below). Every passage in
double quotation marks in this file was copied from a document the researcher downloaded, and each
researcher checked every quotation by script against the saved text. Single quotation marks, where
they appear, mark a researcher's own words, never a source's. Each entry states its read level
("full text", "abstract only", "table of contents"), and **a lesson copies the read level from here,
never from memory** (drafting defect 22).*

**Cite by part and number**: "R2.6" is Part R2, entry 6. Parts R3 and R4 number some entries with a
letter suffix (R3.4c) or without the R (4.20 in Part R4 is R4.20).

**The `unread:` line is the build's enforcement.** McArdle, Katch and Katch's *Exercise Physiology*
was never opened, not even its contents page. Hardcastle, Ekelund, LeSuer and Stricker are papers
known only through another source's description. A lesson may not cite any of them. Many other
entries were read at abstract level only, and a lesson that uses them says so in the body at the
point of use.

**Boundaries with other Core courses, settled here so no lesson re-opens them.**
- **Nutrition: The Evidence** (T3, the course before this one) teaches energy balance, why a
  fixed-deficit rule overpredicts weight loss, constrained energy expenditure and what it implies
  for exercise and weight (its lesson 3), and the protein figures for people who train (its lesson
  5), and supplements in general (its lesson 10). **This course does not re-teach any of them.** It
  records the ACSM weight position (R5.27) and the guidelines' own hedges about weight (R1) only so
  that a lesson can state the boundary in one or two sentences and point to Nutrition.
- **Mental Fitness** lesson 5, "Exercise, asked three ways", teaches exercise for depression and
  anxiety. This course points there.
- **Sleep** teaches sleep and its measurement, including what consumer devices can see. This course
  covers only what wrist devices report about heart rate and calories (R3.9), and points there for
  sleep tracking.
- **First Aid and CPR** comes later in this term. This course's safety material is about when to stop
  and who to ask, not about treating anybody.

---

## The first things Stage 1 found, and they decide the course

**1. The big effect is starting; the small effects are the arguments.** Every synthesis read agrees
that almost any sensible resistance programme makes an untrained adult stronger than doing nothing
(R2.2, 137 systematic reviews; R2.3, 178 trials in which every prescription beat no exercise), and
that almost any aerobic training raises fitness (R3.4a). The variables people argue about (failure
or not, machines or free weights, periodised or not, HIIT or steady, how many days at a fixed volume)
make small or undetectable differences in the trials. **ACSM's 2026 resistance training position
stand (R2.2) is built on exactly this finding**, and it replaced a far more prescriptive 2009 stand
(R2.1), which makes the change itself a teaching case in how guidance moves.

**2. Who was trained, for how long, and measured how.** The resistance training literature is mostly
8 to 12 weeks in young, often male, untrained adults, tested on the one-repetition maximum of the
very lift they practised (R2.16), and strength gains are specific to what was practised (R2.5: d =
1.84 on trained tests against 0.8 on untrained ones). The HIIT literature's most famous result is 9
sedentary men in a laboratory for 12 weeks (R3.4c). **This is the question this course adds to the
institute's claim sort**, after Memory's sample, Focus's instrument, Note-Taking's setting, Sleep's
clock, Mental Fitness's comparison group and Nutrition's "instead of what?": *trained whom, for how
long, measured on what?*

**3. Three sentences that are routinely spoken as one.** (a) *Exercise changes the body*: fitness,
strength, blood pressure, glucose control. Experimental and established, in weeks. (b) *Active
people live longer*: observational, enormous, consistent, dose-responsive, graded "high certainty"
by WHO for association (R1.1). (c) *Becoming active will make you live longer*: the causal claim,
with the thinnest direct evidence. The one large randomised trial of exercise and death in a general
older population, Generation 100 (R1.9), found no difference in mortality, and its authors say
why and say the null does not refute a causal link. Twin analyses split (R1.10); a Mendelian
randomisation study leans causal with wide intervals (R1.11). **This is not a scandal and the course
must not present it as one**; it is the cleanest case on the Core of the difference between an
association and an effect, in a subject where the association is very strong.

**4. Every intensity number is an average dressed as a personal instruction.** 220 minus age was
never derived from a study (R3.3a); even the best formula misses a given person by about ±20 beats
(R3.3c); METs assume one resting rate for everyone (R3.2a). The talk test and a 0 to 10 effort scale
are the beginner's tools on the evidence read (R3.3e, R3.2b), and repetitions in reserve is the
lifter's, with people misjudging it by about a rep (R2.10).

**5. The risk concentrates in the inactive person who suddenly goes hard.** ACSM's screening model
(R4.1), the cardiac-event framing it rests on, the exertional rhabdomyolysis guideline and the
spin-class case series (R4.4) all point the same way. Lifting itself is not a high-injury activity
among competitors (R4.5: about 2 to 4 injuries per 1,000 hours against 15 to 81 for field sports,
with the population attached). The course's safety spine is "start below what you could do, and
build", and it carries a callout on every lesson built from the wording in R4.3 and R4.4.

**6. Practitioners are primary sources on how practitioners think.** Rippetoe's novice linear
progression (R5.8), Renaissance Periodization's volume landmarks (R5.9) and Stronger By Science's
reading of the volume evidence (R5.10) are recorded in their authors' own words, with their
commercial interests stated once, the way `docs/SOURCE_NOTES.md` treats Hormozi: a useful framework,
labelled as a framework, next to what the trials measured.

---

## Part R1. The guidelines and what they rest on: dose-response, observational versus causal

*Researcher 1, Stage 1, 2026-09-24. Every passage inside quotation marks below was copied from a
file downloaded in this session and saved under `SCRATCH/research/raw/r1/`, then checked by script
(whitespace-normalised `grep -F`) against that file. Where a PDF line break swallowed a hyphen
(pdftotext renders "muscle-strengthening" at a line end as "musclestrengthening"), the quotation
restores the hyphen and the entry says so. Read levels are stated for each source and are not
rounded up.*

---

## The first thing this section found

**The guidelines are built almost entirely on observational cohorts, the guideline writers say so,
and the single large randomised trial of exercise and death in a general population did not find
the effect the cohorts predict.** That is not a scandal and the course must not present it as one.
It is the most useful teaching fact in this section, because it separates three different
sentences a reader will meet:

1. *Exercise changes the body* (fitness, strength, blood pressure, glucose control). This is
   experimental and established: trials show it within weeks.
2. *Active people live longer and get fewer chronic diseases.* This is observational, enormous,
   consistent, dose-responsive, and graded "high certainty" by WHO (R1.1), but for association.
3. *Becoming active will make you, specifically, live longer.* This is the causal claim. It is
   the one the public hears, and it is the one with the thinnest direct evidence: one RCT
   (Generation 100, R1.9) that was null on its primary outcome for reasons its authors discuss
   candidly, one twin analysis that found no effect in identical twins (R1.10) and an earlier one
   by the same group that did (R1.10), and one recent Mendelian randomisation study that supports
   a causal effect with wide intervals (R1.11).

A second finding: **the shape of the curve is the message all three guideline bodies chose to
lead with.** WHO, the US and the UK all say, in nearly the same words, that the biggest gain comes
from going from nothing to something, and that there is no lower threshold. That is the sentence
the course can build a first lesson on, and it is honestly graded.

A third: **the UK CMO guidelines were revised on 10 July 2026** (R1.3). The recommendations are
unchanged but the text now mentions GLP-1 drugs and cites 2025 to 2026 evidence. Any lesson
quoting *the 2019 UK guidelines* should cite the 2026 HTML version that is now the live document.

---

## R1.1 WHO guidelines on physical activity and sedentary behaviour (2020)

**Citation.** World Health Organization. *WHO guidelines on physical activity and sedentary
behaviour.* Geneva: WHO; 2020. viii, 93 p. ISBN 9789240015128. Handle 10665/336656.
PDF retrieved via the IRIS DSpace API:
https://iris.who.int/server/api/core/bitstreams/faa83413-d89e-4be9-bb01-b24671aef7ca/content
(saved as `who2020.pdf`, text as `who2020.raw.txt` and `who2020.txt`).

**Read level.** Full text downloaded. Read closely: the adult, older-adult, pregnancy and
chronic-condition recommendation boxes and good practice statements; the adult "Supporting
evidence and rationale" section including the dose-response, bout-length and type/domain
discussions and the GDG conclusions; the methods passage on GRADE. Skimmed: children's sections.
Not read: disability sections, web annex evidence profiles (a separate document, not downloaded).

**Design.** A WHO guideline. The Guideline Development Group (GDG) used the US 2018 PAGAC
evidence synthesis (R1.4) and updated it with newer reviews: for adults "28 reviews and 3 pooled
cohort studies, published from 2017 through to November 2019". Certainty graded by GRADE.

**The adult recommendations, verbatim, with their grades** (checked):

- "All adults should undertake regular physical activity." Graded "Strong recommendation, moderate certainty evidence".
- "Adults should do at least 150–300 minutes of moderate-intensity aerobic physical activity; or at least 75–150 minutes of vigorous-intensity aerobic physical activity; or an equivalent combination of moderate- and vigorous-intensity activity throughout the week, for substantial health benefits." Strong recommendation, moderate certainty evidence.
- "Adults should also do muscle-strengthening activities at moderate or greater intensity that involve all major muscle groups on 2 or more days a week, as these provide additional health benefits." Strong recommendation, moderate certainty evidence.
- "Adults may increase moderate-intensity aerobic physical activity to more than 300 minutes; or do more than 150 minutes of vigorous-intensity aerobic physical activity; or an equivalent combination of moderate- and vigorous-intensity activity throughout the week for additional health benefits." Graded "Conditional recommendation, moderate certainty evidence". (Note: the "more is better" recommendation is the only conditional one.)

Good practice statements for adults (checked): "Doing some physical activity is better than doing
none." and "If adults are not meeting these recommendations, doing some physical activity will
benefit their health." and "Adults should start by doing small amounts of physical activity, and
gradually increase the frequency, intensity and duration over time."

**Older adults (65+).** The same aerobic and strengthening recommendations, plus (checked): "As
part of their weekly physical activity, older adults should do varied multicomponent physical
activity that emphasizes functional balance and strength training at moderate or greater
intensity, on 3 or more days a week, to enhance functional capacity and to prevent falls."
Strong recommendation, moderate certainty evidence. (Hyphenation note: the PDF breaks
"musclestrengthening" in the older adults box; the quotation above does not include that word.)
Good practice statement: "Older adults should be as physically active as their functional ability
allows, and adjust their level of effort for physical activity relative to their level of
fitness."

**Pregnant and postpartum women.** "It is recommended that all pregnant and postpartum women
without contraindication should:" then "undertake regular physical activity throughout pregnancy
and postpartum;" and "do at least 150 minutes of moderate-intensity aerobic physical activity
throughout the week for substantial health benefits; and" and "incorporate a variety of aerobic
and muscle-strengthening activities. Adding gentle stretching may also be beneficial." Each graded
strong, moderate certainty. Also: "Women who, before pregnancy, habitually engaged in
vigorous-intensity aerobic activity, or who were physically active, can continue these activities
during pregnancy and the postpartum period." Strong, moderate. Framing sentence: "These guidelines
are public health and population-based. Clinical guidance should be sought for women with
complications associated with pregnancy or delivery." (the PDF prints "populationbased" across a
line break; hyphen restored).

**Chronic conditions** (cancer survivors, hypertension, type-2 diabetes, HIV): the same 150 to 300
/ 75 to 150 and strengthening recommendations, strong, moderate certainty. Good practice
statement: "Pre-exercise medical clearance is generally unnecessary for individuals without
contraindications prior to beginning light- or moderate-intensity physical activity not exceeding
the demands of brisk walking or everyday living." (PDF prints "lightor moderate-intensity";
hyphen restored.) And: "Adults with these chronic conditions may wish to consult with a physical
activity specialist or health-care professional for advice on the types and amounts of activity
appropriate for their individual needs, abilities, functional limitations/ complications,
medications, and overall treatment plan."

**Sedentary behaviour, adults.** "Adults should limit the amount of time spent being sedentary.
Replacing sedentary time with physical activity of any intensity (including light intensity)
provides health benefits." Strong, moderate. And: "To help reduce the detrimental effects of high
levels of sedentary behaviour on health, adults should aim to do more than the recommended levels
of moderate- to vigorous-intensity physical activity." Strong, moderate. No sitting-time limit is
given; the BJSM summary (R1.2) says "evidence was insufficient to quantify a sedentary behaviour
threshold."

**The dose-response passage (the core of this section).** "Overall the evidence across
cardiovascular and metabolic health outcomes shows a consistent curvilinear inverse dose-response
relationship between physical activity and major outcomes such as all-cause mortality,
cardiovascular disease mortality, incident type-2 diabetes (67), and incident site-specific
cancers in adults." Then: "the shape of the dose-response curve indicates that there is no lower
threshold for benefit, and the greatest benefits are seen at the lower end of the dose-response
curve (65)."

Key figure cited: a meta-analysis of eight device-measured cohorts (their ref 65, Ekelund et al.
2019), "mean follow-up of 5.8 years (range 3–14.5 years)", adjusted HRs versus least active
quartile: "2nd quartile (adjusted HR= 0.48 [95% CI: 0.43 to 0.54]); 3rd quartile (adjusted HR=
0.34 [95% CI: 0.26 to 0.45]); and 4th quartile (adjusted HR= 0.27 [95% CI: 0.23 to 0.32])."
Also: "Maximal risk reductions for moderate- to vigorous-intensity physical activity were observed
at 24 minutes per day (equivalent to 168 minutes per week)". And at the top end: "although reduced
risk of mortality is observed up to 750 minutes of moderate- to vigorous-intensity physical
activity per week, the relative risk of mortality levels off beyond 300 minutes per week." (The raw PDF text prints "moderateto" across a line break; the layout text shows "moderate-" at the line end, so the hyphen is restored.)
*Teaching caution:* a hazard ratio of 0.27 for the most versus least active is an association in
device-measured cohorts, many of them older adults; the least active quartile will include people
who are inactive because they are already ill. The WHO text does not discuss this.

**Bouts.** "Previous WHO recommendations (1) concluded that aerobic activity should be performed
in bouts of at least 10 minutes duration. However, new evidence, using device-based assessments,
demonstrates that physical activity of any duration, without a minimum threshold, is associated
with improved health outcomes, including all-cause mortality (65, 82)." And: "Based on new
evidence, the recommendation for bouts of least 10 minutes duration has been removed." (sic: "of
least" is in the original.)

**GDG conclusions for adults, verbatim** (checked):
- "There is high certainty evidence that any level and any intensity of physical activity is associated with lower risk of all-cause mortality and cardiovascular disease mortality, incidence of hypertension, cardiovascular disease and type-2 diabetes."
- "There is evidence that more physical activity is associated with larger effects on health outcomes, although the relative benefits level off at higher levels of physical activity. There was insufficient evidence to identify the exact level where diminished returns start to occur."
- "There is moderate certainty evidence that physical activity of any duration is associated with improved health outcomes, and prior specification that aerobic activity should be performed in bouts of at least 10 minutes duration should be removed."
- "There is moderate certainty evidence that muscle-strengthening activities undertaken on 2 or more days a week, provide additional health benefits, but there is insufficient evidence to specify a specific duration for optimal health benefits." (PDF: "musclestrengthening"; hyphen restored.)
- "There is low certainty evidence that physical activity recommended for adults will not be harmful and that the health benefits from such activity outweigh the risks."

**Muscle-strengthening evidence cited.** Stamatakis et al. 2018, pooled 11 cohorts: meeting both
aerobic and strengthening guidelines versus neither, "adjusted HR= 0.71 [95% CI: 0.57 to 0.87]";
strength guideline alone versus not, "HR= 0.80 [95% CI: 0.70 to 0.91]". WHO: "These data affirm
that health benefits associated with muscle-strengthening exercise were independent of aerobic
physical activity and also provide evidence to support recommending a frequency of 2 days per
week of muscle-strengthening exercise." The WHO also says the dose-response for "volume and/or
intensity of aerobic physical activity and muscle-strength training and specific health outcomes"
needs more evidence.

**How the certainty was graded (important for the causal lesson).** "Observational evidence from
well-conducted longitudinal studies was also upgraded to reflect more appropriately the increased
certainty in findings regarding associations between physical activity or sedentary behaviour and
outcomes from such studies." (PDF prints "wellconducted"; hyphen restored.) The GRADE factors for
upgrading are listed as "magnitude of effect, dose-response, and effects of confounders". So
"high certainty" in this document is certainty about an association, upgraded for size and
dose-response; it is not a claim that trials have shown the effect.

**Risks.** "All physical activity comes with some risk." And: "Generally, the risks of adverse
events are very low with moderate-intensity physical activity and when increases in physical
activity frequency, intensity and duration are gradual (35)." And: "sudden cardiac adverse events
are rare and associated with acute sessions of relatively vigorous-intensity physical activity"
(PDF: "vigorousintensity"; restored).

**Weight (boundary only).** "Overall the evidence shows that higher levels of physical activity
may be associated with more favourable measures of adiposity and attenuation of weight gain in
adults (35). Further research is needed to establish consistent results and strength of
associations." The adult box: "measures of adiposity may also improve."

**Good for.** The recommendations with their exact grades; "some is better than none"; the curve
with no lower threshold; bouts of any length; the frank admission that observational evidence was
upgraded; weight claims hedged with "may".

**Does NOT show.** That activity causes the mortality reductions (it grades associations). A
specific muscle-strengthening duration. A sitting-time limit. Anything about individual
prescription.

**Classification.** The recommendations themselves are established (consensus of the relevant
expert body). The size of the mortality effect as causal is contested (see R1.9 to R1.11).

---

## R1.2 Bull et al. 2020, BJSM summary of the WHO guidelines

**Citation.** Bull FC, Al-Ansari SS, Biddle S, et al. World Health Organization 2020 guidelines on
physical activity and sedentary behaviour. *Br J Sports Med.* 2020;54(24):1451-1462.
doi:10.1136/bjsports-2020-102955. PMCID PMC7719906. Saved as `bull2020_bjsm.txt`.

**Read level.** Full text downloaded (PMC). Read: abstract and research-gaps paragraph. The
recommendations themselves were read from the primary WHO document (R1.1), which is the better
citation.

**Useful verbatim.** Abstract conclusion: "They reaffirm messages that some physical activity is
better than none, that more physical activity is better for optimal health outcomes and provide a
new recommendation on reducing sedentary behaviours." Results: "reducing sedentary behaviours is
recommended across all age groups and abilities, although evidence was insufficient to quantify a
sedentary behaviour threshold." Research gaps: "The most common need cited is more research on the
dose-response relationship between volume and/or intensity of physical activity and health
outcomes. Such information is key to establishing minimal effective doses and maximum safety
thresholds of physical activity for different population subgroups."

**Good for.** A peer-reviewed, citable summary written by the GDG itself, and its own statement
that minimal effective doses are not yet established.

---

## R1.3 UK Chief Medical Officers' Physical Activity Guidelines (2019, updated 10 July 2026)

**Citation.** Department of Health and Social Care. *UK Chief Medical Officers' physical activity
guidelines.* First published 7 September 2019; "Updated 10 July 2026". HTML:
https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/physical-activity-guidelines-uk-chief-medical-officers-report/uk-chief-medical-officers-physical-activity-guidelines
Saved as `uk_cmo.html` / `uk_cmo.txt`.

**Read level.** Full text of the current (2026) HTML version: foreword, executive summary, adult
and older adult guidelines and supporting evidence. The 2019 PDF is no longer served at its old
asset URL (returned "gone"); **the original 2019 wording was not read**. The gov.uk change note
says the 2026 revision was "reformatted in HTML and updated to include: more recent evidence in the
adult supporting evidence section; a revised introduction to reflect new evidence; a new foreword."
The text itself says the reviews "have allowed for updates to the supporting evidence for the
existing guidelines, without changing the overall guidance."

**Adult guidelines, verbatim** (checked, from the adult guideline section):
- "Adults should do activities to develop or maintain strength in the major muscle groups. This could include resistance exercises. Muscle strengthening activities should be done at least 2 days a week, but any strengthening activity is better than none." (The executive summary has the same sentence with "on at least 2 days a week".)
- "Each week, adults should aim to accumulate at least 150 minutes (2 and a half hours) of moderate intensity activity (such as brisk walking or cycling), or 75 minutes of vigorous intensity activity (such as running), or even shorter durations of very vigorous intensity activity (such as sprinting or stair climbing), or a combination of moderate, vigorous and very vigorous intensity activity."
- "Adults should aim to minimise the amount of time spent being sedentary and when physically possible should break up long periods of inactivity with at least light physical activity."

Note the order: **the UK puts strength first**, before the aerobic minutes. It also names a "very
vigorous" category that WHO and the US do not.

**Older adults, verbatim:** "Older adults should maintain or improve their physical function by
undertaking activities aimed at improving or maintaining muscle strength, balance and flexibility
on at least 2 days a week. These could be combined with sessions involving moderate aerobic
activity or could be additional sessions aimed specifically at these components of fitness."
And: "Some physical activity is better than none. Even light activity brings some health benefits
compared to being sedentary, while more daily physical activity provides greater health and social
benefits."

**The strength-and-balance rationale.** "Alongside general physical activity we all must maintain
our strength and balance." And: "in adults and older adults they help to maintain strength and
delay the natural decline in muscle mass and bone density which occurs from around 50 years of
age." The report describes a life-course figure (Figure 3) with "A capability threshold denotes the
level needed for healthy, independent life and mobility", and a second line where regular strength
and balance activity "peaks higher and later, resulting in a higher level of capability and a
longer duration above the capability line." This is a conceptual diagram, not a data plot; the
course may describe it as such.

**The curve, UK wording.** Foreword: "The health benefits of physical activity are significant
when we go from no activity to even some activity. Health gains then rapidly build to the point
when we are doing around 150 minutes a week. After this, benefits still increase but often at a
slower rate." And: "if you do no physical activity do some, and if you do some do a bit more."
And: "they are not absolute thresholds and we recognise the benefits that can be achieved at levels
both above and below 150 minutes per week." Bouts: "the 150 minutes of moderate to vigorous
physical activity can be accumulated in bouts of any length (see reference 125), and/or achieved
in one or 2 sessions per week while still leading to health benefits."

**Sedentary.** "Prolonged sitting is harmful, even in people who achieve the recommended levels of
moderate to vigorous physical activity. Despite this, the evidence does not currently support
including a specific time limit or minimum threshold of sedentary time within these guidelines."
(The first sentence is stated without a hedge; the evidence it rests on is observational. Record
as the CMOs' view, not as established causation.)

**Weight (boundary).** "The evidence reviewed suggests that greater than 150 minutes of physical
activity along with dietary restriction may be required for weight loss. Given the
interdependency of energy intake and expenditure, it is not possible to specify how much of an
increase in physical activity is likely to result in weight loss."

**New in 2026: GLP-1 drugs.** "When taking these both fat and muscle decrease." And: "Although
evidence is still emerging, physical activity including muscle strengthening activity is likely to
be of particular importance for maintaining muscle mass and preventing weight regain in those
taking, or who have recently taken, GLP-1 agonist drugs." Hedged ("still emerging", "likely");
keep the hedges if used.

**Rhetoric to handle carefully.** The foreword opens: "If physical activity were a drug, we would
refer to it as a miracle cure". This is a value/advocacy sentence from the CMOs, not a finding. A
lesson could quote it as an example of how public-health bodies speak, set beside R1.9.

**Good for.** Strength and balance emphasis; the plainest statement of the curve; "any
strengthening activity is better than none"; the weight boundary sentence.
