## R1.4 Physical Activity Guidelines for Americans, 2nd edition (2018), and the 2018 PAGAC Scientific Report

**Citations.** U.S. Department of Health and Human Services. *Physical Activity Guidelines for
Americans, 2nd edition.* Washington, DC: HHS; 2018.
https://health.gov/sites/default/files/2019-09/Physical_Activity_Guidelines_2nd_edition.pdf
(saved `pag2.pdf`, `pag2.raw.txt`). And: 2018 Physical Activity Guidelines Advisory Committee.
*2018 Physical Activity Guidelines Advisory Committee Scientific Report.* Washington, DC: HHS;
2018. https://health.gov/sites/default/files/2019-09/PAG_Advisory_Committee_Report.pdf (saved
`pagac.pdf`, `pagac.raw.txt`).

**Read level.** PAG: full text downloaded; read the key guidelines, chapter 1 sections on
progressing toward targets and bouts, chapter 2 all-cause mortality and Figure 2-1, the weight
management and musculoskeletal paragraphs, appendix 1 on steps. PAGAC: full text downloaded
(roughly 277,000 words); read only the all-cause mortality conclusion statements (Part F chapter
6), the steps and bouts conclusion statements (Part F chapter 1), and one paragraph on step counts
(Part D). The rest was searched by keyword, not read.

**Key guidelines for adults, verbatim** (checked):
- "Adults should move more and sit less throughout the day. Some physical activity is better than none. Adults who sit less and do any amount of moderate-to-vigorous physical activity gain some health benefits."
- "For substantial health benefits, adults should do at least 150 minutes (2 hours and 30 minutes) to 300 minutes (5 hours) a week of moderate-intensity, or 75 minutes (1 hour and 15 minutes) to 150 minutes (2 hours and 30 minutes) a week of vigorous-intensity aerobic physical activity, or an equivalent combination of moderate- and vigorous-intensity aerobic activity. Preferably, aerobic activity should be spread throughout the week."
- "Additional health benefits are gained by engaging in physical activity beyond the equivalent of 300 minutes (5 hours) of moderate-intensity physical activity a week."
- "Adults should also do muscle-strengthening activities of moderate or greater intensity and that involve all major muscle groups on 2 or more days a week, as these activities provide additional health benefits."

Older adults: "As part of their weekly physical activity, older adults should do multicomponent
physical activity that includes balance training as well as aerobic and muscle-strengthening
activities." And "Older adults should determine their level of effort for physical activity
relative to their level of fitness."

**The dose-response figure (Figure 2-1).** Titled "Relationship of Moderate-to-Vigorous Physical
Activity to All-Cause Mortality", adapted from Moore, Patel, Matthews 2012 *PLoS Med* (pooled
cohorts of leisure-time activity). Its annotations, as printed: "No lower threshold for benefit",
"Steep early slope", "About 70% of benefit reached by 8.25 MET-hours per week", "No obvious best
amount", "No evidence of increased risk at high end". Surrounding text: "As Figure 2-1 shows, a
large benefit occurs when a person moves from being inactive to being insufficiently active." And:
"Even at very high levels of physical activity (3 to 5 times the key guidelines), there is no
evidence of increased risk." And the headline number: "It has been estimated that people who are
physically active for approximately 150 minutes a week have a 33 percent lower risk of all-cause
mortality than those who are not physically active." *Caution:* "It has been estimated" is the
document's own hedge and the source is observational; a lesson must not restate the 33 percent as
what a person gains by starting.

**PAGAC conclusion statements (the grades behind the curve).** "Strong evidence demonstrates a
dose-response relationship between physical activity and all-cause mortality. The shape of the
curve is nonlinear, with the greatest benefit seen early in the dose-response relationship. The
relationship of moderate-to-vigorous physical activity and risk reduction has no lower limit." And
"Risk appears to continue to decrease with increased exposure up to at least three to five times
the amounts of the lower bound of moderate-to-vigorous physical activity recommended in the 2008
Guidelines (i.e., 150 minutes per week)." Graded "PAGAC Grade: Strong." Source of evidence
stated as "Systematic reviews, meta-analyses, pooled analyses" (of cohorts).

**Bouts.** PAG: "The 2018 Advisory Committee concluded that bouts of any length contribute to the
health benefits associated with the accumulated volume of physical activity. Even a brief episode
of physical activity like climbing up a few flights of stairs counts." PAGAC grade for this:
"Moderate evidence indicates that bouts of any length of moderate-to-vigorous physical activity
contribute to the health benefits associated with accumulated volume of physical activity. PAGAC
Grade: Moderate." PAG lists among what is new: "Elimination of the requirement for physical
activity of adults to occur in bouts of at least 10 minutes;".

**Progression and the inactive.** "Even small increases in moderate-intensity physical activity
provide health benefits. There is no threshold that must be exceeded before benefits begin to
occur." And: "For any given increase in moderate-to-vigorous physical activity, the relative gain
in benefits is greater for insufficiently active people than for people who are already meeting
the key guidelines." And on injury: "The risk of injury to bones, muscles, and joints is directly
related to the gap between a person’s usual level of activity and a new level of activity."
(Apostrophe: source uses a curly ’ in "person’s"; the check matched on the curly form.)

**Steps (PAGAC, 2018).** "Insufficient evidence is available to determine whether a relationship
exists between step counts per day and all-cause and cardiovascular disease mortality. PAGAC
Grade: Not assignable." PAG appendix: "Popular advice, such as walking 10,000 steps a day, is not a
guideline per se, but a way people may choose to meet the key guidelines." PAGAC Part D: the steps
equivalent to 150 to 300 minutes "varies from individual to individual and it may be less than the
commonly suggested 10,000 steps." (The 2022 meta-analysis in R1.7 postdates this "not assignable"
grade.)

**How few people meet it.** PAG foreword: "nearly 80 percent of adults are not meeting the key
guidelines for both aerobic and muscle-strengthening activity, while only about half meet the key
guidelines for aerobic physical activity." Chapter 1: "only 26 percent of men, 19 percent of women,
and 20 percent of adolescents report sufficient activity to meet the relevant aerobic and
muscle-strengthening guidelines". Self-report.

**Musculoskeletal.** "Progressive muscle-strengthening activities preserve or increase muscle mass,
strength, and power. Greater amounts (through higher frequency, heavier weights, or more resistance)
improve muscle function to a greater degree." (This is the experimental sense of *exercise works*,
and is well supported by trials; other researchers' sections cover it.)

**Weight (boundary).** "Many people need more than the equivalent of 150 minutes of
moderate-intensity activity a week to maintain their weight." And: "Combining both caloric
restriction and physical activity tend to be most beneficial for weight loss rather than just
caloric restriction or just physical activity." (sic "tend".) And: "The health benefits of physical
activity are generally independent of body weight." Point to Nutrition for energy balance.

**Piercy et al. 2018 JAMA** (the peer-reviewed summary). Piercy KL, Troiano RP, Ballard RM, et al.
The Physical Activity Guidelines for Americans. *JAMA.* 2018;320(19):2020-2028.
doi:10.1001/jama.2018.14854. PMCID PMC9582631. Saved `piercy2018_jama.txt`. Read level: abstract
and opening paragraph. Useful: "Approximately 80% of US adults and adolescents are insufficiently
active." And: "Evidence graded as strong or moderate was the basis of the key guidelines." And:
"Individuals performing the least physical activity benefit most by even modest increases in
moderate-to-vigorous physical activity."

---

## R1.5 ACSM position stand: Garber et al. 2011

**Citation.** Garber CE, Blissmer B, Deschenes MR, Franklin BA, Lamonte MJ, Lee IM, Nieman DC,
Swain DP; American College of Sports Medicine. Quantity and quality of exercise for developing and
maintaining cardiorespiratory, musculoskeletal, and neuromotor fitness in apparently healthy
adults: guidance for prescribing exercise. *Med Sci Sports Exerc.* 2011;43(7):1334-1359.
doi:10.1249/MSS.0b013e318213fefb. PMID 21694556.

**Read level. Abstract only** (Europe PMC, saved `garber2011_abstract.txt`; the same abstract on
the University of Rhode Island repository page, `uri.html`). The publisher page served only a
truncated summary; no free full text was found. **The body of the position stand, including its
tables of evidence categories, was not read.**

**Abstract, verbatim** (checked against the Europe PMC abstract, which drops superscripts, so
"min·d" there is min·d⁻¹ in the original; quotations below avoid those unit strings):
"The scientific evidence demonstrating the beneficial effects of exercise is indisputable, and the
benefits of exercise far outweigh the risks in most adults." And: "A program of regular exercise
that includes cardiorespiratory, resistance, flexibility, and neuromotor exercise training beyond
activities of daily living to improve and maintain physical fitness and health is essential for
most adults." Recommendations, paraphrased from the abstract because the unit strings are damaged:
moderate-intensity cardiorespiratory training at least 30 min a day on at least 5 days a week (at
least 150 min a week), or vigorous at least 20 min a day on at least 3 days (at least 75 min a
week), or a combination reaching at least 500 to 1000 MET-min a week. Verbatim: "On 2-3 d·wk,
adults should also perform resistance exercises for each of the major muscle groups, and neuromotor
exercise involving balance, agility, and coordination." (Europe PMC form; original has d·wk⁻¹.)
"Crucial to maintaining joint range of movement, completing a series of flexibility exercises for
each the major muscle-tendon groups (a total of 60 s per exercise) on ≥2 d·wk is recommended." (sic
"each the".) "Adults who are unable or unwilling to meet the exercise targets outlined here still
can benefit from engaging in amounts of exercise less than recommended." And on screening:
"Consultations with a medical professional and diagnostic exercise testing for CHD are useful when
clinically indicated but are not recommended for universal screening to enhance the safety of
exercise."

**Secondary summary of the tables (not the primary).** Len Kravitz, PhD (University of New
Mexico), "From 1998 to 2011: ACSM Publishes Updated Exercise Guidelines", PDF at
https://www.unm.edu/~lkravitz/Article%20folder/ACSMGuidelinesUNM.pdf (saved
`kravitz_acsm2011.pdf/.txt`). Read in full (6 pages). Kravitz reproduces the resistance table as:
"Major muscle groups should be trained 2-3 days/week with a 48-hour rest between sessions for
muscle groups"; intensity "40-50% of 1RM or very light to light load for beginning older persons" (the table continues the cell onto a second line, "and for beginning sedentary persons"), "60-70% of 1RM or moderate to hard load for novice to
intermediate adult exercisers", "≥80% of 1RM or hard to very hard load for experienced weight
lifters"; repetitions "8-12 repetitions to improve strength and power in most adults", "10-15
repetitions to improve strength in beginning, middle aged and older persons"; sets "2-4 sets are
recommended for strength and power of most adults" and "Single set training for novice and older
adults". Neuromotor: "Intensity Not determined at this time" and "The optimal volume of repetitions
and sets is currently unknown". Flexibility: "the relationship between flexibility and a reduction
of musculoskeletal injury has not been substantiated in the literature." **These are Kravitz's
rendering of Garber's tables, one remove from the primary**, and Kravitz gives the page range as
"1334-1349" where the journal record says 1334-1359. A lesson that uses these numbers must say
they are the ACSM's as summarised, or R2/R3 must supply the primary.

**Classification.** Consensus guidance from a professional body (established as ACSM's position,
not as settled science: the neuromotor rows are explicitly "Not determined at this time").

---

## R1.6 Muscle-strengthening and mortality: Momma et al. 2022; how few adults do it

**Citation.** Momma H, Kawakami R, Honda T, Sawada SS. Muscle-strengthening activities are
associated with lower risk and mortality in major non-communicable diseases: a systematic review
and meta-analysis of cohort studies. *Br J Sports Med.* 2022;56(13):755-763.
doi:10.1136/bjsports-2021-105061. PMCID PMC9209691. Saved `momma2022.txt`.

**Read level.** Full text (PMC): abstract, results for all-cause mortality and dose-response,
certainty paragraph, discussion and limitations.

**Design.** Systematic review and meta-analysis of prospective cohort studies; adults 18+ "without
severe health conditions"; exposure self-reported muscle-strengthening activity; outcomes all-cause
mortality, CVD, cancer, diabetes. MEDLINE and Embase to June 2021. 16 studies.

**Figures.** All-cause mortality, two-group analysis: "Seven studies with 42 133 cases of all-cause
mortality among 263 058 participants" ; "Muscle-strengthening activities were associated with a
15% lower risk of all-cause mortality (RR 0.85; 95% CI 0.79 to 0.93; p<0.001)". Heterogeneity
"I2=83.0%". Dose-response (six studies, 236 331 participants): "The lowest RR (RR 0.83; 95% CI 0.79
to 0.86) was observed at 40 min/week of muscle-strengthening activities, and the RR estimate for up
to approximately 140 min/week was <1.00." Combined strengthening and aerobic versus none, three
studies: "associated with a 40% lower risk of all-cause mortality (RR 0.60; 95% CI 0.54 to 0.67;
I2=59.3%)".

**Abstract verbatim.** "J-shaped associations with the maximum risk reduction (approximately
10–20%) at approximately 30–60 min/week of muscle-strengthening activities were found for all-cause
mortality, CVD and total cancer, whereas an L-shaped association showing a large risk reduction at
up to 60 min/week of muscle-strengthening activities was observed for diabetes." Conclusion:
"however, the influence of a higher volume of muscle-strengthening activities on all-cause
mortality, CVD and total cancer is unclear when considering the observed J-shaped associations."

**Certainty (the key caveat).** "The grading of the certainty of the evidence was generally very
low. The main reason for downgrading the evidence was indirectness because most of the studies
included in this review were conducted in the USA." Limitations include: "we could not test for
publication bias. Therefore, the pooled estimates in this study might have been overestimated
because of potential publication bias." And: "observational studies were included in the
meta-analysis and were thus potentially influenced by residual, unknown and unmeasured confounding
factors." On guidelines: "Although the recommendation is primarily based on the benefit for
musculoskeletal health,11–13 these guidelines are partly supported by our results in terms of
preventing premature death and NCDs." And: "the current recommendation of at least 2 days/week
could be reasonable, although a higher volume may require caution."

**Tension worth teaching.** WHO (R1.1) graded the strengthening-and-mortality link "moderate
certainty"; Momma et al. graded their pooled evidence "generally very low". Different evidence sets
and different GRADE judgements; both are honest; the lesson should show the reader that "the
certainty" is a judgement someone made.

**Does NOT show.** That lifting more than an hour a week is harmful. The J-shape's upturn rests on
few studies and wide intervals; the authors say "unclear". A lesson must not say *too much lifting
shortens life*.

**How few adults do it.**
- Bennie JA, Lee DC, Khan A, et al. Muscle-Strengthening Exercise Among 397,423 U.S. Adults:
  Prevalence, Correlates, and Associations With Health Conditions. *Am J Prev Med.*
  2018;55(6):864-874. doi:10.1016/j.amepre.2018.07.022. **Abstract only** (Europe PMC,
  `bennie2018_us_abstract.txt`). 2015 BRFSS telephone survey: "Overall, 30.2% (95% CI=29.9, 30.5)
  met the muscle-strengthening exercise recommendations (two or more times/week) and 57.8% (95%
  CI=57.5, 58.2) reported no muscle-strengthening exercise." Conclusion: "Three in five U.S. adults
  do not engage in any muscle-strengthening exercise".
- Bennie JA, et al. The epidemiology of muscle-strengthening exercise in Europe: A 28-country
  comparison including 280,605 adults. *PLoS One.* 2020;15(11):e0242220.
  doi:10.1371/journal.pone.0242220. PMCID PMC7688125. Full text downloaded; abstract read
  (`bennie2020_europe.txt`). European Health Interview Survey 2013 to 2014: "Overall, 17.3% (95% CI
  = 17.1%-17.5%) reported sufficient muscle-strengthening exercise (≥2 days/week)." Range "lowest
  prevalence reported in South-eastern European countries (Romania, Malta and Cyprus: range:
  0.7%-7.4%), and the highest prevalence in the Nordic countries (Iceland, Sweden, and Denmark:
  range: 34.1%-51.6%)."
- US national figure for 2020 (NCHS Data Brief No. 443, August 2022): 24.2 percent of adults met
  both aerobic and strengthening guidelines. **Search-result level only**: the CDC PDF returned
  "Access Denied" to curl. Do not quote without re-fetching.
- Abildso CG, et al. MMWR 2023;72(4) (PMC9925130, saved `mmwr2023_prevalence.txt`, read in part):
  NHIS 2020, leisure time; "a low proportion of U.S. adults met leisure-time aerobic,
  muscle-strengthening, and combined physical activity guidelines", lowest in the most rural
  counties "(nonmetropolitan; 38.2%, 21.1%, and 16.1%, respectively)" for aerobic,
  strengthening and combined.

All prevalence figures are self-reported survey data.

---

## R1.7 Steps: Paluch et al. 2022; Lee et al. 2019; where 10,000 came from

**Paluch 2022.** Paluch AE, Bajpai S, Bassett DR, et al.; Steps for Health Collaborative. Daily
steps and all-cause mortality: a meta-analysis of 15 international cohorts. *Lancet Public Health.*
2022;7(3):e219-e228. doi:10.1016/S2468-2667(21)00302-9. PMCID PMC9289978. Saved `paluch2022.txt`.
**Read level:** full text; abstract, introduction, age results, discussion, limitations.

Design: meta-analysis of participant-level data harmonised across 15 cohorts ("seven were published
and eight were unpublished"), device-measured steps over about one week at baseline, outcome
all-cause mortality. "The total sample included 47 471 adults, among whom there were 3013 deaths
(10.1 per 1000 participant-years) over a median follow-up of 7.1 years". Quartile medians "3553 for
quartile 1, 5801 for quartile 2, 7842 for quartile 3, and 10 901 for quartile 4." Adjusted HRs
versus quartile 1: "0.60 (95% CI 0.51–0.71) for quartile 2, 0.55 (0.49–0.62) for quartile 3, and
0.47 (0.39–0.57) for quartile 4." By age: "progressively decreasing risk of mortality among adults
aged 60 years and older with increasing number of steps per day until 6000–8000 steps per day and
among adults younger than 60 years until 8000–10 000 steps per day." Intensity: "We found
inconsistent evidence that step intensity had an association with mortality beyond total volume of
steps." On the origin of 10,000: "it is not based on evidence, but instead originates from a
marketing campaign in Japan." Limitations: "The data are derived from observational studies;
therefore, causal inferences cannot be made." and "residual confounding and reverse causality might
still be present." And on devices: "Step counts obtained from research and consumer devices are
highly correlated but can vary by 20% or more".

**Lee 2019.** Lee IM, Shiroma EJ, Kamada M, Bassett DR, Matthews CE, Buring JE. Association of Step
Volume and Intensity With All-Cause Mortality in Older Women. *JAMA Intern Med.*
2019;179(8):1105-1112. doi:10.1001/jamainternmed.2019.0899. PMCID PMC6547157. Saved
`lee2019_steps_women.txt`. **Read level:** full text; abstract, introduction, limitations,
conclusion.

Population: "16 741 women" from the Women's Health Study, "mean (SD) age was 72.0 (5.7) years",
accelerometer worn 7 days in 2011 to 2015, "mean follow-up of 4.3 years, 504 women died." Median
steps by quartile "2718, 4363, 5905, and 8442"; HRs "1.00 (reference), 0.59 (95% CI, 0.47-0.75),
0.54 (95% CI, 0.41-0.72), and 0.42 (95% CI, 0.30-0.60)". "In spline analysis, HRs were observed to
decline progressively with higher mean steps per day until approximately 7500 steps/d, after which
they leveled." Conclusion: "Among older women, as few as approximately 4400 steps/d was
significantly related to lower mortality rates compared with approximately 2700 steps/d."
Limitations: "reverse causation bias remains a possibility" and "Women in the WHS are primarily
white, of higher socioeconomic status, and more active".

**Origin of 10,000 steps**, from Lee 2019, hedged: "However, the origin of the goal of 10 000 steps
per day is unclear. It likely derives from the trade name of a pedometer sold in 1965 by Yamasa
Clock and Instrument Company in Japan called Manpo-kei, which translates to “10 000 steps meter” in
Japanese.5,6" Keep "is unclear" and "likely". Paluch 2022 (same research network) states it more
flatly ("originates from a marketing campaign in Japan"), citing Lee. **No primary source (a 1965
advertisement or Yamasa record) was read.** Lee's refs 5 and 6 were not opened.

**Tudor-Locke et al. 2011.** Tudor-Locke C, Craig CL, Brown WJ, et al. How many steps/day are
enough? For adults. *Int J Behav Nutr Phys Act.* 2011;8:79. doi:10.1186/1479-5868-8-79. PMCID
PMC3197470. Saved `tudorlocke2011_adults.txt`. Read: the summary paragraph on MVPA-equivalent steps.
"directly studied estimates of free-living behaviour suggest that a total daily volume of
ambulatory physical activity associated with meeting minimal amounts of MVPA is at least
7,000-8,000 steps/day." Does not discuss the 1965 origin in the passages read.

**Good for.** A clean *where did this number come from?* lesson: a marketing number that turned out
to be in the right region for younger adults and above the plateau for older ones, in observational
data. **Does NOT show** that taking more steps causes longer life, or that 7,500 is a ceiling for
benefits other than mortality.

---

## R1.8 Cardiorespiratory fitness and mortality: Kodama 2009; Mandsager 2018

**Kodama 2009.** Kodama S, Saito K, Tanaka S, et al. Cardiorespiratory fitness as a quantitative
predictor of all-cause mortality and cardiovascular events in healthy men and women: a
meta-analysis. *JAMA.* 2009;301(19):2024-2035. doi:10.1001/jama.2009.681. **Abstract only**
(Europe PMC, `kodama2009_abstract.txt`).

Meta-analysis of observational cohorts of healthy participants, fitness measured as maximal
aerobic capacity in METs. "Data were obtained from 33 eligible studies (all-cause mortality, 102
980 participants and 6910 cases; CHD/CVD, 84 323 participants and 4485 cases). Pooled RRs of
all-cause mortality and CHD/CVD events per 1-MET higher level of MAC (corresponding to 1-km/h
higher running/jogging speed) were 0.87 (95% confidence interval [CI], 0.84-0.90) and 0.85 (95% CI,
0.82-0.88), respectively." Categories: "low CRF (< 7.9 METs), intermediate CRF (7.9-10.8 METs), or
high CRF (> or = 10.9 METs)". Low versus high: "RR for all-cause mortality of 1.70 (95% CI,
1.51-1.92; P < .001)". Conclusion wording is associational: "Better CRF was associated with lower
risk of all-cause mortality and CHD/CVD."

**Mandsager 2018.** Mandsager K, Harb S, Cremer P, Phelan D, Nissen SE, Jaber W. Association of
Cardiorespiratory Fitness With Long-term Mortality Among Adults Undergoing Exercise Treadmill
Testing. *JAMA Netw Open.* 2018;1(6):e183605. doi:10.1001/jamanetworkopen.2018.3605. PMCID
PMC6324439. Saved `mandsager2018.txt`. **Read level:** full text; abstract, methods, results
summary, limitations.

Design: "This retrospective cohort study enrolled patients at a tertiary care academic medical
center from January 1, 1991, to December 31, 2014, with a median follow-up of 8.4 years."
Population: "Consecutive adult patients referred for symptom-limited exercise treadmill testing"
(Cleveland Clinic), "122 007 patients (mean [SD] age, 53.4 [12.6] years; 72 173 [59.2%] male)".
Fitness: peak estimated METs on one treadmill test. Results: "elite vs low: adjusted hazard ratio
[HR], 0.20; 95% CI, 0.16-0.24" and "low vs elite: adjusted HR, 5.04; 95% CI, 4.10-6.20", which
"was comparable to or greater than traditional clinical risk factors" (smoking "adjusted HR, 1.41;
95% CI, 1.36-1.46"). Conclusion: "Cardiorespiratory fitness is inversely associated with long-term
mortality with no observed upper limit of benefit."

**Its own limitations, which undercut the headline.** "The primary limitation of the study reflects
its retrospective nature, in that the association between CRF and mortality does not prove
causation. The degree to which high CRF preselects patients with lower mortality vs causes a
reduction in mortality is not discernible from our study." And: "our study demonstrates that
patients who can exercise to an extreme level live longest, acknowledging that there may be many
measured and unmeasured factors that contribute to this association." Yet the abstract also calls
fitness "a modifiable indicator of long-term mortality". **Teaching use:** the same paper contains
both the popular sentence and the sentence that limits it. The population was patients referred
for a clinical stress test, not the general public; socioeconomic status was not adjusted for.
Also note fitness is partly inherited (R1.10), so *fitness predicts death* is not the same claim as
*training raises fitness and so prevents death*.

---

## R1.9 The trial: Generation 100 (Stensvold et al. 2020, BMJ)

**Citation.** Stensvold D, Viken H, Steinshamn SL, et al. Effect of exercise training for five
years on all cause mortality in older adults, the Generation 100 study: randomised controlled
trial. *BMJ.* 2020;371:m3485. doi:10.1136/bmj.m3485. PMCID PMC7539760. Saved
`stensvold2020_gen100.txt`.

**Read level.** Full text: abstract, introduction, methods (design, participants, sample size),
mortality results, discussion, limitations, conclusion.

**Design.** RCT in Trondheim, Norway. All residents aged 70 to 77 invited (n=6966); "1567 (790
women)" enrolled. Randomised 2:1:1 to control (asked to follow national guidelines, "30 minutes of
moderate level physical activity almost every day", n=780), MICT at about 70% of peak heart rate
(n=387) or HIIT at about 90% of peak heart rate (n=400), two supervised-or-guided sessions a week,
for five years. Primary outcome: all-cause mortality. "An exploratory hypothesis was that HIIT
lowers mortality more than MICT."

**What the training arms did, and who was excluded (added 2026-09-24 from the primary, Methods,
at lesson 1's Stage 4 review, F17 and F24).** The training arms "were asked to exchange two of the
five 30 minute moderate intensity physical activity sessions each week (as recommended by Norwegian
health authorities) with two HIIT sessions" or two MICT sessions. "Every sixth week both groups met
separately for supervised spinning sessions (ergometer cycling) with an exercise physiologist", and
"Supervised training with exercise physiologists present was also offered twice" a week: offered,
not required. Exclusion criteria "before and during the study were uncontrolled hypertension
(untreated systolic blood pressure >220 mm Hg, or diastolic blood pressure >110 mm Hg); symptomatic
valvular disease; hypertrophic cardiomyopathy; unstable angina pectoris; primary pulmonary
hypertension; heart failure; severe arrhythmia; diagnosed dementia; cancer that made participation
impossible; chronic communicable infectious diseases, illness, or disabilities that precluded
exercise; or participation in other exercise trials." So the harms sentence below applies to
screened volunteers.

**Power assumption versus reality.** "With an expected 10% mortality rate after five years, we
determined that 600 participants would be needed in the combined MICT and HIIT group and control
group to detect a 50% reduction in mortality (from 10% to 5%)". Observed: "The observed mortality
rate after five years was 4.6% (n=72), with 4.7% (n=37) in the control group, 4.5% (n=35) in the
combined MICT and HIIT group, 5.9% (n=23) in the MICT group, and 3.0% (n=12) in the HIIT group".
At baseline "87.5% of participants reported to have overall good health, with 80% reporting medium
or high physical activity levels".

**Results.** "All cause mortality did not differ between the control group and combined MICT and
HIIT group." HIIT versus control "hazard ratio 0.63, 95% confidence interval 0.33 to 1.20"; MICT
versus control "(1.24, 0.73 to 2.10)"; HIIT versus MICT "(0.51, 0.25 to 1.02)". Table 3, the
primary comparison, combined MICT+HIIT against control: unadjusted "0.94 (0.59 to 1.41)", adjusted
"0.92 (0.58 to 1.47)". Table 3 also gives HIIT against MICT unadjusted "0.49 (0.25 to 0.99)" (the
0.51 above is the adjusted figure, the one the abstract reports). Peak oxygen uptake
at five years was "0.3 mL/kg/min (P=0.17) higher in the combined MICT and HIIT group compared with
the control group", and no group showed the expected age decline: "We observed no decline in peak
oxygen uptake in any group".

**What its authors concluded, verbatim.** Abstract: "This study suggests that combined MICT and
HIIT has no effect on all cause mortality compared with recommended physical activity levels.
However, we observed a lower all cause mortality trend after HIIT compared with controls and MICT."
Final conclusion: "Compared with the Norwegian recommended guidelines for physical activity,
supervised exercise (HIIT and MICT combined) showed no effect on all cause mortality,
cardiovascular disease events, or cancer events in older adults. Our data do, however, suggest that
HIIT lowers the risk of premature death compared with recommended guidelines and MICT." *Note:*
that second sentence rests on an exploratory comparison whose confidence intervals include no
effect (0.33 to 1.20; 0.25 to 1.02). A lesson may quote it but must set the intervals beside it.

**Their own account of why it may have been null.** "participants in the control group had a high
level of activity throughout the study, and many exercised using HIIT." "only 50% of participants
in the HIIT group were able to follow the strict criteria for fulfilling the HIIT protocol." "The
healthy volunteer bias might have moderated the effects of exercise on the outcomes." And, the key
sentence for the causal lesson: "the null results from this study do not refute a causal
association between physical activity and longevity." Also: "mortality in the control group was
less than half compared with the 10% death rate in the general population of older adults in
Norway over the five year study period." They go further than the data in one place: "Although
selection bias might play a role, and we cannot fully rule out that the low mortality rate was due
to selection, not the intervention, our results indicate that all three interventions had an
effect and reduced the risk of premature death." (Without an untreated arm this cannot be tested;
record as the authors' interpretation.)

Why trials are rare, in their words: "Real life randomised exercise trials with sufficient
statistical power covering adults of most ages is challenging because a large sample size and long
and costly follow-up is needed as death is relatively rare in young and middle aged people." And:
"Sceptics of the benefits from physical activity have argued that the guideline recommendations
lack the high quality evidence provided by randomised clinical trials."

Harms: "Three participants (two in MICT group, one in HIIT group) experienced fracture injuries
while exercising on a slippery surface during outdoor training. No cardiovascular disease related
events occurred during supervised exercise sessions."

**Good for.** The honest centre of a lesson on what the guidelines rest on: the one direct test
was underpowered by its own success at recruiting healthy, active people, and its control group
exercised. **Does NOT show** that exercise does not lower mortality; its authors say so.

---

## R1.10 Twin studies: Kujala et al. 1998 and Karvinen et al. 2015 (Finnish Twin Cohort)

**Kujala 1998.** Kujala UM, Kaprio J, Sarna S, Koskenvuo M. Relationship of leisure-time physical
activity and mortality: the Finnish twin cohort. *JAMA.* 1998;279(6):440-444.
doi:10.1001/jama.279.6.440. **Abstract only** (Europe PMC, `kujala1998_abstract.txt`).

"7925 healthy men and 7977 healthy women" aged 25 to 64 in 1975, followed for deaths 1977 to
1994. Whole cohort, versus sedentary: "0.71 (95% confidence interval [CI], 0.62-0.81) in occasional
exercisers and 0.57 (95% CI, 0.45-0.74) in conditioning exercisers". Within twin pairs discordant
for death (n=434): "the odds ratio for death was 0.66 (95% CI, 0.46-0.94) in occasional exercisers
and 0.44 (95% CI, 0.23-0.83) in conditioning exercisers compared with those who were sedentary".
Conclusion: "Leisure-time physical activity is associated with reduced mortality, even after
genetic and other familial factors are taken into account." **This is the proponent side from a
twin design.**

**Karvinen 2015.** Karvinen S, Waller K, Silvennoinen M, et al. Physical activity in adulthood:
genes and mortality. *Sci Rep.* 2015;5:18259. doi:10.1038/srep18259. PMCID PMC4678877. Saved
`karvinen2015_twins.txt`. **Read level:** full text of the article body (abstract, introduction,
human results, discussion); methods skimmed.

Same research group, longer follow-up, stricter exposure (persistent vigorous activity reported in
1975, 1981 and 1990; mortality to July 2013). Individual-level: fully adjusted HR "0.73
(0.61–0.88)". Pairs discordant for persistent vigorous activity: "A pairwise analysis of these 179
twin pairs showed that the mortality HR for persistent vs. non-persistent vigorous activity was
0.65 (0.46–0.91), and after adjusting for all covariates including health status, the HR was 0.72
(0.48–1.07)." DZ pairs "HR = 0.58, 95% CI: 0.39–0.88"; **MZ pairs "HR = 1.00, 95% CI: 0.52–1.94"**,
34 pairs. The authors note: "The heritability of physical activity (see below) contributed to the
statistical power of the analysis among MZ pairs." Heritability of leisure activity estimated at
"53% (95% CI: 46–59%)". A secondary analysis with more pairs gave MZ "0.85 [0.56–1.30]". From the
primary (added 2026-09-24, lesson 1's Stage 4 review, F9): that analysis used pairs discordant in
1975 and 1981 only ("shorter PA discordance at baseline"), "778 DZ and 231 MZ vigorous activity
discordant twin pairs", with 55 deaths among the MZ pairs; the 0.85 is "Unadjusted pairwise HR".
"When this secondary analysis was repeated in the baseline-healthy subgroup the HRs were 0.64
(0.45–0.89) for DZ pairs and 1.05 (0.58–1.88) for MZ pairs, respectively."

Their framing, verbatim: "Despite suggestive evidence from population-based associations,
scientists have not been able to show a beneficial effect of physical activity on the risk of death
in controlled intervention studies among individuals who have been healthy at baseline." And: "we
propose that genetic pleiotropy might partly explain the frequently observed associations between
high baseline physical activity and later reduced mortality in humans." And, keeping the other
side: "It is to note that randomized controlled trials show that vigorous physical activity has
other health benefits such as improvement of both self-reported and objectively reported physical
functioning and reduction of depression". The paper also reports a rat experiment in which
voluntary wheel running shortened lifespan in female rats of both bred strains. **Corrected
2026-09-24 (lesson 1's Stage 4 review, F2): this entry used to say the authors "do not ask" for the
rat result to be carried to humans. They do.** Abstract: "Based on both our animal and human
findings, we propose that genetic pleiotropy might partly explain the frequently observed
associations between high baseline physical activity and later reduced mortality in humans", and
"Here, in both animals and/or human twins, we show that the same genetic factors influence physical
activity levels, cardiorespiratory fitness, and risk of death." They say their "controlled
interventional study with laboratory rats bred for low and high intrinsic fitness contrast[s]" with
the human cohorts. The introduction adds: "Based on past studies on elite athletes and twins, we
previously suggested that genetic pleiotropy may explain at least some of the observed association".
So the sceptical case rests on rats, twins and earlier athlete studies, not on the 34 MZ pairs alone.
A lesson may decline to carry a rat result to humans on its own account, but must say the authors
use it.

**Caveats.** 34 MZ pairs is small; an HR of 1.00 with an interval of 0.52 to 1.94 is compatible
with a large benefit or none. Absence of evidence in a small sample, not evidence of absence.
Self-reported activity.

---

## R1.11 Reverse causation and Mendelian randomisation

**Lee DH et al. 2021.** Lee DH, Rezende LFM, Ferrari G, et al. Physical activity and all-cause and
cause-specific mortality: assessing the impact of reverse causation and measurement error in two
large prospective cohorts. *Eur J Epidemiol.* 2021;36(3):275-285. doi:10.1007/s10654-020-00707-3.
PMCID PMC8035269. Saved `leeDH2021_reversecausation.txt`. **Read level:** abstract.

Nurses' Health Study and Health Professionals Follow-up Study, "A total of 133,819 participants",
repeated biennial questionnaires, "47,273 deaths". HR per 10 MET-hours/week: "0.95 (0.94–0.96) for
baseline PA, 0.78 (0.77–0.79) for simple updated PA and 0.87 (0.86–0.88) for cumulative average
PA. Simple updated PA showed the strongest inverse association, suggesting larger impact of reverse
causation." And: "Application of 2-year lag substantially reduced reverse causation". Conclusion:
"Cumulative average PA showed robust evidence that PA is inversely associated with mortality in a
dose-response manner." **Good for:** showing that reverse causation (sick people stop exercising)
is real and measurable, and that epidemiologists who take it seriously still find an association.
This is the best proponent-side methods paper read for this section.

**Jiesisibieke and Schooling 2026 (MR).** Jiesisibieke ZL, Schooling CM. Assessing the causal
impact of leisure-time physical activity and screen time on lifespan: a Mendelian randomization
study. *Eur Rev Aging Phys Act.* 2026;23(1):14. doi:10.1186/s11556-026-00406-0. PMCID PMC13088598.
Saved `jiesisibieke2026_MR.txt`. **Read level:** abstract, methods paragraphs on assumptions,
results summary, part of discussion.

Design: two-sample MR, European-ancestry summary statistics, 16 SNPs for leisure-time MVPA; outcome
"Lifespan was proxied by parental attained age" (UK Biobank). Results: "Leisure-time moderate to
vigorous intensity physical activity was associated with longer lifespan for men (1.41 years per
doubling the odds of being physically active in leisure time, 95% confidence interval (CI) 0.21 to
2.62) and women (1.68 years, 95% CI 0.12 to 3.25)." Conclusion, hedged: "Leisure-time moderate to
vigorous intensity physical activity may extend lifespan, while the role of leisure-time screen
time is less clear." The instruments explained little of the exposure ("0.45%" of variance for
MVPA). The authors cite an earlier MR as "underpowered" (odds ratio for reaching the 90th survival
percentile "1.89, 95% CI 0.53 to 6.70"). **Caveats:** a single, recent study; wide intervals;
parental lifespan as proxy; MR assumptions (no pleiotropy) cannot be fully verified. **Added
2026-09-24 (lesson 1's Stage 4 review, F3), from the methods and results: the authors tested for
pleiotropy rather than assuming it away.** "We used sensitivity analysis to assess horizontal
pleiotropy (exclusion restriction)" (weighted median, MR-Egger, MR-PRESSO, and multivariable MR
adjusting for education and lifestyle factors). "The p-value for the MR-Egger intercept did not show
violations of the exclusion restriction assumption". Against that: "MR-Egger estimates differed from
the IVW estimates; MR-Egger is sensitive to random measurement error and weak instrument bias, given
the I 2 GX was low, indicating lack of the instrument strength for MR-Egger." These checks cannot
rule pleiotropy out. The abstract
"Objectives" paragraph in the saved PMC text contains a garbled insertion ("confirmAuthors’
contributions the value"), so that sentence is not quoted.

---

## R1.12 Patterns: VILPA (Stamatakis 2022) and the "weekend warrior" (O'Donovan 2017)

**Stamatakis 2022.** Stamatakis E, Ahmadi MN, Gill JMR, et al. Association of wearable
device-measured vigorous intermittent lifestyle physical activity with mortality. *Nat Med.*
2022;28(12):2521-2529. doi:10.1038/s41591-022-02100-x. PMCID PMC9800274. Saved
`stamatakis2022_vilpa.txt`. **Read level:** full text of abstract, introduction, discussion and
limitations; methods on reverse-causation exclusions.

Design: UK Biobank accelerometry substudy, wrist device, machine-learning classification;
"25,241 nonexercisers (mean age 61.8 years, 14,178 women/11,063 men)", "average follow-up of 6.9
years, during which 852 deaths occurred". Results: at the median of "3 length-standardized bouts
per day (lasting 1 or 2 min each)" the abstract reports "a 38%–40% reduction in all-cause and
cancer mortality risk and a 48%–49% reduction in CVD mortality risk". Design-limit language:
"Although we cannot entirely rule out reverse causation bias, our results were very robust to
relevant sensitivity analyses." Deaths in the first 2 years were excluded. **Caution for lessons:**
the abstract says "reduction" and "elicit", causal-sounding verbs, in an observational study; the
conclusion paragraph says "associated with". Use the associational wording. VILPA was not observed
directly; it was inferred from wrist movement among people who said they did no leisure exercise
about 5.5 years earlier.

**O'Donovan 2017.** O'Donovan G, Lee IM, Hamer M, Stamatakis E. Association of "Weekend Warrior"
and Other Leisure Time Physical Activity Patterns With Risks for All-Cause, Cardiovascular Disease,
and Cancer Mortality. *JAMA Intern Med.* 2017;177(3):335-342. doi:10.1001/jamainternmed.2016.8014
(**note: the DOI ends 8014, not 7014 as in the brief**). PMID 28097313. **Abstract only** (PubMed,
`odonovan2017_abstract.txt`). An erratum was published in 2022 (JAMA Intern Med 2022;182(5):579);
**its content was not read**, and the abstract's sex split ("45.9% male; 44.1% female") does not sum
to 100, which may be what it corrects. Do not quote the sex split.

Design: pooled Health Survey for England and Scottish Health Survey, 11 cohorts, adults 40+,
self-reported leisure activity at one time point, mortality linkage, 1994 to 2012. "Among the 63
591 adult respondents ... 8802 deaths from all causes". HRs versus inactive: "0.66 (95% CI,
0.62-0.72) in insufficiently active participants who reported 1 to 2 sessions per week, 0.70 (95%
CI, 0.60-0.82) in weekend warrior participants, and 0.65 (95% CI, 0.58-0.73) in regularly active
participants." Conclusion, hedged: "Weekend warrior and other leisure time physical activity
patterns characterized by 1 or 2 sessions per week may be sufficient to reduce all-cause, CVD, and
cancer mortality risks regardless of adherence to prevailing physical activity guidelines."
(The quotation of the sample size above uses an ellipsis; the full sentence is in the saved file.)
**Design limits:** single self-report, "weekend warrior" group small, observational. Good for:
the guidelines' *or one or two sessions* (UK) and "spread throughout the week" as a preference, not
a requirement.

---

## What this section found that should shape the course

- **Lead with the curve, not the number.** All three guideline bodies say the biggest gain is from
  none to some, with no lower threshold (WHO R1.1, PAG and PAGAC R1.4, UK R1.3). That is graded
  strong/high for association and is the least-misleading first message.
- **Say out loud what "high certainty" means.** WHO upgraded observational evidence for size and
  dose-response (R1.1). The lesson on *what the guidelines rest on* should teach the three
  sentences (changes the body / active people live longer / you will live longer) and which kind
  of study licenses each.
- **Generation 100 is the course's set-piece** for observational versus causal (R1.9): a real
  trial, a null primary result, authors who explain why and say it does not refute causation. Pair
  it with the twin studies (R1.10), the reverse-causation methods paper (R1.11) and the MR study
  (R1.11) so each side speaks.
- **Strength belongs in lesson one, not as an add-on.** Every guideline includes 2 or more days of
  muscle-strengthening; the UK lists it first; only about 17 to 30 percent of adults report meeting
  it (R1.6). The mortality evidence for strength is thinner and graded lower than for aerobic
  activity (Momma "very low" versus WHO "moderate"); the course should say both.
- **Bouts of any length count**, and one or two sessions a week are associated with most of the
  benefit (WHO, PAG, UK; O'Donovan R1.12). This removes a common barrier and is well sourced.
- **Steps: a marketing number that landed close.** 10,000 has no evidential origin (Lee, Paluch);
  mortality curves level around 6,000 to 8,000 for 60+ and 8,000 to 10,000 under 60 (R1.7), in
  observational data.
- **Fitness is the strongest predictor, and partly inherited** (Kodama, Mandsager R1.8; Karvinen
  R1.10). A lesson on fitness tests must not slide from *fit people live longer* to *training will
  make you live as long as them*.
- **Weight is a boundary.** Guidelines hedge activity-for-weight-loss heavily ("may", "not possible
  to specify") and say benefits are "generally independent of body weight" (PAG). Point to
  Nutrition.
- **Guidelines are population advice, not prescription.** WHO's pregnancy and chronic-condition
  boxes defer to clinicians; PAG and WHO say effort should be judged relative to one's own fitness.
  The course's closing refusal can rest on this.
- **Cite the 2026 UK text, not the 2019 PDF** (R1.3).

## Misconceptions this section can correct

- *You need 10,000 steps a day.* Lee 2019 and Paluch 2022 (R1.7); PAG appendix: "is not a
  guideline per se".
- *Exercise only counts in sessions of 10 minutes or more.* Removed by WHO 2020 and PAG 2018
  (R1.1, R1.4).
- *If you can't do 150 minutes, there's no point.* WHO good practice statements, PAG "There is no
  threshold", UK "not absolute thresholds" (R1.1, R1.3, R1.4).
- *The guidelines are just about cardio.* All three include strengthening on 2 or more days
  (R1.1, R1.3, R1.4).
- *It's proven by trials that exercise makes you live longer.* The one general-population RCT was
  null on its primary outcome (R1.9); the evidence is overwhelmingly observational (R1.1 methods).
- *It's all confounding, exercise doesn't really extend life.* Also not what the evidence says:
  Gen 100 authors say the null does not refute causation, Lee 2021 finds a robust association after
  handling reverse causation, MR suggests a causal effect (R1.9, R1.11).
- *Doing all your exercise at the weekend is useless.* O'Donovan 2017 (R1.12), UK guidelines
  "one or 2 sessions per week" (R1.3).
- *More strength training is always better for longevity.* Momma 2022: J-shape, benefit
  plateau around 30 to 60 min/week, "unclear" above that, very low certainty (R1.6).
- *Exercise is mainly for weight loss.* PAG: benefits "generally independent of body weight";
  UK: "not possible to specify how much of an increase in physical activity is likely to result in
  weight loss" (R1.3, R1.4).
- *You need a doctor's clearance before starting to walk.* WHO chronic-conditions good practice
  statement; ACSM: testing "not recommended for universal screening" (R1.1, R1.5).

## Contested questions in this section

**1. Does physical activity cause longer life, or do healthy, genetically favoured people both
exercise more and live longer?** *Classification: contested (empirical).*
- *Causal side, in proponents' words:* WHO graded the association "high certainty" (R1.1). Kujala
  1998: "associated with reduced mortality, even after genetic and other familial factors are taken
  into account" (R1.10). Lee DH 2021: "Cumulative average PA showed robust evidence that PA is
  inversely associated with mortality in a dose-response manner" (R1.11). Jiesisibieke and
  Schooling 2026: activity "may extend lifespan" (R1.11). Gen 100 authors: the null "do not refute
  a causal association" (R1.9).
- *Sceptical side, in its proponents' words:* Karvinen 2015: "scientists have not been able to show
  a beneficial effect of physical activity on the risk of death in controlled intervention studies
  among individuals who have been healthy at baseline" and "genetic pleiotropy might partly explain"
  the association (R1.10). Mandsager 2018 on its own fitness data: "The degree to which high CRF
  preselects patients with lower mortality vs causes a reduction in mortality is not discernible
  from our study" (R1.8). Gen 100 reports the "Sceptics" argument about missing RCT evidence, but
  **no sceptic's own published statement beyond Karvinen was read**.
- *What is not contested:* that exercise improves fitness, strength, blood pressure and glucose
  control in trials; that active people have lower mortality in cohorts.

**2. How much muscle-strengthening is enough, and is there a ceiling?** *Classification: contested
(empirical), low certainty.* WHO: 2 or more days, "insufficient evidence to specify a specific
duration". Momma: maximum association at 30 to 60 min/week, higher volume "unclear", very low
certainty. No proponent of *more is harmful* was read; the J-shape is the authors' observation, not
a claim of harm.

**3. Is sitting harmful independently of exercise?** *Classification: contested (empirical).* UK
CMOs state "Prolonged sitting is harmful, even in people who achieve the recommended levels"; WHO
recommends limiting sedentary time and doing more MVPA to offset it (strong, moderate certainty) but
finds evidence insufficient for a threshold; the MR study found the screen-time association "less
evident after adjusting for education" (R1.11). Only lightly covered here.

**4. Are the high *elite fitness* and "VILPA" effect sizes real?** *Classification: contested
(empirical and presentational).* The authors present them strongly (Mandsager "no observed upper
limit of benefit"; Stamatakis "38%–40% reduction") while their own limitations sections concede
selection and reverse causation. The course should present the numbers with their design.

**5. "If physical activity were a drug, we would refer to it as a miracle cure"** *Classification: value/advocacy
claim* by the UK CMOs (R1.3). Present as how health authorities choose to speak.

## Unread or unreachable

- Garber et al. 2011 ACSM position stand, **body and tables** (abstract only; tables via Kravitz's
  secondary summary).
- UK CMO guidelines, **original 2019 PDF** (asset URL "gone"; the 2026 HTML revision was read) and
  the 2026 supplementary information document.
- WHO 2020 **Web Annex: Evidence profiles** (the GRADE tables behind each grade).
- PAGAC 2018 report beyond the chapters named in R1.4 (the full report was downloaded; most was
  keyword-searched, not read).
- Kodama 2009, Kujala 1998, Bennie 2018, O'Donovan 2017: **abstracts only**; O'Donovan 2017
  **erratum (2022)** not read.
- NCHS Data Brief No. 443 (2022), US 24.2 percent meeting both guidelines in 2020: search-result
  level only; CDC returned "Access Denied".
- Ekelund et al. 2019 BMJ (device-measured activity and mortality meta-analysis, WHO ref 65) and
  Stamatakis et al. 2018 (strength guideline pooled cohorts, WHO ref 84): known only through WHO's
  description.
- Moore, Patel, Matthews 2012 *PLoS Med* (source of PAG Figure 2-1): known only through the PAG
  figure caption.
- Ekelund et al. 2026 *Lancet* (deaths averted by small changes) and the Australian 2026 movement
  guidelines: known only from UK CMO reference list.
- Lee 2019 references 5 and 6 on the Manpo-kei origin; no 1965 primary source for the pedometer.
- Karvinen 2015 supplementary tables (S1 to S3).
- Any Mendelian randomisation study beyond Jiesisibieke and Schooling 2026 (Han et al. 2026 was
  found by search, not opened).

## Part R2. How strength and muscle adapt to resistance training, and what the programming variables do

*Researcher 2, Stage 1, 2026-09-24. Raw texts saved under `SCRATCH/research/raw/r2/` (full texts as
`<name>.txt`, PubMed abstracts in `pubmed_abstracts.txt` and `pubmed_flat.txt`, line-joined copies of
everything in `raw/r2/flat/`). Every passage inside quotation marks below was checked with `grep -F`
against those saved texts after joining wrapped lines; for the two PDF texts (Pelland preprint, arXiv
preprint) the check was run after Unicode NFKC normalisation, which only turns typographic ligatures
such as "ﬁ" back into "fi". Nothing was quoted from WebFetch or search-result summaries.*

**Abbreviations used throughout.** RT, resistance training. 1RM, one-repetition maximum: the most
weight a person can lift once in a given exercise. RM, repetition maximum (a ‘10RM’ load is one you
can lift 10 times and not 11). RIR, repetitions in reserve. SMD or ES, standardised mean difference
or effect size, a unitless measure: in this literature roughly 0.2 is called small, 0.5 moderate, 0.8
large, though authors vary. CTRL, a non-exercising control group.

---

## The first thing this section found, and it should shape the course

**Two different questions live under ‘what works’, and the evidence answers them very differently.**

1. *Does resistance training, of almost any sensible kind, make an untrained adult stronger and
   (to a smaller degree) more muscular than doing nothing?* Yes, and this is about as settled as
   anything in exercise science: 137 systematic reviews and more than 30,000 participants in the
   2026 ACSM position stand (R2.2), and a network meta-analysis of 178 trials in which every
   prescription tested beat no exercise (R2.3).
2. *Which program is best?* Here the honest answer is that most of the variables people argue about
   (failure or not, machines or free weights, periodised or not, how many days a week at a fixed
   volume) make small or undetectable differences in the trials, and the trials were mostly short
   (a median of about 8 weeks in several meta-analyses), mostly in untrained young adults, and
   mostly measured strength on the very exercise that was trained.

The difference between these is the course's spine for this part: **the big effect is starting; the
small effects are the arguments.** And a second spine runs underneath it: **‘stronger’ is measured
on something, and strength gains are specific to what was practised** (R2.5). A study that trained
the bench press and tested the bench press is partly measuring skill at the bench press.

---

## R2.1 ACSM (2009) position stand: Progression models in resistance training for healthy adults

**Citation.** American College of Sports Medicine. Progression models in resistance training for
healthy adults. *Med Sci Sports Exerc* 2009;41(3):687-708. doi:10.1249/MSS.0b013e3181915670.
PMID 19204579. (Indexed under the corporate author; the 2009 writing group's names were not opened. The 2002
predecessor, MSSE 34(2):364-380, lists W. J. Kraemer first.)

**Read level.** Abstract only (PubMed). The full text on journals.lww.com is paywalled; the page
fetched returned only the abstract. The evidence grades attached to each recommendation were not read.

**What it recommended** (all verbatim from the abstract):
- Novices: "For novice (untrained individuals with no RT experience or who have not trained for
  several years) training, it is recommended that loads correspond to a repetition range of an 8-12
  repetition maximum (RM)."
- Intermediate and advanced: "it is recommended that individuals use a wider loading range from 1 to
  12 RM in a periodized fashion with eventual emphasis on heavy loading (1-6 RM) using 3- to 5-min
  rest periods between sets performed at a moderate contraction velocity (1-2 s CON; 1-2 s ECC)."
- Progression: "When training at a specific RM load, it is recommended that 2-10% increase in load
  be applied when the individual can perform the current workload for one to two repetitions over the
  desired number."
- Frequency: "The recommendation for training frequency is 2-3 d x wk(-1) for novice training, 3-4 d
  x wk(-1) for intermediate training, and 4-5 d x wk(-1) for advanced training."
- Hypertrophy: "loads corresponding to 1-12 RM be used in periodized fashion with emphasis on the 6-12
  RM zone using 1- to 2-min rest periods between sets at a moderate velocity. Higher volume,
  multiple-set programs are recommended for maximizing hypertrophy."
- Its own hedge: "recommendations should be applied in context and should be contingent upon an
  individual's target goals, physical capacity, and training status."

**Good for.** The historical baseline: what ‘the textbook’ said for 17 years, and the origin of the
familiar ‘8 to 12 reps’ and ‘add 2 to 10% when you beat your target by one or two reps’ rules. That
2-for-2-style progression rule is a practical tool a lesson can teach, labelled as expert
recommendation rather than a trial finding.

**Caveats.** The 2026 stand (R2.2) says both the 2002 and 2009 stands "were criticized (13,14) for
lacking evidence-based rigor", and gives one example: the 2009 recommendation that novices train two
to three full-body sessions a week for hypertrophy "was graded as the highest possible quality of
evidence (11). However, this recommendation was informed by only three studies (178–180) (n = 59
total participants), and two of the studies did not investigate frequency (178,179)." That example is
the 2026 authors' characterisation; the 2009 text was not opened to check it.

---

## R2.2 ACSM (2026) position stand: Resistance training prescription for muscle function, hypertrophy, and physical performance in healthy adults: an overview of reviews

**Citation.** Currier BS, D'Souza AC, Singh MAF, Lowisz CV, Rawson ES, Schoenfeld BJ, Smith-Ryan AE,
Steen JP, Thomas GA, Triplett NT, Washington TA, Werner TJ, Phillips SM. *Med Sci Sports Exerc*
2026;58(4):851-872. doi:10.1249/MSS.0000000000003897. PMID 41843416. PMCID PMC12965823. Plus the ACSM
press release "ACSM Unveils Landmark 2026 Resistance Training Guidelines" (its title continues with a subtitle calling it the first update in 17 years)
(acsm.org/resistance-training-guidelines-update-2026/, fetched 2026-09-24).

**Read level.** Full text of the article (Europe PMC XML: introduction, methods, all results
sections, Table 1 to Table 6 as rendered, discussion, limitations, disclosures). Supplemental
appendices (per-review data, AMSTAR scores, evidence grades) not opened. Press release read in full.

**Yes, ACSM has replaced the 2009 stand.** The article says it "updates the American College of
Sports Medicine 2009 Position Stand". Received February 2025, accepted September 2025, issue April 2026.

**Design.** An overview of reviews (umbrella review): 137 systematic reviews of randomised trials,
more than 30,000 participants, searched to October 2024. Eligible trials: healthy adults 18 or over,
RT for at least 6 weeks (range 6 to 52 weeks), at least 12 sessions. Reviews scored 1 to 9 of a
possible 11 on AMSTAR.

**Who the evidence is about.** "In most cases, reviews examined people with minimal or no RT
experience (i.e., novice); however, some reviews included more advanced trainees." And, in full:
"Previously, we showed that training experience had a minimal impact on strength and hypertrophy
outcomes ( 17 ); nonetheless, much of the evidence synthesized here is from inexperienced trainees."
(2026-09-24, lesson 4's review, F2: the first clause was missing here, so the lesson quoted only the
half that suits a narrow-literature argument. Checked against the Europe PMC full text.) Strength, "Unless otherwise specified,
strength was voluntary isotonic one-repetition maximum (1RM) in the same mode as that in which the
training was performed." Ages were not separated: "our recommendations are evidence-based across all
ages."

**On specificity and carryover (added 2026-09-24, lesson 4's review, F1; checked against the full
text).** "Some adaptations can be affected by modifying RTx variables, although there is a
considerable carryover of training effects on general muscular performance in various domains in
nonadvanced trainees ( 17 , 24 )." This is the stand's own counterweight to R2.5: a lesson that
stresses specificity should carry it.

**Forms of training (Table 6, added 2026-09-24, lesson 4's review, F14).** "Strength is improved by
RT, including circuit RT, elastic band RT, home-based RT, and velocity-based RT." That is each form
against no exercise, not against each other.

**Headline findings, verbatim from the abstract.**
- "Compared with no exercise (control), RT significantly improved muscle strength, size
  (hypertrophy), power, endurance, contraction velocity, gait speed, balance, and multiple physical
  function outcomes. Few RT prescription (RTx) variables affected primary adaptations."
- "However, voluntary strength was enhanced by lifting heavier loads (≥80% one-repetition maximum),
  through a complete range of motion, for 2-3 sets, at the beginning of training sessions, and ≥2
  sessions/wk. Muscle hypertrophy was enhanced by higher volumes (≥10 sets/wk) and eccentric
  overload."
- "Training to momentary muscle fatigue, equipment type, exercise complexity, set structure, time
  under tension, blood flow restriction, and periodization did not consistently impact training
  outcomes."
- Conclusion: "Healthy adults should perform progressive RT, with variable prescription consistent
  with our findings, to improve muscle function, size, and physical performance."

**The primary recommendation, from the discussion.** "Our primary recommendation is that healthy
adults perform RT with high effort (effort can be measured through various scales, but can be
achieved with various loads and sets per the FITT-VP principle) at least twice weekly, with all major
muscle groups being engaged."

**On hypertrophy, what did not matter.** "RT did not influence hypertrophy performed with low (1
d/wk) versus high (>5 d/wk) frequency when total volume was equated; low (30% 1RM) to high (100% 1RM)
loads; contractions to muscle failure/fatigue; blood flow restriction; varying loads during
repetitions; fast (0.5 s) versus slow (8 s) repetitions; power training techniques; periodization
(linear, undulating, and nonperiodized); or exercise order within training sessions."

**On volume.** "Clearly, one set is superior to zero sets (CTRL), and two sets are superior to one
set (17,24,88–93,128,131–133), but the exact number of sets required to optimize adaptations cannot be
ascertained." It cites a meta-regression (reference 187, which the stand's reference list names as Pelland et al.'s published *Sports Med* paper, R2.8, not the preprint; corrected 2026-09-24 at lesson 5's review, F5)
as showing "a dose-response that plateaued and showed diminishing returns beyond ~2–3 sets/exercise
for strength and ~18–20 weekly sets for hypertrophy with varying loads; thus, healthy adults are
advised to complete at least two sets per exercise."

**On failure.** "Completing sets to fatigue (momentary muscular failure) does not enhance gains in
strength, hypertrophy, and power, and so is not necessary for benefits to occur." The next sentence,
the stand's safety caution: "It may also be that lifting to fatigue is inadvisable for certain
populations (e.g., older individuals) due to risks to vascular health and an increased risk of injury
resulting from poor form (24,97–99)." (2026-09-24, lesson 5's review, F2: missing here, checked
against the full text.) And: "Sufficient
effort (assessed using various scales) can be accomplished by completing sets with various RTx and
completion of “near-failure” or a target of 2–3 repetitions in reserve (RIR)". But also: "there is
insufficient evidence to quantify exact RIR and perceived exertion targets".

**On progression, a notable softening.** "Notably, as the results in Table 4 highlight, progression
is not necessary to achieve beneficial outcomes, and overload, or more accurately, increasing the
stimulus in some manner, is likely a requirement only for those seeking continued longer term
progress."

**On periodisation.** "The impact of periodization on strength could not be determined, although one
review found that periodized programs were slightly favored over nonperiodized programs to maximize
strength gains under volume-equated conditions (117)." Reference 117 is Moesgaard 2022 (R2.11).
"With appropriate progressive overload, periodization is not significantly
superior to nonperiodized programs; thus, periodization is less important than previously
hypothesized (11,12) for healthy adults to improve muscle function and hypertrophy." And: "We note,
however, that definitions of periodization vary (166,167)". It also notes
"periodization and programming are often conflated (168)". (2026-09-24, lesson 5's review, F1: the
first sentence and its "although" half were missing here, so the lesson carried only the sceptical
half. Checked against the Europe PMC full text.)

**On the old guidelines being too demanding.** "Notably, others have estimated that developing
general muscular fitness (strength, hypertrophy, and power) according to previous ACSM guidelines
may, in some cases, require training for 20 h or more per week (176)." Reference 176 is Carpinelli RN,
"Challenging the American College of Sports Medicine 2009 position stand on resistance training", *Med
Sport* 2009;13(2):131-7: the estimate comes from a paper written against the 2009 stand, not read here.
The stand then concedes: "The specific program details outlined in previous Position Stands may be an
appropriate starting point for some individuals; however, several other RT programs, as evidenced
here, can also be effective." (2026-09-24, lesson 5's review, F6: both added, checked against the
full text and its reference list.) And: "we propose that
individualizing programs to increase RT participation is, from our perspective, more important than
conforming to specific RTx criteria outlined in previous Position Stands."

**On participation.** "Current estimates (from self-report) are that only ~30% of American adults
complete some muscle-strengthening activities at least 2 d/wk, and nearly 60% complete no
muscle-strengthening exercise (169–173)."

**On safety.** "Resistance training is safe for healthy adults of all ages (5)". It reports an
analysis of more than 38,000 participants in which "exercise did not increase the risk of serious
adverse events." (Other researchers cover safety; recorded here for cross-reference only.)

**On body regions (explicitly the authors' view, not a finding).** "In the view of the authors,
applying the recommendations herein to the body regions “upper” and “lower” for “push” and “pull”
exercises (i.e., four body regions) is sufficient to target major muscle groups."

**The authors' own limits.** "randomized trials in sports and exercise medicine-related fields are
often poor quality with small sample sizes, poor randomization methodology, lack of preregistration,
poor reporting of adverse events, and other shortcomings (199)". Overlap between reviews was checked
only for strength. "Overviews of reviews do not permit inferences about the comparative effectiveness
of different interventions (14)."

**Ten more passages, verbatim (added 2026-09-24, lesson 7's review, Reviewer F; each string-checked
against the Europe PMC full text of PMC12965823).** Lesson 7's Sources note said its paraphrases of
these "were checked against the full text"; they were real, but none was recorded here.
- No comparison of regions: "The available evidence did not compare distinct body regions or muscle
  groups." Six regions: "The movement directions “horizontal” and “vertical” may also be considered
  for upper body exercises (i.e., six body regions)."
- The two sentences straight after the progression sentence above: "We note that in some
  populations, due to inexperience (and potential safety concerns), RTx necessitates that loads are
  necessarily low and progression is a requirement to achieve a meaningful benefit." "Continued
  progression could be a personal decision and part of the individualization of RTx (see below)."
  **Keep "due to inexperience": it names beginners.**
- Progression for the untrained: "Untrained individuals will benefit from various RT programs,
  provided that progression, the variables outlined here as affecting outcomes, and adherence are
  core principles." With the abstract's conclusion ("Healthy adults should perform progressive RT"),
  this is the other half of the "progression is not necessary" sentence; quote them together.
- Relative load: "Such adaptation would require increasing the stimulus, such as load, volume,
  training frequency, exercise selection, or duration (even if only slightly), as the muscle continues
  to adapt to produce further adaptations ( 164 )." And: "Still, the same relative load can also be
  sustained when regular strength testing is performed or perceived exertion scales are used to
  increase absolute load commensurate with strength gains ( 113 , 165 )."
- What did not change strength: "Strength was not affected by RT performed with contractions to
  muscle failure/fatigue, free weights versus machines, unstable versus stable surfaces; fast (<2 s)
  versus moderate-slow (>2 s) contractions, morning versus evening training sessions, short (<1 min)
  versus long (>1 min) between-set rest intervals, eccentric versus concentric contractions, power
  training techniques, or different set structures (cluster and complex)."
- The earlier stands' details, rest included: "Previous guidelines have recommended healthy adults
  use free weights and machines to complete two to three RT sessions per week, with eight to ten
  exercises involving major muscle groups per session, one to four sets per exercise, eight to twenty
  repetitions per set, 2–3 min rest between sets, loads 40%–70% 1RM, and follow the principles of
  progressive overload, specificity, and variation ( 10 , 11 )."
- Periodisation defined: "One of the most common forms of variation is periodization—intentionally
  modifying prescription variables (e.g., load, volume, frequency) throughout an RT program." And the
  "definitions vary" sentence whole (quoted only to its citation under "On periodisation" above): "We
  note, however, that definitions of periodization vary ( 166 , 167 ), but broadly involve the
  systematic manipulation of RTx variables with the goal of optimizing performance adaptations,
  managing fatigue, preventing overtraining/injury, and peaking for specific goals."
- Too little data, including same-session concurrent training: "There were insufficient data to
  determine if strength was affected by blood flow restriction ( 110 – 112 ), Olympic-style
  weightlifting ( 69 ), intrasession autoregulation ( 113 , 114 ), varied exercise selection ( 115 ),
  periodization ( 24 , 116 – 118 ), or concurrent training (i.e., aerobic and RT in the same training
  session) ( 119 – 124 )."
- Frequency and volume: "For example, RT frequency was found to impact strength; however, there was
  insufficient evidence to conclude a dose-response relationship, and the impact is diminished when
  volume is equated ( 73 , 76 , 90 )." A third weekly session adds sets and practice together; this
  sentence is why a lesson can't credit the practice alone.

**Press release.** Adds a quotation from Phillips: "“The best resistance training program is the one
you’ll actually stick with,”" and "“Training all major muscle groups at least twice a week matters
far more than chasing the idea of a ‘perfect’ or complex training plan. Whether it’s barbells, bands,
or bodyweight, consistency and effort drive results.”" **Discrepancy to flag:** the press release
bullet reads "Lift heavier loads [80% of one-repetition maximum (1RM)] for 2–3 sets per exercise",
while the paper says "≥80%". A lesson should cite the paper.

**Conflicts of interest (disclosed).** "B. J. S. formerly served on the scientific advisory board for
Tonal Corporation, a manufacturer of fitness equipment. No other authors reported conflicts relevant
to this work." Two authors (Currier, Phillips) also led R2.3, which this stand cites as reference 17,
so the stand is partly a summary of its own authors' work.

**Good for.** The single best anchor for the whole programming part of the course, current and
institutional. Lesson sentence it licenses (a paraphrase, not a quotation): *In healthy adults, most of the difference comes from
doing resistance training at all; heavier loads help strength, more sets help muscle size, and most
other variables have not been shown to matter consistently.*

**Does not show.** Anything about athletes, clinical populations, or long-term (years) training. It
cannot rank programs against each other (its own caveat). Its recommendations on strength rest mostly
on 1RM tested in the trained exercise (see R2.5).

---

## R2.3 Currier et al. (2023): network meta-analysis of resistance training prescriptions

**Citation.** Currier BS, Mcleod JC, Banfield L, et al. Resistance training prescription for muscle
strength and hypertrophy in healthy adults: a systematic review and Bayesian network meta-analysis.
*Br J Sports Med* 2023;57(18):1211-1220. doi:10.1136/bjsports-2023-106807. PMCID PMC10579494.

**Read level.** Full text (Europe PMC), including results, discussion and limitations; supplements not
opened.

**Design and population.** Bayesian network meta-analysis of randomised trials in healthy adults
(athletes, military, people with chronic disease and trials that explicitly mention obese or overweight
participants excluded), 6 weeks minimum. Prescriptions coded by load (higher, 80% 1RM or more; lower, under 80%),
sets (single or multiple) and weekly frequency (1, 2, 3 or more). "The strength network included 178
studies (n=5097; women=45%). The hypertrophy network included 119 studies (n=3364; women=47%)." Risk
of bias: "In the strength network, 22%, 67% and 1% of studies had a high, moderate or low risk of
bias, respectively."

**Key figures.** "All RTxs were superior to CTRL for muscle strength and hypertrophy. Higher-load
(>80% of single repetition maximum) prescriptions maximised strength gains, and all prescriptions
comparably promoted muscle hypertrophy." Top-ranked for strength: "higher-load, multiset,
thrice-weekly training (standardised mean difference (95% credible interval); 1.60 (1.38 to 1.82) vs
CTRL)"; for hypertrophy: "higher-load, multiset, twice-weekly training (0.66 (0.47 to 0.85) vs
CTRL)". Note the size difference: effects on strength (SMD 0.75 to 1.60) run well above effects on
muscle size (0.10 to 0.66).

**The comparisons between programs mostly could not be told apart.** "The 95% CrI contained zero for
a striking 91% (101/111) of all between-RTx comparisons (table 2)." But the authors add the right
hedge: "The available evidence does not permit definitive, statistically valid conclusions about the
equivalency of each RTx, despite most comparisons between RTxs not being statistically significantly
different from each other." That is, ‘not shown to differ’ is not ‘shown to be the same’.

**Stated stance (a value claim, labelled as such by the authors).** "In our view, especially given the
low participation rates in RT, practitioners should not avoid prescribing, nor should individuals be
discouraged from completing non-top-ranked RTx." And: "we propose that, for most adults, regularly
engaging in any RTx is more important than training to optimise strength and hypertrophy outcomes."

**Useful incidental method.** The authors converted RM to %1RM with "%1RM=100−(RM(2.5))" (so a 10RM
load is treated as about 75% 1RM). This is a rough convention, not a law (see R2.18).

**Caveats.** "We do not know how these RTx affect relevant health outcomes." Load, sets and frequency
were binned into categories. Elite athletes excluded.

---

## R2.4 Early strength gains are largely neural

**Sources.**
- Moritani T, deVries HA. Neural factors versus hypertrophy in the time course of muscle strength
  gain. *Am J Phys Med* 1979;58(3):115-130. PMID 453338. **Abstract only.**
- Sale DG. Neural adaptation to resistance training. *Med Sci Sports Exerc* 1988;20(5 Suppl):S135-145.
  doi:10.1249/00005768-198810001-00009. **Abstract only.**
- Škarabot J, Brownstein CG, Casolo A, Del Vecchio A, Ansdell P. The knowns and unknowns of neural
  adaptations to resistance training. *Eur J Appl Physiol* 2021;121(3):675-685.
  doi:10.1007/s00421-020-04567-3. PMCID PMC7892509. **Full text.**

**Moritani and deVries.** Seven young men and eight women, 8 weeks of "isotonic strength training".
"The results indicated that neural factors accounted for the larger proportion of the initial
strength increment and thereafter both neural factors and hypertrophy took part in the further
increase in strength, with hypertrophy becoming the dominant factor after the first 3 to 5 weeks."
The untrained arm also got stronger, which they read as "cross education". The abstract does not say
how hypertrophy was estimated (the full paper was not opened, so the method is unknown to this section and a lesson should not describe it).

**Sale.** "Strength training may cause adaptive changes within the nervous system that allow a
trainee to more fully activate prime movers in specific movements and to better coordinate the
activation of all relevant muscles, thereby effecting a greater net force in the intended direction
of movement."

**Škarabot et al. (the modern review).** "the initial (< 2–4 weeks) increases in force production are
thought to be primarily underpinned by neural adaptations". The lines of evidence it lists:
"task-specificity of strength in the absence of significant morphological adaptations", "the
disproportionate increase in muscle force relative to muscle size", the increase in voluntary
activation, cross-education, and "increased muscle force generating capacity following weeks of
imagined contractions". Motor-unit recordings show that after training "increased force production
following resistance training is accompanied by decreased motor unit recruitment threshold and
increased discharge rate". But where in the nervous system it happens is unknown: "the precise site
of putative neural adaptations remains unclear." And a caution on the evidence base, with its scope
(corrected 2026-09-24, lesson 4's review, F11, against the full text): the review says it "will be
largely concentrated on studies employing single-joint/single-muscle isometric contractions during
assessments" (a scoping choice for the review), and that "the majority of mechanistic studies
reviewed in this article rely on simplified models of resistance exercise". Its "(< 2–4 weeks)"
