sentence cites Moritani and deVries 1979 among its references, so the two timings below are not fully
independent.

**Classification.** Established: early gains outpace muscle growth, and part of the gain is neural.
Contested: exactly how many weeks, and the site of the change. Note the two sources give different
timings (Moritani, hypertrophy dominant "after the first 3 to 5 weeks"; Škarabot, neural dominance in
the initial "< 2–4 weeks"). A lesson should say ‘the first few weeks’ rather than a number.

**Good for.** Explaining to a beginner why they get stronger long before they look different, and
why the first month's gains will not continue at that pace.

---

## R2.5 Specificity: strength gains are largest on the exercise you trained

**Sources.**
- Spitz RW, Kataoka R, Dankel SJ, et al., Loenneke JP. Quantifying the generality of strength
  adaptation: a meta-analysis. *Sports Med* 2023;53(3):637-648. doi:10.1007/s40279-022-01790-0.
  **Abstract only** (PubMed).
- Hammert WB, Kataoka R, Yamada Y, Sallberg RW, Kang A, Buckner SL, Loenneke JP. Non-specific strength
  changes between high- and low-load isotonic resistance training: a systematic review and
  meta-analysis. *Sports Med* 2026;56(3):763-773. doi:10.1007/s40279-025-02370-8. PMCID PMC13018011.
  **Full text.**
- Spitz RW, Bell ZW, Wong V, Yamada Y, Song JS, Buckner SL, Abe T, Loenneke JP. Strength testing or strength
  training: considerations for future research. *Physiol Meas* 2020;41(9):09TR01. **Abstract only.**

**Spitz et al. 2023.** Twelve studies with a time-matched non-exercise control, healthy adults,
isotonic training, with strength then tested both in the trained movement and on a different
isometric or isokinetic device. Inclusion criterion 7 (added 2026-09-24, lesson 4's review, F1,
against the PubMed abstract): "the non-specific strength task had to test a muscle involved in the
training (i.e., could not have trained chest press and test handgrip strength)". So the 0.8 is the
trained muscle on an unfamiliar device, not strength in general. "The overall effect of resistance training on changes in strength
within a movement that was not directly trained was 0.8 (Cohen's d)" (95% CI 0.22 to 1.4), against
"The overall effect of resistance training on strength changes within a movement that was directly
trained was 1.84 (Cohen's d)" (95% CI 1.23 to 2.4). Conclusion: "strength increases in both the
specific and non-specific strength tests. However, the smaller effect size associated with
non-specific strength suggests that it will be difficult for a single study to meaningfully
investigate the transfer of strength training adaptions."

**Hammert et al. 2026.** Ten studies, 245 participants, all training to task failure, comparing
heavy and light isotonic training on *non-specific* (isometric or isokinetic) strength. "The overall
effect size (Cohen's d) was 0.322 with a standard error of 0.17, a 95% confidence interval of - 0.08 to
0.72 (p = 0.104)". Conclusion: "The results of the current analysis were inconclusive as to whether
high- and low-load isotonic training induced differential changes in non-specific strength." From the
introduction: "Despite a large body of evidence indicating that high-load resistance training is more
effective than low-load training for increasing task-specific strength [7, 11–13], it becomes much
more difficult to detect changes [8] and predict whether adaptations will favor high- or low-load
training when strength changes are assessed using less familiar tasks".

**Spitz et al. 2020 (practice effect).** Reviewing heavy-versus-light studies, "the differences
between high load and low load exercise can be reduced when the group training with a low load is
allowed additional exposure to the maximal strength test." That is, part of heavy training's 1RM
advantage may be practice at lifting heavy.

**Also consistent:** Schoenfeld 2017 (R2.6) found heavy loads beat light loads on 1RM but not on
isometric strength; Haugen 2023 (R2.13) found free-weight training improved free-weight tests more
and machine training improved machine tests more.

**Good for.** A core critical-reading tool: when a study says ‘strength increased 25%’, ask
*measured how, on what*. The ACSM stand's own default outcome is 1RM "in the same mode as that in
which the training was performed" (R2.2).

**Classification.** Established that trained-task gains exceed untrained-task gains. Contested:
how much heavy loading matters for ‘general’ strength once the test is unfamiliar (Hammert 2026
inconclusive, with a point estimate favouring heavy).

---

## R2.6 Load: heavy versus light

**Sources.**
- Schoenfeld BJ, Grgic J, Ogborn D, Krieger JW. Strength and hypertrophy adaptations between low- vs.
  high-load resistance training: a systematic review and meta-analysis. *J Strength Cond Res*
  2017;31(12):3508-3523. doi:10.1519/JSC.0000000000002200. **Abstract only.**
- Morton RW, Oikawa SY, Wavell CG, et al., Phillips SM. Neither load nor systemic hormones determine
  resistance training-mediated hypertrophy or strength gains in resistance-trained young men. *J Appl
  Physiol* 2016;121(1):129-138. doi:10.1152/japplphysiol.00154.2016. PMCID PMC4967245. **Full text
  saved; abstract used for figures.**
- Lopez P, Radaelli R, Taaffe DR, et al., Pinto RS. Resistance training load effects on muscle
  hypertrophy and strength gain: systematic review and network meta-analysis. *Med Sci Sports Exerc*
  2021;53(6):1206-1216. doi:10.1249/MSS.0000000000002585. PMCID PMC8126497. **Full text** (a
  corrigendum exists, MSSE 2022;54(2):370, not opened).

**Schoenfeld et al. 2017.** 21 studies; every set to "momentary muscular failure"; low load defined
as 60% 1RM or less, high as more than 60%; 6 weeks minimum. "Gains in 1RM strength were significantly
greater in favor of high- vs. low-load training, whereas no significant differences were found for
isometric strength between conditions. Changes in measures of muscle hypertrophy were similar between
conditions." Conclusion: "maximal strength benefits are obtained from the use of heavy loads while
muscle hypertrophy can be equally achieved across a spectrum of loading ranges."

**Morton et al. 2016 (an RCT, in trained men).** "Forty-nine resistance-trained men (23 ± 1 yr, mean ±
SE) performed 12 wk of whole-body RT", randomised to 20 to 25 reps at about 30 to 50% 1RM or 8 to 12
reps at about 75 to 90% 1RM, "with all sets being performed to volitional failure". 1RM rose in both,
"with only the change in bench press being significantly different between groups (HR, 9 ± 1, vs. LR,
14 ± 1 kg, P = 0.012)". Lean mass and fibre size rose with no group difference. And on the hormone
myth: "acute postexercise systemic hormonal rises are not related to or in any way indicative of
RT-mediated gains in muscle mass or strength."

**Lopez et al. 2021.** 28 studies, 747 healthy adults, all to volitional failure; low load more than
15RM, moderate 9 to 15RM, high 8RM or fewer. "Although muscle hypertrophy improvements seem to be load
independent, increases in muscle strength are superior in high-load RT programs." Strength: "superior
for both high-load and moderate-load compared with low-load RT in overall and subgroup analysis (SMD,
0.60-0.63 and 0.34-0.35, respectively; P < 0.001-0.003), with a nonsignificant but superior effect for
high compared with moderate load (SMD, 0.26-0.28, P = 0.068)". Participants: "A total of 747 healthy
men and women with an average age of 23.4 ± 3.0 yr participated in the included studies." Most of the studies involved men, "19 of 28, or 67.9%", and "untrained participants (21 of 28, or 75.0%" (study reference numbers omitted).
"None of the studies included highly strength-trained individuals".

**Classification.** Established, for young healthy adults training to failure over weeks: muscle
growth similar across a wide load range; 1RM favours heavier loads. The light-load finding depends on
sets being taken very hard (all three sources required failure). Whether light loads build *general*
strength as well as heavy is unresolved (R2.5).

**Good for.** Freeing a beginner from the idea that only heavy weights ‘count’, while being honest
that getting good at lifting heavy things requires lifting heavy things.

---

## R2.7 Volume: how many sets

**Source.** Schoenfeld BJ, Ogborn D, Krieger JW. Dose-response relationship between weekly resistance
training volume and increases in muscle mass: a systematic review and meta-analysis. *J Sports Sci*
2017;35(11):1073-1082. doi:10.1080/02640414.2016.1210197. **Abstract only.**

**Figures.** "The final analysis comprised 34 treatment groups from 15 studies." "Each additional set
was associated with an increase in effect size (ES) of 0.023 corresponding to an increase in the
percentage gain by 0.37%." Weekly sets in three bands (<5, 5-9 and 10+ per muscle) "showed a trend for
an effect of weekly sets (P = 0.074)". Conclusion: "a graded dose-response relationship whereby
increases in RT volume produce greater gains in muscle hypertrophy."

**Caveat.** Fifteen studies. A likely early source (not verified as the origin) of the widely repeated ‘10+ sets per muscle per week’ figure,
which in this analysis was a trend, not a significant category difference.

---

## R2.8 Pelland et al. (2024 preprint, 2025/2026 Sports Medicine): the dose-response shape for volume and frequency

**Citation.** Pelland JC, Remmert JF, Robinson ZP, Hinson SR, Zourdos MC. The resistance training dose
response: meta-regressions exploring the effects of weekly volume and frequency on muscle hypertrophy
and strength gains. *Sports Med* 2026;56(2):481-505 (online December 2025).
doi:10.1007/s40279-025-02344-w. PMID 41343037. Preprint: SportRxiv, posted 2024-10-04,
https://sportrxiv.org/index.php/server/preprint/view/460.

**Read level.** Published version: abstract only (Springer page blocked by a bot challenge). Preprint:
full text (the PDF linked as current on SportRxiv; marked "Preprint not peer reviewed"). Figures below
come from the **preprint** and may differ in the published version; a lesson should cite the
published abstract for the headline and the preprint only for the shape, labelled as such.

**Design and population (published abstract).** "A series of multi-level meta-regressions were
performed for muscle hypertrophy and strength, utilizing 67 total studies of 2058 participants (79.1%
male, 20.9% female; average age 25.16 ± 5.22 years). All models were adjusted for the duration of the
intervention and training status." Sets that only indirectly train the measured muscle (a bench press
for the triceps, say) were counted as half a set (the ‘fractional’ method), which fitted best.

**Headline (published abstract).** "The posterior probability of the marginal slope exceeding zero
for the effect of volume on both hypertrophy and strength was 100%, indicating that gains in muscle
size and strength increase as volume increases. However, both best-fit models suggest diminishing
returns, with the diminishing returns for strength being considerably more pronounced." On frequency:
for hypertrophy, "compatibility with negligible effects"; for strength, "strength gains increase with
increasing frequency, albeit with diminishing returns."

**The shape (preprint).** Interventions "lasted 10.42 ± 4.48 weeks". Hypertrophy: best fit a square
root curve, "diminishing returns that accelerate with higher volumes", and "the minimum effective
dose occurs with low volume (4 ‘fractional’ weekly sets)"; "no clear plateau in the primary
meta-regression", but "caution is warranted as few studies have explored ~25+ ‘fractional’ weekly
sets." Strength: best fit a reciprocal curve with "strong diminishing returns and a functional
plateau"; "one set was identified as the minimum effective dose. Additional increments in the SDES
were observed up to approximately 4 ‘fractional’ weekly sets, but not beyond this point." (SDES is the
authors' "smallest detectable effect size", 2.05% for hypertrophy and 3.96% for strength.) Their own
hedge: "The modest quality of overall model fits and the width of the uncertainty intervals suggest
that multiple dose-response forms are compatible with the present analysis".

**Why frequency helps strength (their hypothesis, not a finding).** "Higher frequencies allow for
more frequent practice with the assessed exercise or a similar motor pattern. Indeed, simply
practicing the test provides a robust stimulus for strength gains (118)."

**Good for.** The single clearest picture of ‘more helps, but each extra set buys less’, with the
striking asymmetry that strength flattens out at very low volumes while muscle size keeps creeping
up. A lesson can say that a few hard sets a week produce most of the strength benefit a beginner will
see in a short study.

**Caveats.** Mostly young men; about 10-week studies; most effects trained to failure (preprint:
"78.47% of effects were categorized as failure training for hypertrophy and 78.12% for strength").

---

## R2.9 Frequency

**Sources.**
- Grgic J, Schoenfeld BJ, Davies TB, et al. Effect of resistance training frequency on gains in
  muscular strength: a systematic review and meta-analysis. *Sports Med* 2018;48(5):1207-1220.
  doi:10.1007/s40279-018-0872-x. **Abstract only.**
- Schoenfeld BJ, Ogborn D, Krieger JW. Effects of resistance training frequency on measures of muscle
  hypertrophy: a systematic review and meta-analysis. *Sports Med* 2016;46(11):1689-1697.
  doi:10.1007/s40279-016-0543-8. **Abstract only.**
- Schoenfeld BJ, Grgic J, Krieger J. How many times per week should a muscle be trained to maximize
  muscle hypertrophy? *J Sports Sci* 2019;37(11):1286-1295. doi:10.1080/02640414.2018.1555906.
  **Abstract only.**

**Strength (Grgic 2018).** 22 studies. "Effect sizes increased in magnitude from 0.74, 0.82, 0.93, and
1.08 for training 1, 2, 3, and 4+ times per week, respectively. A subgroup analysis of volume-equated
studies showed no significant effect (p = 0.421) of RT frequency on muscular strength gains." "these
effects seem to be primarily driven by training volume". And the population note: "More evidence
among resistance-trained individuals is needed as most of the current studies were performed in
untrained participants."

**Hypertrophy, 2016 then 2019: the finding changed.** The 2016 analysis (ten studies): "frequencies of
training twice a week promote superior hypertrophic outcomes to once a week". The 2019 update (25
studies): "there is strong evidence that resistance training frequency does not significantly or
meaningfully impact muscle hypertrophy when volume is equated. Thus, for a given training volume,
individuals can choose a weekly frequency per muscle groups based on personal preference."

**Good for.** A clean example of a meta-analytic conclusion reversing as studies accumulated, by the
same lead author, which the course can use to teach provisional knowledge without cynicism. Practical
point: frequency matters mostly as a way to fit in more volume and more practice.

---

## R2.10 Proximity to failure, and how well people judge it

**Sources.**
- Grgic J, Schoenfeld BJ, Orazem J, Sabol F. Effects of resistance training performed to repetition
  failure or non-failure on muscular strength and hypertrophy: a systematic review and meta-analysis.
  *J Sport Health Sci* 2022;11(2):202-211. doi:10.1016/j.jshs.2021.01.007. PMCID PMC9068575. **Full
  text.**
- Refalo MC, Helms ER, Trexler ET, Hamilton DL, Fyfe JJ. Influence of resistance training
  proximity-to-failure on skeletal muscle hypertrophy: a systematic review with meta-analysis. *Sports
  Med* 2023;53(3):649-665. doi:10.1007/s40279-022-01784-y. PMCID PMC9935748. **Full text** (read:
  abstract, methods summary, results headings, parts of discussion).
- Robinson ZP, Pelland JC, Remmert JF, et al., Zourdos MC. Exploring the dose-response relationship
  between estimated resistance training proximity to failure, strength gain, and muscle hypertrophy:
  a series of meta-regressions. *Sports Med* 2024;54(9):2209-2231. doi:10.1007/s40279-024-02069-2.
  **Abstract only.**
- Zourdos MC, Klemp A, Dolan C, et al. Novel resistance training-specific rating of perceived exertion
  scale measuring repetitions in reserve. *J Strength Cond Res* 2016;30(1):267-275.
  doi:10.1519/JSC.0000000000001049. **Abstract only.**
- Halperin I, Malleron T, Har-Nir I, et al. Accuracy in predicting repetitions to task failure in
  resistance exercise: a scoping review and exploratory meta-analysis. *Sports Med* 2022;52(2):377-390.
  doi:10.1007/s40279-021-01559-x. **Abstract only.**

**Grgic 2022.** Fifteen studies, 394 participants for strength (265 men, 129 women); "All
participants in the studies were young adults." Training lasted "6 to 14 weeks, with a median of 8
weeks". No overall difference: strength "(ES = -0.09, 95% confidence interval (95%CI): -0.22 to 0.05)"
and hypertrophy "(ES = 0.22, 95%CI: -0.11 to 0.55)". In trained people only: "the analysis showed a
significant effect of training to failure for muscle hypertrophy (ES = 0.15, 95%CI: 0.03-0.26)".
Conclusion: "Training to muscle failure does not seem to be required for gains in strength and muscle
size. However, training in this manner does not seem to have detrimental effects on these
adaptations, either."

**Refalo 2023.** Fifteen studies, hypertrophy only. "There was a trivial advantage for resistance
training performed to set failure versus non-failure for muscle hypertrophy in studies applying any
definition of set failure [effect size=0.19 (95% confidence interval 0.00, 0.37), p=0.045]". With
failure strictly defined, "no advantage for either resistance training performed to momentary
muscular failure versus non-failure for muscle hypertrophy [effect size=0.12 (95% confidence interval
-0.13, 0.37), p=0.343]". Their reading: "a potential non-linear relationship between proximity-to-failure
and muscle hypertrophy."

**Robinson 2024.** RIR was *estimated by the authors* from study descriptions. "In all of the best-fit
models for strength, the confidence intervals of the marginal slopes for estimated RIR contained a
null point estimate, indicating a negligible relationship with strength gains. However, in all of the
best-fit models for muscle hypertrophy, the marginal slopes for estimated RIR were negative and their
confidence intervals did not contain a null point estimate, indicating that changes in muscle size
increased as sets were terminated closer to failure." With the hedge: "caution is warranted when
interpreting the present analysis due to its exploratory nature."

**The RIR-based RPE scale (Zourdos 2016).** 29 people (15 experienced, 14 novice squatters, mean age
24). "Subjects reported an RPE value that corresponded to an RIR value (RPE-10 = 0-RIR, RPE-9 = 1-RIR,
and so forth)." Conclusion: "The RIR-based RPE scale is a practical method to regulate daily training
load and provide feedback during a 1RM test." (A validation in squat only, in a small sample.)

**How accurate are people? (Halperin 2022).** 12 studies, 414 participants. "participants tended to
underpredict the number of repetitions to task failure by 0.95 repetitions (95% confidence interval
[CI] 0.17-1.73), but with considerable heterogeneity". Accuracy was better nearer failure, with
heavier loads (12 reps or fewer) and in later sets; "participants' training status did not seem to
influence prediction accuracy". **The hedges on those moderators, verbatim** (corrected 2026-09-24
at lesson 2's Stage 4 review; the summary above had dropped them): "prediction accuracy slightly improved when the predictions were made closer to set failure (β =  - 0.025, 95% CI - 0.05 to 0.0014) and when the number of repetitions performed to task failure was lower"
(the near-failure interval includes zero), and "Set number trivially influenced prediction accuracy with slightly increased accuracy in later sets".
The heterogeneity is between studies (I2 = 97.9%); the abstract reports "minimal between-participant
variation in predictive accuracy (standard deviation 1.45 repetitions, 95% CI 0.99-2.12)". Conclusion: "Participants were imperfect in their ability to predict
proximity to task failure independent of their training background. It remains to be determined
whether the observed degree of inaccuracy should be considered acceptable."

**Good for.** Teaching effort: a set ‘counts’ when it is reasonably hard, not only when it ends in
failure; on average people think they have fewer reps left than they do (by about one), and guess
slightly better in sets of 12 reps or fewer, so a little worse on long, light sets. Pair with R2.2's
"2–3 repetitions in reserve". (Hedged 2026-09-24 at lesson 5's review, F10: this line had said
"usually" and "worse", which the moderator hedges above don't support.)

**Classification.** Established: failure is not required for strength or muscle gains in the short
trials. Contested: whether going closer to failure adds hypertrophy (Robinson says yes as a
dose-response; Refalo says trivially at most; ACSM 2026 says not consistently).

---

## R2.11 Periodisation versus non-periodised training

**Sources.**
- Williams TD, Tolusso DV, Fedewa MV, Esco MR. Comparison of periodized and non-periodized resistance
  training on maximal strength: a meta-analysis. *Sports Med* 2017;47(10):2083-2100.
  doi:10.1007/s40279-017-0734-y. **Abstract only.** (A published comment on it exists, *Sports Med*
  2018;48(2):491-494, PMID 29178086, not opened.)
- Moesgaard L, Beck MM, Christiansen L, Aagaard P, Lundbye-Jensen J. Effects of periodization on
  strength and muscle hypertrophy in volume-equated resistance training programs: a systematic review
  and meta-analysis. *Sports Med* 2022;52(7):1647-1666. doi:10.1007/s40279-021-01636-1. **Abstract
  only.**
- Buckner SL, Jessee MB, Mouser JG, et al., Loenneke JP. The basics of training for muscle size and
  strength: a brief review on the theory. *Med Sci Sports Exerc* 2020;52(3):645-653. **Abstract only.**

**Williams 2017 (proponent result).** 18 studies, 1988 to 2015, 81 effects, 1RM in squat, bench or leg
press: periodised training beat non-periodised "(ES = 0.43, 95% CI 0.27-0.58; P < 0.001)". "Variation
in training stimuli appears to be vital for increasing maximal strength". Note: "Improvements in 1RM
were greater among untrained participants." Volume was not required to be equal between groups.

**Moesgaard 2022 (volume-equated).** 35 studies. 1RM favoured periodisation "(ES 0.31, 95% confidence
interval (CI) [0.04, 0.57]; Z = 2.28, P = 0.02)"; hypertrophy did not differ "(ES 0.13, 95% CI [-0.10,
0.36]; Z = 1.10, P = 0.27)". Undulating beat linear for 1RM, but only in trained people: "this was
only the case for trained and not previously untrained individuals".

**Buckner 2020 (sceptic).** "The periodization of resistance exercise is often touted as the most
effective strategy for optimizing muscle size and strength adaptations. This narrative persists
despite a lack of experimental evidence to demonstrate its superiority." And on its theoretical basis,
Selye's general adaptation syndrome: "To our knowledge, normal exercise in humans has never been shown
to produce a general adaptation syndrome."

**ACSM 2026's verdict.** Strength: "The impact of periodization on strength could not be
determined, although one review found that periodized programs were slightly favored over
nonperiodized programs to maximize strength gains under volume-equated conditions (117)", and 117 is
Moesgaard 2022; overall, "periodization is less important than previously hypothesized". (The
"although" half restored 2026-09-24 at lesson 5's review, F1; see R2.2.)

**Classification.** Contested for strength (modest effects, larger in some analyses, definitions vary
and are "often conflated" with programming); fairly settled that periodisation does not add
hypertrophy. For a beginner the evidence points both ways: Williams found the 1RM advantage larger in
untrained participants (without equal volume), while Moesgaard's undulating-over-linear result held
only in trained people. (Corrected 2026-09-24 at lesson 7's review, F9: this line had read "For a
beginner the question barely arises", which settled a contested question in the sceptics' direction
and left out Williams's untrained result.)

---

## R2.12 Individual variation and the ‘non-responder’ debate

**Sources.**
- Hubal MJ, Gordish-Dressman H, Thompson PD, et al., Clarkson PM. Variability in muscle size and
  strength gain after unilateral resistance training. *Med Sci Sports Exerc* 2005;37(6):964-972. PMID
  15947721. **Abstract only.**
- Ahtiainen JP, Walker S, Peltonen H, et al., Häkkinen K. Heterogeneity in resistance training-induced
  muscle strength and mass responses in men and women of different ages. *Age* 2016;38(1):10.
  doi:10.1007/s11357-015-9870-1. **Abstract only** (publisher does not allow XML download).
- Churchward-Venne TA, Tieland M, Verdijk LB, et al., van Loon LJC. There are no nonresponders to
  resistance-type exercise training in older men and women. *J Am Med Dir Assoc* 2015;16(5):400-411.
  doi:10.1016/j.jamda.2015.01.071. **Abstract only.**
- Bamman MM, Petrella JK, Kim JS, Mayhew DL, Cross JM. Cluster analysis tests the importance of
  myogenic gene expression during myofiber hypertrophy in humans. *J Appl Physiol* 2007;102(6):2232-2239.
  **Abstract only.**
- Pelland JC et al. The effect of resistance training volume on individual-level skeletal muscle
  adaptations: a novel replicated within-participant unilateral trial. bioRxiv preprint,
  doi:10.1101/2025.07.24.666533. **Abstract only; not peer reviewed.**

**Hubal 2005 (the famous range).** 585 people (342 women, 243 men) at eight centres; 12 weeks of
progressive training of the non-dominant elbow flexors; biceps size by MRI. "Size changes ranged from
-2 to +59% (-0.4 to +13.6 cm), 1RM strength gains ranged from 0 to +250% (0 to +10.2 kg), and MVC
changes ranged from -32 to +149% (-15.9 to +52.6 kg)." (The unit prints as "cm" in the PubMed text; a
cross-sectional area is in square centimetres.) "Men and women exhibit wide ranges of response to
resistance training, with some subjects showing little to no gain, and others showing profound
changes, increasing size by over 10 cm and doubling their strength." Sex: "Men experienced 2.5% greater gains
for CSA (P < 0.01) compared with women. Despite greater absolute gains in men, relative increases in
strength measures were greater in women versus men (P < 0.05)." (Corrected 2026-09-24, lesson 4's
review, F4, against the PubMed abstract: the entry had quoted from "relative increases" on, dropping
the absolute-gains clause.) The conclusion's sex sentence (added 2026-09-24, lesson 9's review, P12,
checked against the PubMed abstract): "Men had only a slight advantage in relative size gains compared
with women, whereas women outpaced men considerably in relative gains in strength." This, not
Roberts 2020, is the source read for a *relative* (per cent) size comparison; the design is one arm's
elbow flexors for 12 weeks.

**Ahtiainen 2016.** 287 untrained adults aged 19 to 78 plus 72 controls, pooled. Size change "4.8 ±
6.1 % (range from -11 to 30 %)", strength "21.1 ± 11.5 % (range from -8 to 60 %)". "Age and sex did
not affect to the RT responses." Allowing for control-group noise, "29 and 7 % of the subjects were
defined as low responders for the RT-induced changes in muscle size and strength, respectively."

**Proponents of the non-responder concept.** Bamman 2007 clustered 66 previously untrained people
after 16 weeks as "extreme responders (Xtr, n=17), modest responders (Mod, n=32), and nonresponders
(Non, n=17) based on mean myofiber hypertrophy", with fibre growth averaging "2,475 microm2 in Xtr,
1,111 microm2 in Mod, and -16 microm2 in Non." This is the clearest proponent use of the term read
here; note it defines non-response on one outcome (fibre size).

**Opponents.** Churchward-Venne 2015, 12 weeks (n = 110) and 24 weeks (n = 85) of supervised training
in adults over 65: "Nonresponsiveness was not apparent in any subject, as a positive adaptive response
on at least one training outcome was apparent in every subject." And: "The level of responsiveness was
strongly affected by the duration of the exercise intervention, with more positive responses
following more prolonged exercise training. We conclude that there are no nonresponders to the
benefits of resistance-type exercise training on lean body mass, fiber size, strength, or function in
the older population."

**The measurement-error argument.** The Pelland 2025 preprint trained each leg at a different volume
across two 11-week phases in 16 people to separate true individual differences from noise: "Despite
substantial gross variability, we failed to detect irrefutable evidence of meaningful IRV" and
"gross variability in training outcomes does not necessarily indicate true inter-individual
differences". Small, preprint.

**Classification.** Established: measured responses vary enormously. Contested: how much of that is
true biology versus measurement error, day-to-day variation and too-short studies, and whether anyone
truly fails to respond on every outcome. Partly a definitional dispute (non-responder on *what*, over
*how long*).

**Good for.** Telling a learner that their result may be far from the average without being told
they are a ‘non-responder’; the course's refusal point (it cannot predict any one person's response)
fits here.

---

## R2.13 Free weights versus machines

**Source.** Haugen ME, Vårvik FT, Larsen S, Haugen AS, van den Tillaar R, Bjørnsen T. Effect of
free-weight vs. machine-based strength training on maximal strength, hypertrophy and jump performance:
a systematic review and meta-analysis. *BMC Sports Sci Med Rehabil* 2023;15(1):103.
doi:10.1186/s13102-023-00713-4. PMCID PMC10426227. **Full text.**

(The brief named ‘Haugen 2023 PeerJ’; the paper found and read is in *BMC Sports Science, Medicine and
Rehabilitation*.)

**Population.** Thirteen studies, 1016 participants (789 men, 219 women), adults 18 to 60, "The
average duration of the studies was 8.9 ± 1.9 weeks (range 6–12)". "None of the studies examined
competitive athletes."

**Findings.** "Strength in free-weight tests increased significantly more with free-weight training
than with machines (SMD: -0.210, CI: -0.391, -0.029, p = 0.023), while strength in machine-based tests
tended to increase more with machine training than with free-weights (SMD: 0.291, CI: -0.017, 0.600, p
= 0.064)." In direct comparison, no differences for dynamic strength, isometric strength, jump or
hypertrophy "(SMD: -0.055, CI: -0.397, 0.287, p = 0.751)". Conclusion: "Current body of evidence
indicates that strength changes are specific to the training modality, and the choice between
free-weights and machines are down to individual preferences and goals."

**Good for.** Another specificity example, and a practical permission: pick the equipment you will use.

---

## R2.14 Sex and age

**Sources.**
- Roberts BM, Nuckols G, Krieger JW. Sex differences in resistance training: a systematic review and
  meta-analysis. *J Strength Cond Res* 2020;34(5):1448-1460. doi:10.1519/JSC.0000000000003521.
  **Abstract only.** (The brief said *Sports Medicine*; it is *JSCR*.)
- Peterson MD, Rhea MR, Sen A, Gordon PM. Resistance exercise for muscular strength in older adults: a
  meta-analysis. *Ageing Res Rev* 2010;9(3):226-237. doi:10.1016/j.arr.2010.03.004. PMCID PMC2892859.
  **Full text** (via NCBI).
- Borde R, Hortobágyi T, Granacher U. Dose-response relationships of resistance training in healthy
  old adults: a systematic review and meta-analysis. *Sports Med* 2015;45(12):1693-1720.
  doi:10.1007/s40279-015-0385-9. PMCID PMC4656698. **Full text.**
- Grgic J, Garofolini A, Orazem J, Sabol F, Schoenfeld BJ, Pedisic Z. Effects of resistance training on
  muscle size and strength in very elderly adults: a systematic review and meta-analysis of randomized
  controlled trials. *Sports Med* 2020;50(11):1983-1999. **Abstract only.**

**Sex (Roberts 2020).** Same protocol in young to middle-aged men and women. Hypertrophy: "no
significant difference between males and females (effect size [ES] = 0.07 ± 0.06; P = 0.31; I = 0)".
Upper-body strength: "a significant effect favoring females (ES = -0.60 ± 0.16; P = 0.002; I = 72.1)".
Lower body: no significant difference. Hedge: "it is possible that untrained females display a higher
capacity to increase upper-body strength than males. Further research is required to clarify why this
difference occurs only in the upper body and whether the differences are due to neural, muscular,
motor learning, or are an artifact of the short duration of studies included." Consistent with Hubal
2005 (relative strength gains greater in women). These are **relative** gains; men usually gain more
in absolute kilograms. *Correction, 2026-09-24 (course-wide neutrality audit, following R5.15's
correction):* Roberts reports **effect sizes**, not per cent change, and uses "relative" only for
upper-body strength. "These are relative gains" was this file's gloss and carried into lesson 4 as
"as a share of where each person started". For a per-cent comparison by sex, use Hubal 2005 (R2.12).

**Older adults (Peterson 2010).** 47 studies, 1079 participants aged 50 or over (randomised and
non-randomised). "Strength increases ranged from 9.8 – 31.6 kg, and percent changes were 29 ± 2, 24 ±
2, 33 ± 3, and 25 ± 2, respectively for leg press, chest press, knee extension, and lat pull." "RE is
effective for improving strength among older adults, particularly with higher intensity training."

**Older adults (Borde 2015).** 25 RCTs, 819 participants, mean age 70.4 (range 60 to 90), mean training
period 21.2 weeks. "RT improved muscle strength substantially (mean SMDbs = 1.57; 25 studies), but had
small effects on measures of muscle morphology (mean SMDbs = 0.42; nine studies)." Hedge: "the poor
overall methodological study quality (mean PEDro score 4.6 points) and the considerable large
heterogeneity".

**Very elderly (Grgic 2020).** 22 RCTs, 75 and over. Strength "[difference in ES = 0.97; 95%
confidence interval (CI) 0.50, 1.44; p = 0.001]"; in those 80 and over, "(difference in ES = 1.28; 95%
CI 0.28, 2.29; p = 0.020)". Handgrip did not differ significantly. "There were minimal reports of
adverse events associated with the training programs in the included studies."

**Good for.** Two myths at once: that women gain strength poorly (effect sizes equal or better in
Roberts; relative strength gains greater in Hubal, R2.12),
and that it is too late at 70 or 80 (strength gains are large; size gains smaller).

---

## R2.15 Concurrent training and the ‘interference effect’

**Sources.**
- Wilson JM, Marin PJ, Rhea MR, et al. Concurrent training: a meta-analysis examining interference of
  aerobic and resistance exercises. *J Strength Cond Res* 2012;26(8):2293-2307. **Abstract only.**
- Schumann M, Feuerbacher JF, Sünkeler M, et al. Compatibility of concurrent aerobic and strength
  training for skeletal muscle size and function: an updated systematic review and meta-analysis.
  *Sports Med* 2022;52(3):601-612. doi:10.1007/s40279-021-01587-7. PMCID PMC8891239. **Full text.**

**Wilson 2012.** 21 studies. Mean effect sizes, strength training alone versus concurrent: hypertrophy
1.23 versus 0.85; strength 1.76 versus 1.44; power 0.91 versus 0.55 ("Significant differences were
found between all the 3 groups" for power). "resistance training concurrently with running, but not
cycling, resulted in significant decrements in both hypertrophy and strength." "interference effects
of endurance training are a factor of the modality, frequency, and duration of the endurance training
selected."

**Schumann 2022.** 43 studies. Maximal strength SMD "- 0.06 (95% confidence interval [CI] - 0.20 to
0.09; p = 0.446)", explosive strength "- 0.28 (95% CI - 0.48 to - 0.08; p = 0.007)", hypertrophy "-
0.01 (95% CI - 0.16 to 0.18; p = 0.919)". "Concurrent aerobic and strength training does not
compromise muscle hypertrophy and maximal strength development. However, explosive strength gains may
be attenuated, especially when aerobic and strength training are performed in the same session."

**Classification.** Fairly settled for recreational amounts: doing cardio does not stop you getting
stronger or bigger; the possible cost is to explosive power, and at high endurance volumes. The two
meta-analyses differ on running; the newer and larger one found no moderator effect of running
versus cycling.

---

## R2.16 Who these studies trained, and for how long

No single meta-research paper was found that characterises the whole RT literature, so this is
assembled from the meta-analyses' own descriptions (each checked above):

| Source | Participants | Duration |
|---|---|---|
| Pelland 2026 (67 studies) | 79.1% male; mean age 25.16 | 10.42 ± 4.48 weeks (preprint) |
| Lopez 2021 (28 studies) | mean age 23.4; "Most of the studies involved men (19 of 28, or 67.9%" (not "men only", corrected 2026-09-24); 75% untrained | not extracted |
| Grgic 2022 failure (15 studies) | "All participants in the studies were young adults" | median 8 weeks (6 to 14) |
| Haugen 2023 (13 studies) | 789 men, 219 women | 8.9 ± 1.9 weeks |
| Currier 2023 (178 studies) | 45% women (strength network) | 6 weeks minimum |
| ACSM 2026 (137 reviews) | "much of the evidence synthesized here is from inexperienced trainees" | 6 to 52 weeks |
| Borde 2015 (older adults) | mean age 70.4 | 21.2 weeks mean |

**The long-term exception.** Steele J, et al. Long-term time-course of strength adaptation to minimal
dose resistance training through retrospective longitudinal growth modeling. *Res Q Exerc Sport*
2023;94(4):913-930. PMID 35591809. **Abstract only.** Observational training records from "a private
international exercise company": "14,690 participants (60% female; aged 48 ± 11 years)" doing
"1x/week, single sets to momentary failure of six exercises" for up to "352 weeks (~6.8 years)".
Findings: by about one to two years "strength had practically reached a" plateau (the authors put the word in quotation marks), but with "approximately ~30-50%
gains over the first year reaching ~50-60% of baseline 6 years later." No control group; company data;
the authors say it is "unclear if the" plateau "can be overcome through alternative approaches".

**Good for.** The course's critical-reading lesson: the typical trial is two to three months long in
young, often male, untrained volunteers, testing 1RM on the trained lift. That is the population and
the outcome that ‘the evidence says’ usually refers to.

---

## R2.17 Detraining and ‘muscle memory’

**Sources.**
- Bosquet L, Berryman N, Dupuy O, et al., Mujika I. Effect of training cessation on muscular
  performance: a meta-analysis. *Scand J Med Sci Sports* 2013;23(3):e140-149. doi:10.1111/sms.12047.
  **Abstract only.**
- Blocquiaux S, Gorski T, Van Roie E, et al., Thomis M. The effect of resistance training, detraining
  and retraining on muscle strength and power, myofibre size, satellite cells and myonuclei in older
  men. *Exp Gerontol* 2020;133:110860. doi:10.1016/j.exger.2020.110860. **Abstract only.** (A
  corrigendum exists, not opened.)
- Yang Y, et al. Training session and detraining duration affect lower limb muscle strength
  maintenance in middle-aged and older adults: a systematic review and meta-analysis. *J Aging Phys Act*
  2022;30(3):552-566. **Abstract only.**

**Bosquet 2013.** 103 studies. Detraining effects: submaximal strength "SMD (95% CI) = -0.62 (-0.80 to
-0.45)", maximal force "-0.46 (-0.54 to -0.37)", maximal power "-0.20 (-0.28 to -0.13)". "A
dose-response relationship between the amplitude of SMD and the duration of training cessation was
identified. The effect of resistance training cessation was found to be larger in older people (> 65
years old)." Note: these are losses from the trained level, not necessarily back below baseline.

**Blocquiaux 2020.** 30 older men, 12 weeks training, then detraining and retraining of similar
length. "Detraining resulted in a modest loss of strength and power (-5 to -15%, p ≤ .004)". "Less
than eight weeks of retraining were needed to reach the post-training level of one-repetition maximum
strength." Conclusion: "Gained strength and power and fibre type changes were partially preserved
following 12 weeks of detraining, allowing for a fast recovery of the 1RM performance following
retraining."

**Yang 2022.** "Muscle strength after detraining is still higher than the level before training".

**Good for.** Reassurance with honesty: a break costs some strength, more the longer it lasts and the
older you are, and it comes back faster than it came. The cellular mechanism (myonuclear muscle
memory) is a hypothesis in humans; Blocquiaux's biopsy data, from a subset of six men, are described by the authors as "in support of the myonuclear domain theory".

---

## R2.18 Estimating 1RM from a set of reps

**Sources.**
- Marzagao T. A weight-dependent 1RM prediction equation optimized on 303,494 near-failure sets across
  388 exercises. arXiv:2603.17495 (preprint; author affiliated with Fitbod, Inc., a fitness app
  company). **Full text** (literature review section is what is used here).
- Reynolds JM, Gordon TJ, Robergs RA. Prediction of one repetition maximum strength from multiple
  repetition maximum testing and anthropometry. *J Strength Cond Res* 2006;20(3):584-592.
  **Abstract only.**
- Epley 1985 and Brzycki 1993 themselves: **not opened** (see Unread).

**The formulas (as printed in the Marzagao preprint's literature review).** "The Epley equation, 1RM =
w × (1 + r / 30) , originated not from a peer-reviewed study but from a poundage chart published in a
resistance training manual for athletes at the University of Nebraska (Epley, 1985)." "Brzycki (1993)
proposed the equation 1RM = w / (1.0278 − 0.0278 × r) in a practitioner-oriented article in the
Journal of Physical Education, Recreation & Dance." (w is the weight lifted, r the reps to failure.
Brzycki's form is algebraically the same as w × 36 / (37 − r).) Worked example: 60 kg for 8 reps gives
Epley 76 kg, Brzycki about 74.5 kg. The same preprint: "A common feature of all classical equations is
that they were derived from a narrow empirical base: typically young, trained males performing the
bench press."

**Reynolds 2006.** 70 adults (34 men, 36 women) aged 18 to 69, bench (chest) press and leg press:
"the 5RM data produced the greatest prediction accuracy", and "Data reveal that no more than 10
repetitions should be used in linear equations to estimate 1RM for the LP and CP actions."

**Good for.** A practical lesson tool (you do not need to test a true max to know roughly where you
are), with the caveats that estimates get worse above about 10 reps and differ by exercise.

**Caveat.** The Marzagao preprint is not peer reviewed and comes from a company that sells a training
app; it is used here only for its description of the classical equations' origins, which should be
cross-checked against Brzycki 1993 or a textbook (NSCA *Essentials*) at Stage 2 if a lesson leans on
it. **Lesson 7 leans on it, and the cross-check was not done** (noted 2026-09-24 at lesson 7's
review): the lesson attributes the Nebraska-chart origin to the preprint, names the author's Fitbod
affiliation, and says Epley 1985 was not opened. Its step of adding the reps in reserve to the reps
done before applying the formula is the lesson's own inference, labelled there. Reynolds's ten-rep
limit is for the leg press and chest press only.

---

## R2.19 Does muscle growth cause strength gain? (a live dispute)

**Sources.**
- Taber CB, Vigotsky A, Nuckols G, Haun CT. Exercise-induced myofibrillar hypertrophy is a
  contributory cause of gains in muscle strength. *Sports Med* 2019;49(7):993-997.
  doi:10.1007/s40279-019-01107-8. **Full text** (author-hosted PDF, andrewvigotsky.com).
- Loenneke JP, Buckner SL, Dankel SJ, Abe T. Exercise-induced changes in muscle size do not contribute
  to exercise-induced changes in muscle strength. *Sports Med* 2019;49(7):987-991.
  doi:10.1007/s40279-019-01106-9. **Not opened** (no abstract indexed; Springer blocked).
- Loenneke JP, Dankel SJ, Bell ZW, Buckner SL, Mattocks KT, Jessee MB, Abe T. Is muscle growth a mechanism for increasing
  strength? *Med Hypotheses* 2019;125:51-56. doi:10.1016/j.mehy.2019.02.030. PMID 30902152.
  **Abstract only.**

**The sceptical side (the Loenneke group, from the Loenneke et al. 2019 *Medical Hypotheses* abstract, a proponent source).**
"at present, there is no experimental evidence that would support any paradigm in which muscle
hypertrophy is a mechanism for increasing strength with exercise." Their reason, from the same
abstract (added 2026-09-24, lesson 4's review, F3, against PubMed): "Herein, we suggest that there are
dissociations between changes in muscle size and strength following exercise, anabolic drug
administration, and aging (to a point). These dissociations occur throughout the literature,
suggesting that these changes may be completely separate phenomena." "We are not dismissing the
potential importance of maintaining muscle mass, particularly in clinical populations. What we are
suggesting, however, is that muscle function may not necessarily be improved by these exercise or
pharmacological induced increases in muscle size."

**The contributory-cause side (Taber et al., proponents).** "We posit that substantial confounding due
to “other factors” is likely prevalent for relatively untrained lifters." "We, therefore, find the
question of “Does hypertrophy contribute to strength gain?” less interesting than “To what extent and
under what circumstances does hypertrophy contribute to strength gain?”" Conclusion: "we conclude
that in the long-term muscular hypertrophy contributes to strength." And the methodological point that
connects to R2.16: "short-term studies utilizing untrained subjects may not fully capture the
influence of hypertrophy on strength." Their concession (added 2026-09-24, lesson 4's review, F3,
against the author PDF): "We concede that strength can increase without increases in muscle size
(not necessary) [12], and that an increase in muscle size may not be accompanied by an increase in
strength (not sufficient)". The paper replies to the Loenneke group's *Sports Medicine* paper in its
section 7, "Reply to Loenneke et al.", not to the *Medical Hypotheses* abstract.

**Classification.** Contested among researchers. Nobody in either camp denies that bigger muscles and
stronger people tend to go together; the dispute is about causation within training, and it is sharpened
by the fact that most trials are short and in beginners, where neural and skill changes dominate
(R2.4). A lesson can present this as a clean example of a scientific disagreement in the researchers'
own words, and should not take a side.

---

## R2.20 Creatine (brief, for cross-reference)

**Source.** Kreider RB, Kalman DS, Antonio J, et al. International Society of Sports Nutrition position
stand: safety and efficacy of creatine supplementation in exercise, sport, and medicine. *J Int Soc
Sports Nutr* 2017;14:18. doi:10.1186/s12970-017-0173-z. PMCID PMC5469049. **Full text.**

**Position.** "Creatine monohydrate is the most effective ergogenic nutritional supplement currently
available to athletes with the intent of increasing high-intensity exercise capacity and lean body
mass during training." Dosing: "The quickest method of increasing muscle creatine stores may be to
consume ~0.3 g/kg/day of creatine monohydrate for 5–7-days followed by 3–5 g/day thereafter to
maintain elevated stores." Side effects: "The only consistently reported side effect from creatine
supplementation that has been described in the literature has been weight gain".

**Conflicts, which a lesson must state if it cites this.** The stand was prepared "at the request of
the Council for Responsible Nutrition and ISSN" (a supplement-industry trade association), and the
first author "has received externally-funded grants from industry to conduct research on creatine,
serves as a scientific and legal consultant, and is a university approved scientific advisor for
Nutrabolt." Several other authors declare industry ties. The supplements researcher should decide
whether an independent source (for example a government or non-industry review) is needed before any
lesson recommends it. This section recommends that the course **not** recommend creatine on this
source alone.

---

## What this section found that should shape the course

- **Starting is the big effect.** Every well-designed synthesis agrees that almost any resistance
  program beats none, for strength and, less dramatically, for muscle size, at every adult age studied
  (R2.2, R2.3, R2.14). This should be the first and loudest message.
- **Strength and muscle size respond to different knobs.** Heavier loads matter for 1RM strength;
  total hard sets matter for muscle size; load barely matters for size if sets are hard (R2.6, R2.7,
  R2.8).
- **Diminishing returns are the rule.** One or two hard sets per exercise, twice a week, captures most
  of the short-term strength benefit; more sets keep adding muscle, each set buying less (R2.8, R2.2).
- **Strength is specific.** The largest gains appear on the exercise practised; gains on untrained
  tests are about half the size (R2.5, R2.13). The course should teach learners to ask ‘measured on what?’.
- **The first weeks are mostly the nervous system learning** (R2.4), which explains fast early
  progress and why it slows.
- **The evidence base is short, young, male-leaning and untrained** (R2.16). ‘Studies show’ in this
  field usually means 8 to 12 weeks in 20-somethings. Long-term and trained-lifter questions are much
  less certain.
- **Effort matters but failure is optional** (R2.10), and people misjudge how close they are to
  failure by about a rep, more on light, long sets. A usable teaching target: stop with roughly 1 to 3
  reps left, as ACSM 2026 suggests.
- **Individual responses vary enormously**, but ‘non-responder’ is a contested label (R2.12). The
  course cannot predict any one learner's response.
- **Guidance changed in 2026** from detailed prescriptions to ‘any progressive RT, twice a week, all
  major muscle groups, with high effort’ (a paraphrase) (R2.1 versus R2.2). The course should teach the current stand
  and can use the change as a lesson in how guidelines evolve.

## Misconceptions this section can correct

| Misconception | Correction | Source |
|---|---|---|
| You must lift heavy to build muscle. | Muscle growth is similar across roughly 30 to 90% 1RM when sets are taken very hard; heavy loads matter mainly for 1RM strength. | R2.6 (Schoenfeld 2017, Morton 2016, Lopez 2021) |
| Every set must go to failure. | Failure is not required for strength or size gains in the trials; near-failure is enough. | R2.10 (Grgic 2022, Refalo 2023), R2.2 |
| More is always better. | Returns diminish; strength plateaus at low weekly volumes in the pooled data. | R2.8 |
| You need a complicated periodised plan. | Periodisation does not add hypertrophy and its strength benefit is uncertain; not needed for beginners. | R2.11, R2.2 |
| Free weights are superior to machines (or vice versa). | No difference in direct comparison; each improves its own tests more. | R2.13 |
| Early gains mean muscles have grown. | Early gains are largely neural. | R2.4 |
| The post-workout hormone surge builds muscle. | Acute hormone rises were unrelated to gains. | R2.6 (Morton 2016) |
| Women gain strength less well than men. | Relative gains are similar, and in the upper body greater in women, in short studies. | R2.14 (Roberts 2020, Hubal 2005) |
| Older people cannot gain much strength. | Large strength gains at 65+, 75+ and 80+. | R2.14 (Peterson 2010, Borde 2015, Grgic 2020) |
| Cardio kills your gains. | Not for maximal strength or size; possibly for explosive power. | R2.15 (Schumann 2022) |
| Train each muscle at least twice a week or it will not grow. | With volume equated, frequency does not meaningfully change hypertrophy. | R2.9 (Schoenfeld 2019) |
| If you stop, you lose it all. | Losses are partial and regained quickly. | R2.17 |

## Contested questions in this section

1. **Does training closer to failure build more muscle?** For: Robinson 2024 (dose-response, hypertrophy
   increases as RIR falls; "exploratory"). Against or weaker: Refalo 2023 ("trivial advantage", no
   advantage for strict failure, possibly non-linear); Grgic 2022 (no overall difference; small effect
   in trained people). ACSM 2026: failure "did not consistently impact training outcomes".
   *Classification:* empirical, contested, with converging practice advice (train close to failure,
   failure optional).
2. **Is periodisation superior for strength?** For: Williams 2017 ("Variation in training stimuli
   appears to be vital"), Moesgaard 2022 (modest effect in volume-equated trials, in trained people for
   undulating). Against: Buckner 2020 ("despite a lack of experimental evidence to demonstrate its
   superiority"). ACSM 2026: "could not be determined". *Classification:* empirical, contested;
   partly definitional.
3. **Do non-responders exist?** For: Bamman 2007 (cluster of "nonresponders" on fibre growth). Against:
   Churchward-Venne 2015 ("there are no nonresponders"); Pelland 2025 preprint (variability may be
   noise). *Classification:* empirical and definitional.
4. **Does hypertrophy contribute to strength?** Contributory: Taber 2019. Sceptical: Loenneke 2019 (*Med Hypotheses*)
   (Loenneke group). *Classification:* empirical and causal-inference dispute among researchers.
5. **Heavy versus light for general (non-specific) strength.** Hammert 2026: inconclusive, point
   estimate favouring heavy. *Classification:* empirical, unresolved.
6. **How much should guidelines optimise versus simplify?** ACSM 2026 and Currier 2023 explicitly argue
   that participation matters more than optimisation ("from our perspective"). This is partly a value
   claim about public-health priorities, and should be labelled as such in a lesson; no written
   opposing proponent statement was read, though the 2009 stand represents the older, more
   prescriptive approach.

## Unread or unreachable

- ACSM 2009 position stand full text (paywalled; abstract read) and its evidence grades.
- Pelland et al. 2026 *Sports Medicine* published full text (Springer bot challenge); the SportRxiv
  preprint was read instead, and figures from it may differ in the published version.
- Robinson 2024, Halperin 2022, Grgic 2018, Schoenfeld 2016, Williams 2017, Moesgaard 2022 full texts
  (Springer; abstracts read). The published comment on Williams 2017 (PMID 29178086), not opened.
- Loenneke et al. 2019 *Sports Med* *Exercise-induced changes in muscle size do not contribute to exercise-induced changes in muscle strength* (no
  abstract available; the group's position was taken from the Loenneke et al. 2019 *Med Hypotheses* abstract).
- Moritani and deVries 1979 and Sale 1988 full texts (abstracts read). How Moritani estimated
  hypertrophy is therefore unverified.
- Hubal 2005, Ahtiainen 2016, Churchward-Venne 2015, Bamman 2007, Blocquiaux 2020, Bosquet 2013,
  Roberts 2020, Spitz 2023, Zourdos 2016, Wilson 2012, Steele 2023 (minimal dose), Grgic 2020 very
  elderly: abstracts only.
- Epley 1985 (Nebraska poundage chart) and Brzycki 1993 (JOPERD): not opened; formulas taken from the
  Marzagao preprint's review, which should be checked against a textbook.
- LeSuer et al. 1997 (accuracy of seven 1RM equations): named in Marzagao, not opened.
- Wikipedia and practitioner sites (Stronger by Science, etc.): used only to find sources, not cited.
- No meta-research paper specifically characterising participants and durations across the whole RT
  literature was found; R2.16 is assembled from the meta-analyses' own descriptions.
- Creatine: no non-industry position stand was sought in this section (belongs with the supplements
  researcher).

## Part R3. Endurance (aerobic) training, and how effort and intensity are measured

*Researcher 3, Stage 1, 2026-09-24. Raw texts are saved under `SCRATCH/research/raw/r3/`. Every
passage inside double quotation marks below was checked with `grep -F` against the saved text named
in its entry (a script re-ran the check over the whole file at the end; see the note at the foot).
Read levels are stated per source. 'Abstract only' means the PubMed/Europe PMC abstract and nothing
else. Several publishers (Wiley, Elsevier/ScienceDirect, Cell Press, JACC, LWW, Teesside's
repository) served a bot-verification page to both curl and the browser; I did not attempt to get
past those, so those papers are abstract-only here and the entries say so.*

---

## R3.1 What VO2max is, and what limits it

### R3.1a Bassett DR, Howley ET. Limiting factors for maximum oxygen uptake and determinants of endurance performance. *Med Sci Sports Exerc* 2000;32(1):70-84. doi:10.1097/00005768-200001000-00012

- **Read level:** abstract only (`abs_10647532.txt`). Full text is paywalled.
- **Design:** narrative review. No population of its own.
- **Key claims, verbatim:** "In the exercising human, maximal oxygen uptake (VO2max) is limited by the ability of the cardiorespiratory system to deliver oxygen to the exercising muscles."
  The three lines of evidence it gives are altering oxygen delivery (blood doping, hypoxia,
  beta-blockade), that the training increase in VO2max comes mainly from maximal cardiac output, and
  that a small overperfused muscle can consume very large amounts of oxygen. It concludes "O2 delivery, not skeletal muscle O2 extraction, is viewed as the primary limiting factor for VO2max in exercising humans."
  On the muscle: "Metabolic adaptations in skeletal muscle are, however, critical for improving submaximal endurance performance."
  And: "The speed at lactate threshold (LT) integrates all three of these variables and is the best physiological predictor of distance running performance."
  The "three variables" are named in the sentence before: "Running economy and fractional utilization of VO2max also affect endurance performance."
  And on mitochondria: "Endurance training causes an increase in mitochondrial enzyme activities, which improves performance by enhancing fat oxidation and decreasing lactic acid accumulation at a given VO2."
  *(Added 2026-09-24 at lesson 3's Stage 4 review, checked against the PubMed abstract fetched
  through NCBI E-utilities: lesson 3 paraphrases the fraction-of-the-ceiling framing, and this entry
  did not carry the sentence it comes from.)*
- **Good for:** the lesson distinction between the ceiling (VO2max, set mainly by delivery: heart,
  blood) and how long and how fast you can go below it (muscle adaptations, economy, lactate
  threshold). This is the standard textbook framing.
- **Does not show:** anything about untrained beginners specifically, or health outcomes. Note the
  hedge "is viewed as": it states the prevailing view.
- **Caveat:** it uses the phrase "decreasing lactic acid accumulation", which is the older
  vocabulary R3.9 complicates.

### R3.1b Lundby C, Montero D, Joyner M. Biology of VO2max: looking under the physiology lamp. *Acta Physiol* 2017;220(2):218-228. doi:10.1111/apha.12827

- **Read level:** abstract only (`abs_27888580.txt`).
- **Design:** review/opinion by physiologists.
- **Key claims:** training-induced increases in VO2max are "largely facilitated by expansion of red blood cell volume and an associated improvement in stroke volume, which also adapts independent of changes in red blood cell volume."
  And point (iii): "All previously untrained individuals will respond to endurance exercise training in terms of improvements in VO"
  (the abstract continues with a subscript tag, "2 max provided the stimulus exceeds a certain volume
  and/or intensity"; I quote only up to the tag).
- **Good for:** the mechanism of improvement (more blood, bigger stroke volume), and for R3.6 (it is
  the same group's position on non-response).
- **Caveat:** "will respond" is a strong claim stated by proponents of the dose-dependence view;
  record it as their position.

### R3.1c Hellsten Y, Nyberg M. Cardiovascular adaptations to exercise training. *Compr Physiol* 2015;6(1):1-32. doi:10.1002/cphy.c140080

- **Read level:** abstract only (`abs_26756625.txt`).
- **Design:** narrative review, humans with some animal data.
- **Key claims, verbatim:** "The functionally most important adaptation is the improvement in maximal cardiac output which is the result of an enlargement in cardiac dimension, improved contractility, and an increase in blood volume, allowing for greater filling of the ventricles and a consequent larger stroke volume."
  On the periphery: "The microvascular net increases in size within the muscle allowing for an improved capacity for oxygen extraction by the muscle through a greater area for diffusion, a shorter diffusion distance, and a longer mean transit time for the erythrocyte to pass through the smallest blood vessels."
  Hedge on vascular function: "Endurance training may also induce alterations in the vasodilator capacity, although such adaptations are more pronounced in individuals with reduced vascular function."
- **Good for:** the central (heart, blood volume, stroke volume) and peripheral (capillaries)
  adaptations, from a citable review. Mitochondria are covered by R3.1a (mitochondrial enzyme
  activities) and by the muscle-biopsy data in R3.4c (citrate synthase).
- **Does not show:** time course or how much training produces each change.

### R3.1d The standard textbook: Kenney WL, Wilmore JH, Costill DL. *Physiology of Sport and Exercise*, 9th ed. Human Kinetics, ©2025. ISBN 9781718228429

- **Read level:** publisher's product page only (`hk.txt`): table of contents and blurb. No chapter
  text was available.
- **What the table of contents shows:** the topics this course touches sit in Chapter 5, Energy
  Expenditure (sections: Measuring, Estimating, Predicting Energy Expenditure); Chapter 6, Fatigue,
  Muscle Soreness, and Muscle Cramps; Chapter 9, Cardiorespiratory Responses to Acute Exercise;
  Chapter 10, Principles of Exercise Training; Chapter 12, Adaptations to Aerobic and Anaerobic
  Training (with a section, Adaptations to High-Intensity Interval Training); and Chapter 13,
  Prescription of Exercise for Health and Fitness.
- **Incidental but useful:** the author page says Wilmore "was one of five principal investigators for the HERITAGE Family Study", which ties the textbook to R3.6a.
- **Good for:** naming the standard text as the place the lesson's framing comes from. **Not
  citable for any specific claim**, since no chapter was read.
- McArdle, Katch & Katch, *Exercise Physiology*: **not opened at all** (see Unread).

## R3.2 Absolute intensity: METs and the Compendium

### R3.2a Herrmann SD, Willis EA, Ainsworth BE, et al. 2024 Adult Compendium of Physical Activities: a third update of the energy costs of human activities. *J Sport Health Sci* 2024;13(1):6-12. doi:10.1016/j.jshs.2023.10.010. PMC10818145

- **Read level:** full text (`herrmann2024.txt`), plus the activity tables on pacompendium.com for
  running, walking and bicycling (`pac_running.txt`, `pac_walking.txt`, `pac_bicycling.txt`).
- **Design:** systematic review of measured energy costs (indirect or direct calorimetry), searches
  January 2011 to March 2023, adults with a mean age of 19 to 59. Screened 32,173 abstracts and 1507
  full texts; extracted 2356 values from 701 papers.
- **Definition of a MET, verbatim:** "METs reflect the ratio of a PA's energy cost in mL/kg/min relative to a standardized RMR of 3.5 mL/kg/min or approximately 1.0 kcal/kg/h."
- **The intensity bands, verbatim:** "The Compendium classifies sedentary behaviors as 1.0–1.5 METs, light-intensity PAs as 1.6–2.9 METs, moderate-intensity PAs as 3.0–5.9 METs, and vigorous-intensity PAs as ≥6.0 METs, which are commonly used values in research and public health."
- **Size:** 1114 activities, 912 with measured and 202 with estimated values, 22 Major Headings.
  (An internal inconsistency: the Strengths section says estimated values fell "from 260 in the 2011
  update to 207 in this current update", while the abstract and Table 3 say 202. Cite 202 from
  Table 3 and the abstract, or avoid the number.)
- **MET-minutes, verbatim:** the 2018 US guideline volume "is equivalent to ∼500–1000 MET-min/week, which is sufficient to reduce the risks of morbidity and mortality."
- **The limitation the course needs, verbatim:** "the Compendium can serve as a starting point for prescribing individual activities but does not reflect precise individual EE values."
  They give an example of women with obesity expending about 8% to 15% more than women without
  obesity at the same treadmill speeds, and add the qualifier that carries the example's point:
  "EE may still be categorized in the same general intensity level (i.e., light intensity) but may not reflect an individual's more precisely measured EE."
  (Added 2026-09-24 at lesson 2's Stage 4 review.) The paper gives the Older Adult Compendium's
  reason itself (reference numbers removed): "RMR is comparatively lower in older adults, so in 2024, a Compendium for Older Adults ≥60 years was developed."
  The Adult Compendium is limited to ages 19 to 59, with data from those 60 and over removed. Scope: "the 2024 Adult Compendium is intended for use in non-disabled adults and adults without significant health conditions that affect EE or metabolism".
- **Example values from the website tables (as printed there):**
  - 17170, 3.0 METs, "Walking, 2.5 mph, firm, level surface"
  - 17190, 3.8, "Walking, 2.8 to 3.4 mph, level, moderate pace, firm surface"
  - 17200, 4.8, "Walking, 3.5 to 3.9 mph, level, brisk, firm surface, walking for exercise"
  - 12020, 7.5, "Jogging, general, self-selected pace"
  - 12030, 8.5, "Running, 5.0 to 5.2 mph (12 min/mile)"
  - 12050, 9.3, "Running, 6-6.3 mph (10 min/mile)"
  - 01010, 4.0, "Bicycling, <10 mph, leisure, to work or for pleasure (Taylor Code 115)"
  - 01030, 8.0, "Bicycling, 12-13.9 mph, leisure, moderate effort"
- **Good for:** what a MET is, why brisk walking is moderate and running is vigorous in the
  guidelines, and the honest limit: these are population averages, not your personal energy cost.
- **Does not show:** that 1 MET is anyone's actual resting rate. The paper itself notes RMR varies
  with age, sex, height and body composition, which is why there is now a separate Older Adult
  Compendium for those 60 and over.

### R3.2b CDC. How to Measure Physical Activity Intensity. Page dated Dec. 4, 2025. https://www.cdc.gov/physical-activity-basics/measuring/index.html

- **Read level:** full page, via browser text extraction (curl was refused by the server); the
  relevant sentences are saved in `cdc_measuring_browser.txt`.
- **Absolute intensity, verbatim:** "One MET equals the energy or oxygen used while sitting quietly. Physical activity that burns 3 to 5.9 METs is moderate intensity. Physical activity that burns 6.0 METs or more is vigorous intensity."
- **Relative intensity, verbatim:** "Relatively moderate-intensity activity is a level of effort of 5 or 6 on a scale of 0 to 10, where 0 is the level of effort of sitting, and 10 is maximal effort. Relatively vigorous-intensity activity begins at a 7 or 8 on this scale."
  And: "an activity that is vigorous for a person of average fitness may only be moderate for a very fit person."
- **Talk test, verbatim:** "As a rule of thumb, a person doing moderate-intensity aerobic activity can talk, but not sing, during the activity. A person doing vigorous-intensity activity cannot say more than a few words without pausing for a breath."
- **Observation worth recording:** CDC's older page titled Target Heart Rate and Estimated Maximum Heart
  Rate (the one that gave percentages of 220 minus age) now returns a Page Not Found at its
  current-site address, and the live intensity page gives only METs, the 0 to 10 effort scale and the
  talk test. I did not find a replacement heart-rate page. I cannot say why it was removed; the
  lesson must not claim a reason.
- The same definitions (3.0 to 5.9 METs; 5 or 6 on 0 to 10; vigorous begins at 7 or 8) appear in
  the *Physical Activity Guidelines for Americans*, 2nd ed., which R1 holds (copied here as
  `pag2.txt`; I only grep-checked these lines, and the guideline is R1's source).
