## What this section found that should shape the course

- **Risk concentrates in the inactive person who suddenly goes hard, and it is novelty, not lifting,
  that hurts people.** ACSM's screening, the heart-event data Whitfield summarises, the
  rhabdomyolysis guideline and the spin case series all say the same thing from different angles. The
  course's safety spine can be 'start below what you could do, and build'.
- **The safety callout has solid, quotable wording from four bodies** (4.3), plus the ER signs (4.4)
  and the pregnancy Box 3 (4.20). Callouts should be built only from those words.
- **Strength training is not a high-injury activity.** Competitive lifters report about 2 to 4
  injuries per 1000 hours against about 15 to 81 for soccer, rugby union and cricket (4.5), mostly
  minor. Strength work *prevents* sports injuries in trials (4.7). Keep the population (competitors,
  athletes) attached to every number.
- **Stretching's reputation outruns its evidence.** It has not been shown to prevent injury (4.13) or
  soreness (4.16); long static holds before explosive work cost a few per cent that a dynamic warm-up
  largely erases (4.12); lifting improves range of motion about as much as stretching (4.14).
- **Several 'safety rules' move load rather than remove it** (knees behind toes, 4.8), or are not
  supported by outcome data (lifting posture advice, 4.9). The honest lesson is about what each study
  measured.
- **Recovery gadgets mostly treat how you feel, not how you adapt.** Massage and foam rolling reduce
  soreness a little (4.18); regular cold water after lifting reduced gains in one trial (4.17).
- **Special populations are mostly 'yes, with conditions', not "no".** Pregnancy, older age, low bone
  mass, youth, back pain, diabetes and osteoarthritis all have guidance that encourages resistance
  training with specific stop-signs and supervision conditions. Each lesson that touches them must
  carry the condition with the permission.
- **The PAR-Q+ is the lay screening tool, and it forbids modification.** Link it, describe it,
  do not reproduce it.

## Misconceptions this section can correct

- 'Lifting weights is dangerous.' Keogh & Winwood 2017 (4.5); AHA 2023 on cardiac events (4.26).
- 'Weight training stunts children's growth / damages growth plates.' Faigenbaum & Myer 2010 (4.22),
  with its supervision condition.
- 'Stretching before exercise prevents injury.' Thacker 2004, Lauersen 2014, Behm 2016 (4.12, 4.13).
- 'Stretching after exercise stops soreness.' Herbert 2011 Cochrane (4.16).
- 'Lifting makes you stiff / muscle-bound.' Afonso 2021, Alizadeh 2023 (4.14).
- 'Never let your knees go past your toes.' Fry 2003 (4.8), with its small-sample caveat.
- 'If you're not sore, it didn't work.' Cheung 2003 and McHugh 2003 by inference (4.16), labelled as
  inference.
- 'An ice bath after lifting helps you grow.' Roberts 2015 (4.17).
- 'Everyone should see a doctor before starting exercise.' ACSM 2015 / Whitfield 2017, ADA 2016, NIA
  (4.1, 4.3, 4.24): not if asymptomatic and starting light to moderate.
- 'Women with osteoporosis must not lift heavy.' LIFTMOR (4.21), within its supervised,
  otherwise-healthy sample.
- 'People with back pain should rest.' Hayden 2021 (4.23).
- 'Pregnant women should not lift weights.' ACOG 804, Canadian guideline (4.20), absent
  contraindications.

## Contested questions in this section

1. **Should lifters avoid rounding the lower back?**
   - Caution: repeated flexion under load herniates discs in pig spines; "disc herniation may be more
     linked to repeated flexion extension motions than applied joint compression" (Callaghan &
     McGill 2001). Proponent's own words read at abstract level only.
   - Counter: in people, lumbar flexion while lifting is not shown to predict back pain (low-quality
     evidence, Saraceni 2020), and lifting-technique training does not prevent back pain in workers
     (moderate quality, Verbeek 2011).
   - Classification: contested empirical question; the mechanism is established in the laboratory,
     its relevance to human training is not.
2. **Static stretching before training.**
   - Against: measurable short-term performance loss, bigger with holds of 60 s or more (Behm 2016).
   - For: the loss shrinks when dynamic activity follows, and range of motion rises; Behm et al.
     themselves recommend stretching within a full warm-up.
   - Classification: largely settled in the middle; the residual disagreement is about context and
     value (what performance matters to this person).
3. **Lifting belts.** Performance aid versus possible false security; evidence read is occupational
   and old (Rys & Konz 1995; NIOSH via Thomas 1999). Classification: unsettled; no gym evidence read.
4. **How heavy for people with osteoporosis?** LIFTMOR's supervised >85% 1RM versus AHA's low to
   moderate for severe osteoporosis. Classification: established within each scope; contested only
   if scope is dropped.
5. **Is CrossFit riskier than other training?** Reviews say comparable (Klimek 2018; Rodríguez 2022)
   but on low-quality, mostly retrospective data. Classification: weakly established, low certainty.
6. **Does the knee rule protect knees?** It lowers knee torque and raises hip torque (Fry 2003, seven
   men). Whether that matters for injury is unstudied here. Classification: a value trade-off on thin
   data.

## Unread or unreachable

- Riebe et al. 2015 full text (paywalled; the algorithm read via Whitfield 2017's reproduction).
- 2025 CHAMP/USU exertional rhabdomyolysis guideline PDF (only the USU news release read); O'Connor
  et al. 2021 *Curr Sports Med Rep* summary (abstract only).
- Lauersen 2014, Lauersen 2018, Thorborg 2017, Mottola 2018, Lloyd 2014, Faigenbaum 2016 (BJSM refused
  download; abstracts only; Mottola's recommendations read via CSEP).
- Behm 2016, Thacker 2004, Fry 2003, Saraceni 2020, MacDougall 1985, Cheung 2003, McHugh 2003,
  Roberts 2015, Meeusen 2013, LIFTMOR 2018, Keogh's published version (read the accepted manuscript
  instead), Callaghan & McGill 2001, McGill 1998, Verbeek 2011, Pescatello 2004, Herbert 2011: abstract
  only.
- AAP clinical report on youth resistance training (Stricker et al. 2020): abstract only.
- ACOG 804 contraindication tables (did not survive text extraction).
- NICE NG226 evidence review C.
- AHA public 'warning signs during exercise' page (heart.org blocked the download).
- McGill's books (*Low Back Disorders*, *Back Mechanic*): not opened; no direct proponent statement on
  flexion during gym lifting was read.
- Kuijer's individual studies (only as a co-author of Verbeek 2011).
- Any sports-science review of lifting belts: none found.
- Any study relating soreness to hypertrophy or strength gains: none found or read.
- Montalvo 2017 and Mehrab 2023 CrossFit papers: full text downloaded but only abstracts studied.

## Part R5. Textbooks, syllabi, practitioner sources, popular claims, and free resources

*Researcher 5, Stage 1, 2026-09-24. Raw downloads are in `SCRATCH/research/raw/r5/`. Every passage in
quotation marks below was copied from a file in that folder and checked with `grep -F` against a
whitespace-flattened copy of it (`*.flat`: line breaks and non-breaking spaces turned into single spaces, nothing else changed).
Where the only copy of a page was captured through a browser (three pages behind Cloudflare), the file
says so in its first line, and the quotation was checked against that capture. PubMed abstracts were
fetched from NCBI E-utilities (efetch, plain text), not from a summariser.*

**A note on what "abstract only" means in this section.** Most trials and meta-analyses below were read
at abstract level. The abstract gives design, population, duration and the headline numbers; it does
not give the limitations section, the funding, or the per-study table. A lesson that leans on any of
them for more than its headline should get the full text first.

---

## Part A. The standard textbooks

### R5.1 NSCA, *Essentials of Strength Training and Conditioning* (Haff and Triplett, eds.)

- **Editions.** 4th edition: Human Kinetics, ISBN 9781718210868 (the HKPropel reissue); the UF syllabus
  (R5.5) cites it as "Human kinetics, 2015". **A 5th edition now exists**: Human Kinetics, ISBN
  9781718216273, page count 864, the publisher's page prints "©2027".
  URL: https://us.humankinetics.com/products/essentials-of-strength-training-and-conditioning-5th-edition-with-hkpropel-access
- **Read level.** 5th edition: publisher's description and full table of contents (chapter titles,
  chapter authors and section heads), `raw/r5/hk_nsca5.txt`. 4th edition: search-result level only
  (the publisher's 4th edition page returned no text); chapters 1 to 15 as listed in a search snippet
  match the 5th edition's opening sequence with small title changes. **The book itself was not opened.**
- **What it is assigned for.** The publisher says it is a text "for those preparing for the Certified
  Strength and Conditioning Specialist (CSCS) examination and for upper-level undergraduates taking
  exercise science courses related to strength training and conditioning" (checked). It is the
  NSCA's own exam text, so it is the profession's statement of what a strength coach is expected to
  know, not a neutral survey.
- **Sequence (5th edition, 26 chapters).** Structure and function of body systems; biomechanics of
  resistance exercise; bioenergetics; endocrine responses; adaptations to anaerobic training;
  adaptations to aerobic training; age-related differences; sex-related differences; psychological
  foundations; basic nutrition; nutrition for performance; performance-enhancing substances; test
  selection; administration of tests; performance preparation, mobility and flexibility (ch. 15);
  exercise technique for free weight and machine training (ch. 16); alternative modes; program design
  for resistance training (ch. 18); plyometrics; speed and agility; aerobic endurance; periodization
  (ch. 22); rehabilitation and reconditioning; overreaching, overtraining and recovery (ch. 24, new in
  this edition); facility design; facility policies and legal issues.
- **Useful for a lesson.** Chapter 18's section heads give the profession's standard order for
  building a resistance programme: "Step 1: Needs Analysis", "Step 2: Exercise Selection", "Step 3:
  Training Frequency", "Step 4: Exercise Order", "Step 5: Training Load and Repetitions", "Step 6:
  Volume", "Step 7: Rest Periods" (each checked). That seven-step order is a reasonable skeleton for
  a programming lesson, cited as the NSCA's order, not as a finding.
- **Does not show.** Nothing about evidence strength for any claim. A table of contents is a map.

### R5.2 ACSM, *ACSM's Guidelines for Exercise Testing and Prescription*, 12th edition

- **Citation.** American College of Sports Medicine; senior editor Cemal Ozemek. Wolters Kluwer,
  ISBN 9781975219215, publication date March 24, 2025 (publisher's page, `raw/r5/lww_acsm12.txt`).
  The 12th edition supersedes the 11th (2021).
- **Read level.** Publisher's description (ACSM, `raw/r5/acsm_gl.txt`) and ACSM's own faculty
  "11-12 crosswalk" PDF listing changes chapter by chapter (`raw/r5/acsm_crosswalk.txt`). No full
  table of contents with chapter titles was found; chapter topics below are inferred from the
  crosswalk's entries, except the two titles the crosswalk prints. **The book was not opened.**
- **What it is assigned for.** ACSM's page calls it "the flagship title from ACSM" and says it
  "delivers scientifically based standards on exercise testing and prescription to the certification
  candidate, the professional, and the student" (checked). It is the certification text for ACSM
  exercise professionals and the usual source of the FITT (frequency, intensity, time, type) frame.
- **Structure, as the crosswalk shows it.** Ch. 1 benefits and risks (sedentary behaviour and health);
  ch. 2 retitled "Preparticipation Evaluation" (checked); ch. 3 health-related fitness testing (body
  composition, muscular fitness, balance); ch. 4 clinical exercise testing; **ch. 5 general principles
  of exercise prescription**, where the 12th edition made its largest resistance-training changes; ch. 6
  children, pregnancy, older adults, and a new section on transgender and gender diverse individuals;
  ch. 7 environmental considerations; ch. 8 cardiac conditions; ch. 9 metabolic (diabetes); ch. 10
  musculoskeletal (arthritis); ch. 11 retitled "Neurologic Diseases, Conditions and Disorders"; ch. 12
  behavioural theories.
- **Two crosswalk facts a lesson could use.** Chapter 5 gained a "New Table 5.10 Resistance Training
  Recommendations for the General Public Individual" (checked), separate from a table for the
  competitive athlete; and the flexibility FITT recommendations "have been modified and no longer
  suggest a total of 90 s of flexibility/joint" (checked). The new numbers themselves were not read.
- **Does not show.** The actual 12th edition recommendations. Any lesson quoting them needs the book.

### R5.3 Kenney, Wilmore and Costill, *Physiology of Sport and Exercise*

- **Edition.** The brief names the 8th edition; **the current edition is the 9th**, Human Kinetics,
  ISBN 9781718228429, "©2025", 680 pages (publisher's page; the 8th edition URL now redirects to it).
  URL: https://us.humankinetics.com/products/physiology-of-sport-and-exercise-9th-edition-with-hkpropel-access
- **Read level.** Publisher's description and full table of contents, `raw/r5/k_physiology-of-sport-and-exercise-8th-edition-with-hkpropel-access.txt`.
  The book was not opened.
- **Assigned for.** "Textbook for undergraduate courses in exercise physiology" (checked). The
  publisher notes the authors are "all past presidents of the American College of Sports Medicine"
  (checked).
- **Sequence (22 chapters in 7 parts).** Part I Exercising muscle (structure and function; fuel and
  bioenergetics; neural control; hormonal control). Part II Energy expenditure and fatigue (ch. 6 is
  "Fatigue, Muscle Soreness, and Muscle Cramps", checked). Part III Cardiovascular and respiratory
  function. Part IV Exercise training (ch. 10 principles of training; ch. 11 adaptations to resistance
  training, with a section "Mechanisms of Gains in Muscle Strength"; ch. 12 aerobic and anaerobic
  adaptations; ch. 13 prescription of exercise for health and fitness). Part V Environment. Part VI
  Optimising performance (training for sport; nutrition, body composition and obesity; ergogenic aids).
  Part VII Special considerations (children; ageing; sex differences; cardiovascular disease).
- **Useful for.** The standard teaching order: muscle, then energy, then heart and lungs, then
  training and adaptation, then applications. A Foundation course will want roughly the reverse
  (start from what a learner does) but can borrow the dependency: adaptation lessons need a muscle
  lesson before them.

### R5.4 McArdle, Katch and Katch, *Exercise Physiology: Nutrition, Energy, and Human Performance*, 9th edition

- **Citation.** Wolters Kluwer, ISBN 9781975217297, publication date April 5, 2023
  (`raw/r5/lww_mcardle9.txt`). Read level: publisher's description and table of contents. Not opened.
- **Sequence.** 33 chapters. **It starts from nutrition** (Section 1: carbohydrates, lipids and proteins;
  vitamins, minerals and water; optimal nutrition for physical activity), then energy transfer and
  expenditure (chs. 4 to 11), aerobic delivery (pulmonary, cardiovascular), skeletal muscle structure
  (ch. 18), neural control, endocrine; then Part Two applied: training for anaerobic and aerobic power
  (ch. 21), muscular strength (ch. 22, "Muscular Strength: Training Muscles to Become Stronger",
  checked), ergogenic aids, altitude, heat, diving, microgravity; body composition and weight control
  (chs. 28 to 30); ageing and clinical exercise physiology; molecular biology.
- **Useful for.** Showing that the discipline's heaviest reference book treats nutrition and energy
  balance as the foundation of exercise physiology, which is one reason the boundary with the
  Nutrition course (R5.27) needs drawing deliberately.

---

## Part B. How universities teach it

### R5.5 University of Florida, APK 3113C Principles of Strength and Conditioning, Fall 2025 (Ben Gordon)

- **URL.** https://hhp.ufl.edu/wp-content/uploads/sites/65/2025/08/APK-3113-Syllabus-Fall-2025-Gordon.pdf
  Read level: full syllabus (7 pages), `raw/r5/uf_apk3113.txt`.
- **Textbook.** "While there is no required text, the overwhelming majority of the course content
  comes from the following book: Haff, G. Gregory, and N. Travis Triplett, eds. Essentials of strength
  training and conditioning 4th edition" (checked).
- **Purpose.** "This course is designed to develop the knowledge and practical skills necessary to
  design and implement strength and conditioning programs" (checked), aimed at NSCA-PT and CSCS
  candidates. Prerequisites are two earlier APK courses.
- **Sequence (weekly).** Introduction; needs analysis; periodization, annual plan; modality and
  mobility integration; corrective exercise program design (two weeks); flexibility program design;
  core training; exam 1; **resistance training program (weeks 9 to 11)**; power training; exam 2;
  linear speed; speed, agility and quickness programming.
- **What it shows.** An applied S&C course begins with *needs analysis* and *periodization* before any
  exercise is taught, and spends its biggest block on resistance programme design. It is an athlete
  course: speed, power and agility take a third of the term. A general-education course for adults
  should not copy that weighting.

### R5.6 American Public University System, SPHE 314 Exercise Physiology (archived syllabus)

- **URL.** https://www.amu.apus.edu/docs/shared/course-syllabus/SPHE314.pdf Read level: full syllabus
  (9 pages), `raw/r5/apus_sphe314.txt`.
- **Caveat.** The PDF warns it "is from a previous semester archive and serves only as a preparatory
  reference" (checked), and its set text is the **5th edition** of Wilmore, Costill and Kenney (2008).
  Date of the syllabus itself not printed.
- **Sequence (8 weeks, 22 chapters).** Week 1 introduction and muscle; week 2 nervous system, energy,
  fatigue; week 3 respiratory and cardiorespiratory response to acute exercise, principles of training;
  week 4 adaptations to resistance training, adaptations to aerobic and anaerobic training, heat and
  cold; week 5 altitude, training for sport, body composition; week 6 ergogenic aids, age and exercise;
  week 7 sex differences, benefits of physical activity, cardiovascular disease prevention; week 8
  obesity, diabetes and physical activity.
- **What it shows.** The textbook order (R5.3) survives into a compressed online course nearly
  unchanged: physiology first, training second, health last.

### R5.7 The Open University, OpenLearn free courses on strength and conditioning

Read level: course descriptions, learning outcomes and full section lists, captured through a browser
(the site blocks plain downloads), `raw/r5/ol_speed_toc.txt` and `raw/r5/ol_endur_toc.txt`. The course
text itself was not read.

- **"Training for speed and power in sport and fitness"** (course code E236_2; first published
  02/11/2020, updated 02/03/2026).
  https://www.open.edu/openlearn/health-sports-psychology/training-speed-and-power-sport-and-fitness/content-section-0
  Sections: what are speed and power; speed (mechanics, physiology, methods, speed training in action);
  power (physiology and mechanics, plyometric training, weight training); quiz. The course describes
  strength and conditioning as "the physical preparation of athletes".
- **"Training for endurance in sport and fitness"** (first published 21/10/2020).
  https://www.open.edu/openlearn/health-sports-psychology/training-endurance-sport-and-fitness/content-section-0
  Sections: what is endurance; who needs it; physiological determinants (VO2 max; lactate (anaerobic)
  threshold; exercise economy; anaerobic power/capacity); training endurance; **resistance training for
  endurance**; programming (polarised or 80/20 training; threshold training); quiz.
- **What they show.** A university writing for the public teaches each quality as *definition, then the
  physiology that limits it, then the methods that train it*. That three-beat is a good lesson shape.
  Both courses are free and could be linked as further reading.

---

## Part C. Practitioner sources (how practitioners think, not evidence)

These are recorded as primary sources on the practitioners' own ideas. None of them is a study.
**Declared commercial interests are noted plainly, as one notes any author's interest.**

### R5.8 Mark Rippetoe and Starting Strength: the "novice effect" and the novice linear progression

- **Sources.** (a) Rippetoe M. "The Novice Effect." StartingStrength.com, © 2010 The Aasgaard Company.
  PDF: https://startingstrength.com/articles/novice_effect_rippetoe.pdf ; web version
  https://startingstrength.com/article/the_novice_effect . (b) "The Starting Strength Program",
  https://startingstrength.com/get-started/programs .
- **Read level.** Full text of both. The site blocks automated downloads, so both were retrieved from
  the Internet Archive (PDF snapshot 5 December 2025; web article snapshot 12 March 2026; programs page
  snapshot 21 August 2026): `raw/r5/ss_novice_effect.txt`, `ss_ne_web.txt`, `ss_programs.txt`.
- **Commercial interest.** Starting Strength is a business: the article is copyright The Aasgaard
  Company, which publishes Rippetoe's books (*Practical Programming for Strength Training*, cited in
  the article; *The Barbell Prescription*, cited on the programs page); the site's navigation includes
  "Become a Coach" (coach certification) and the article describes Rippetoe's own gym (WFAC).
- **The idea, in his words.** "The novice effect, simply described, is what happens when a previously
  untrained person begins to lift weights – he gets stronger very quickly at first, and then improves
  less and less rapidly the stronger he gets" (checked, web version). And the definition of a novice:
  the designation "has nothing to do with how strong you are, but rather how far along you are in the
  process of approaching the limit of your ability to adapt" (checked). The programming consequence:
  "In the case of training, an incremental increase to the weight lifted each workout yields the
  fastest results" (checked).
- **The programme, as the site states it.** Three sessions a week on non-consecutive days; squat 3 sets
  of 5, press or bench press 3 sets of 5, deadlift 1 set of 5, adding weight each session. "The goal of
  this program is to add weight each and every time you lift, taking advantage of The Novice Effect
  that allows you to do so" (checked). The site gives expected jumps for "Most healthy men between the
  ages of 18 and 35 or 40" and adds: "The pattern is similar for females and older lifters, but with
  smaller increases" (both checked). It also says: "There is no single prescription for everyone, so
  common sense and paying attention to how your body responds to the increased weight are your best
  guides here" (checked).
- **What the article's evidence is.** Anecdote from his gym: one 20-year-old man who went from 145 x 5
  x 3 to 315 x 5 x 3 in the squat in 11 weeks while gaining 55 pounds of bodyweight, eating "more than
  6000 calories a day" (checked). Then: "This is not an isolated case" (checked), followed by Cliff
  Swanson, 27, who "gained 45 pounds in twelve weeks" (checked), Bryan Fox, from 365 x 5 x 3 to
  515 x 5 x 3 in the squat between August 12 and December 29, "several other guys" this year, and "my
  gym has a long history of working with young men that got much bigger and stronger when they first
  started training" (checked). Cases, not a test. He also argues for session-to-session loading from
  principle, not only from cases: "the most mathematically efficient way to increase any quantity over
  time is to incrementally add to the quantity in a way that allows the increase to accumulate for as
  long as possible" (checked), and the programming sentence above carries a condition: "It must
  obviously be done in a way that permits these increases to continue, and this means that each
  increase must be recovered from and adapted to before the next workout" (checked). And he disputes
  the neural account of early gains: "the standard dogma being that the first few months of strength
  increase are due primarily to increases in neuromuscular efficiency. Perhaps this is true of most
  people on an “evidence-based” periodized program designed by the Certified Strength and Conditioning
  Specialists with the NSCA, but not here." (checked). *(Corrected 2026-09-24 at lesson 7's review,
  Reviewer F, F6 and F11, against `raw/r5/ss_ne_web.flat`: this entry had recorded one case only, and
  lesson 7 said "a single case". The shape of early gains is common ground; the reason is not.)* The
  article is also openly polemical about other methods and about academic programming.
- **What a lesson can take from it.** The practitioner's version of a real and uncontroversial shape:
  beginners gain strength fastest, and gains slow with training age (diminishing returns). The *shape*
  is common ground; what is Rippetoe's own is the claim that a session-to-session linear load increase
  is the most efficient way to use it. That second claim was not tested by anything in this section.
- **Caveats.** His expected rates are for young untrained men; he says so. The "10 pounds in three
  weeks" bodyweight expectation in the PDF is his, is gym-specific and depends on large caloric
  surpluses; a Foundation course should not repeat it as a norm.

### R5.9 Renaissance Periodization (Mike Israetel): volume landmarks MV, MEV, MAV, MRV

- **Source.** "Training Volume Landmarks for Muscle Growth." RP Strength, dated January 04, 2017 on the
  page (the text carries a TL;DR and an app plug that suggest it has been revised since; no revision
  date shown; no byline in the downloaded text). https://rpstrength.com/blogs/articles/training-volume-landmarks-muscle-growth
- **Read level.** Full text, `raw/r5/rp_vl.txt`.
- **Commercial interest.** RP sells programs and an app; the article closes by pointing to it: "The RP
  Hypertrophy App automates much of this volume progression" (checked).
- **The definitions, in RP's words.** "Maintenance Volume is the minimum training needed to preserve
  your current muscle mass." "Minimum Effective Volume is the lowest amount of training that actually
  grows muscle." "Maximum Adaptive Volume represents the range where you make your best gains."
  "Maximum Recoverable Volume is your upper limit" (all four checked; RP's sentence continues after a
  dash: training beyond it "prevents adequate recovery", checked).
- **Numbers RP gives.** Maintenance volume is "typically around 6 working sets per muscle group per
  week when training each muscle at least twice weekly" (checked). A working set assumes "Load range:
  30-85% of 1RM", "Rep range: 5-30 reps per set", "Effort level: 0-4 reps in reserve (RIR)" (checked).
- **How MEV is found.** A self-scored checklist of pump, perceived challenge and soreness (0 to 6
  points). **Soreness is one of the inputs.** That matters because the evidence recorded in R5.16 is
  that early damage and soreness are not what tracks growth. The RP scoring is a coaching heuristic;
  no validation study was cited on the page and none was found.
- **Also in RP's words.** "The volume recommendations in our muscle-specific guides are starting points,
  not gospel" (checked). That hedge belongs in any lesson that mentions the landmarks.
- **Soreness enters twice.** Besides the MEV checklist, the page's "Weekly Set Progression Algorithm"
  scores "Soreness Recovery (1-4 points)" beside performance and uses the two scores to add or hold
  sets the next week (checked, `raw/r5/rp_vl.flat`; added 2026-09-24 at lesson 7's review, F26).
- **Classification.** The idea that there is a volume too low to grow, a productive range and a ceiling
  set by recovery is widely shared. The first two parts match the dose-response data (R2.8, R5.10); the
  ceiling does not: Pelland's preprint found "no clear plateau" for size, with few studies past about
  25 fractional weekly sets, so the recovery ceiling is RP's claim, not a trial finding (corrected
  2026-09-24 at lesson 7's review, F8: this line had called the whole idea consistent with the data). The specific
  landmark numbers and the scoring system are one company's practice claims.

### R5.10 Greg Nuckols, Stronger By Science: what the volume evidence does and does not show

- **Sources, all read in full.** (a) "When does training volume reach the point of diminishing
  returns?" August 3, 2022, https://www.strongerbyscience.com/research-spotlight-volume-returns/
  (`raw/r5/sbs_volume_returns.txt`). (b) "Strength Data Don't Tell You Much About Hypertrophy",
  published 2024-12-04 (page metadata), https://www.strongerbyscience.com/strength-changes-hypertrophy/
  (`sbs_strength_hyp.txt`). (c) "Two Easy Ways to Make Your Novice Strength Training Program More
  Effective", 2014-04-21 (page metadata), https://www.strongerbyscience.com/making-your-novice-strength-training-routine-more-effective-two-quick-tips/ (`sbs_novice.txt`).
- **Commercial interest.** Stronger By Science sells coaching and programs and a paid research review;
  article (a) ends: "This article was published in partnership with MASS Research Review" (checked).
  Nuckols is also a co-author of Roberts et al. 2020 (R5.15), with a Stronger By Science affiliation
  on the paper.
- **(a) The volume evidence, and why it can be read two ways.** Nuckols summarises Schoenfeld and
  colleagues' 2017 meta-regression as showing a roughly linear dose-response up to about 10 sets per
  muscle per week, and the later Baz-Valle meta-analysis (six studies; subjects with "at least one year
  of training experience, and be between 18-35 years old"; interventions of at least 6 weeks; muscle
  thickness or cross-sectional area measured) as comparing 12-20 with 20+ weekly sets. His reading:
  "The present meta-analysis is a bit of a Rorschach test" (checked). Results: triceps growth
  significantly greater with 20+ sets (SMD = 0.50); quads a non-significant SMD = 0.20; biceps no
  meaningful difference. His own take: "~20 sets per muscle per week is approximately the average point
  of rapidly diminishing returns for trained lifters (the threshold for novice lifters is likely quite
  a bit lower)" (checked). And the caution that suits a Foundation course: "Research provides us with a
  starting point, but directed self-experimentation is necessary to optimize training volume for the
  individual" (checked).
- **Who was studied.** Trained men and women aged 18 to 35, for 6 to 8 weeks in five of six studies
  (one lasted 24 weeks). This is the course's recurring question: none of it is about an untrained
  50-year-old.
- **(b) History of the dispute, in a proponent's words.** "By 2010, there was a grand total of 8 studies
  on the impact of training volume on muscle growth, but only 3 of them included groups performing more
  than 10 sets per muscle group, per week" (checked). His central claim: "Strength data is less
  informative about hypertrophy than hypertrophy data is" (checked). He describes the volume argument as
  a feud with a high-volume camp and a low-volume camp calling extra work "junk volume". **The
  low-volume side is here only as Nuckols describes it; no low-volume proponent was read.**
- **(c) A practitioner's critique of novice linear programs.** Nuckols (2014) says the popular novice
  programs are "all basically set up the same way" and, in the next sentence, "All of them accomplish
  their intended purpose relatively well" (checked; it continues "getting you somewhat proficient with
  the core barbell lifts"). So the piece is a friendly amendment, not an attack. He suggests varying
  rep schemes (3x8, then 5x5, then 5x3), which he calls periodising ("1. Periodize"), and, at a
  plateau, deloading a little and building back "with one extra set per exercise" (checked). *(Added
  2026-09-24 at lesson 7's review, Reviewer F, F7, against `raw/r5/sbs_novice.flat`: quoting only
  "all basically set up the same way" had turned the piece into a criticism.)* "Junk volume" is from
  article (b), not (a) or (c). He quotes a 2004 meta-analysis concluding periodized training is
  more effective than non-periodized; **that meta-analysis was not opened here**, and the 2026 ACSM
  overview (R5.26) found periodization "did not consistently impact training outcomes". A lesson should
  not repeat the 2004 claim as settled.

---

## Part D. Popular claims, and the best source found that tests each

### R5.11 Spot reduction: Vispute et al. 2011

- Vispute SS, Smith JD, LeCheminant JD, Hurley KS. The effect of abdominal exercise on abdominal fat.
  *J Strength Cond Res.* 2011;25(9):2559-64. doi:10.1519/JSC.0b013e3181fb4a46. PMID 21804427.
  Read level: abstract only.
- **Design.** RCT. 24 healthy sedentary adults (14 men, 10 women), aged 18 to 40. Abdominal group did 7
  abdominal exercises, 2 sets of 10, 5 days a week for 6 weeks; control did nothing; both kept an
  isocaloric diet. Outcomes: body weight, body fat %, android fat (DXA), abdominal circumference,
  skinfolds, curl-up endurance.
- **Result.** No significant effect on any fat measure. Curl-ups at post-test 47 ± 13 (exercise) versus
  32 ± 9 (control). "Six weeks of abdominal exercise training alone was not sufficient to reduce
  abdominal subcutaneous fat and other measures of body composition" (checked).
- **Does not show.** Anything beyond 6 weeks, or with a caloric deficit. Small sample. The word "alone"
  in the conclusion is the authors' hedge and should stay.
- *Correction, 2026-09-24 (lesson 10's review, F23, against the PubMed abstract, PMID 21804427):* the
  abstract does not name DXA. Its fat measures, verbatim: "body weight, body fat percentage, android
  fat percentage, android fat, abdominal circumference, abdominal skinfold and suprailiac skinfold
  measurements". "(DXA)" above was this file's inference from "android fat"; a lesson should not name
  the scanner unless the full text is opened.

### R5.12 Spot reduction: Ramírez-Campillo et al. 2013

- Ramírez-Campillo R, Andrade DC, Campos-Jara C, et al. Regional fat changes induced by localized
  muscle endurance resistance training. *J Strength Cond Res.* 2013;27(8):2219-24.
  doi:10.1519/JSC.0b013e31827e8681. PMID 23222084. Read level: abstract only.
- **Design.** 11 young adults (7 men, 4 women, aged 23 ± 1) trained only the non-dominant leg for 12
  weeks, 3 sessions a week, 1 set of 960 to 1,200 leg-press repetitions at 10 to 30% 1RM; the other leg
  was the control. DXA before and after; energy intake by food recall (2,646 then 2,677 kcal/day, no
  significant change).
- **Result.** Whole-body fat mass fell 5.1% (13.5 to 12.8 kg). **No significant fat change in the
  trained leg.** Fat fell significantly in the upper extremities (10.2%) and trunk (6.9%). "In
  conclusion, the training program was effective in reducing fat mass, but this reduction was not
  achieved in the trained body segment" (checked).
- *Correction, 2026-09-24 (lesson 10's review, F2, against the PubMed abstract, PMID 23222084):* the
  line above recorded only the trained leg. The abstract says neither leg changed: "No significant
  changes in bone mass, lean mass, fat mass, or fat percentage were observed in both the control and
  trained leg." And: "The reduction of fat mass in the upper extremities and trunk was significantly
  greater (p < 0.05) than the fat mass change observed in the trained leg but not in the control leg."
  So fat came off the arms and trunk and off neither leg, and the trained leg did no better than the
  untrained one. A lesson must not say fat came off "everywhere except" the trained leg.
- **Good for a lesson.** The within-person design (one leg trained, one not) is easy to explain and is
  exactly the design the later meta-analysis treats as the valid test.

### R5.13 Spot reduction: Ramirez-Campillo et al. 2022 meta-analysis

- Ramirez-Campillo R, Andrade DC, Clemente FM, Afonso J, Pérez-Castilla A, Gentil P. A proposed model to
  test the hypothesis of exercise-induced localized fat reduction (spot reduction), including a
  systematic review with meta-analysis. *Human Movement.* 2022;23(3):1-14. doi:10.5114/hm.2022.110373.
  (The brief's "2021" is the online-first year on some indexes; the journal prints 2022.)
- **Read level.** Full text (PDF and HTML), `raw/r5/hm_spot_pdf.txt`, `hm_spot.txt`. Open access.
- **Design.** Systematic review and meta-analysis of controlled studies in which one limb was trained
  and the other was the control. 13 studies, 1158 participants of both sexes, aged 14 to 71; 37
  comparisons; programmes lasted 2 to 20 weeks.
- **Result.** 17 comparisons favoured the trained limb and 20 the untrained. "The effects were
  consistent, with a pooled ES = –0.03, 95% CI: –0.10 to 0.05, p = 0.508, meaning that spot reduction
  was not observed" (checked). Heterogeneity low (I² = 24.3%).
- **Conclusion (checked).** "Localized muscle training had no effect on localized adipose tissue depots,
  i.e., there was no spot reduction, regardless of the characteristics of the population and of the
  exercise program." The abstract adds a value-laden sentence: "The popular belief concerning spot
  reduction is probably derived from wishful thinking and convenient marketing strategies" (checked).
  That second sentence is the authors' opinion about motives, not a finding; a lesson should not
  repeat it as fact.
- **Limits the authors state.** Programmes of 2 to 20 weeks; longer interventions were not addressed
  (paraphrase; the sentence is split across the PDF's two columns). The authors declare no conflict.
- **Classification.** Established, within the tested durations.
- *Added 2026-09-24 (lesson 10's review, P1 and P14, checked against the PDF text):* every included
  study trained a **limb**: inclusion was "a localized exercise intervention (i.e., single-leg
  training) ... compared with a control condition (i.e., contralateral limb)". No included study
  trained the trunk. The quality table lists "Ramirez-Campillo et al., 2013 [21]" (R5.12) among the
  13, so that trial is inside the pooled result, not separate from it. The discussion's own caveats,
  verbatim across the PDF's column breaks: "fat depots from different body regions are not equally
  comparable within a given individual (i.e., comparing arms and legs)" and "abdominal fat may respond
  differently to exercise in males compared with females". So the address of "established" is limbs;
  for the abdomen the course has Vispute's one six-week trial (R5.11).

### R5.14 "Toning" with light weights versus "bulking" with heavy: Schoenfeld et al. 2017

- Schoenfeld BJ, Grgic J, Ogborn D, Krieger JW. Strength and hypertrophy adaptations between low- vs.
  high-load resistance training: a systematic review and meta-analysis. *J Strength Cond Res.*
  2017;31(12):3508-3523. doi:10.1519/JSC.0000000000002200. PMID 28834797. Abstract only.
- **Design.** 21 studies comparing low-load (≤60% 1RM) with high-load (>60% 1RM) training, all sets to
  momentary muscular failure, at least 6 weeks, healthy participants.
- **Result.** 1RM strength gains greater with high loads; isometric strength no different; "Changes in
  measures of muscle hypertrophy were similar between conditions" (checked). Conclusion: "maximal
  strength benefits are obtained from the use of heavy loads while muscle hypertrophy can be equally
  achieved across a spectrum of loading ranges" (checked).
- **What it tests.** The folk distinction that light weights and high reps "tone" while heavy weights
  "bulk". In these trials both built muscle similarly when taken to failure. "Toning" is not a
  physiological term in any source read here; the defensible translation is "building some muscle and
  losing some fat". **Caveat:** all sets were to failure; that is not how most beginners train.

### R5.15 "Lifting makes women bulky": Roberts, Nuckols and Krieger 2020

- Roberts BM, Nuckols G, Krieger JW. Sex differences in resistance training: a systematic review and
  meta-analysis. *J Strength Cond Res.* 2020;34(5):1448-1460. doi:10.1519/JSC.0000000000003521.
  PMID 32218059. Abstract only. Pre-registered (PROSPERO CRD42018094276). (This may overlap another
  researcher's section.)
- **Design.** Young to middle-aged men and women on the same resistance protocol. Hypertrophy: 12
  outcomes from 10 studies. Upper-body strength: 19 outcomes from 17 studies. Lower-body strength: 23
  outcomes from 23 studies.
- **Result.** Hypertrophy no significant difference (ES = 0.07 ± 0.06; P = 0.31); upper-body strength
  favoured females (ES = -0.60 ± 0.16; P = 0.002); lower-body no significant difference. "We found that
  males and females adapted to resistance training with similar effect sizes for hypertrophy and
  lower-body strength, but females had a larger effect for relative upper-body strength" (checked).
- **What it does and does not test.** It shows women gain muscle at a similar *relative* rate to men.
  It does not directly test "bulky", which is about absolute size and appearance. The honest lesson
  sentence is: women build muscle in proportion much as men do; how much that changes how anyone looks
  is not measured here. The authors hedge the upper-body finding ("it is possible that untrained
  females display a higher capacity", checked) and raise the possibility that it is "an artifact of the
  short duration of studies included" (checked).
  - *Correction, 2026-09-24 (lesson 9's review, P12, against the PubMed abstract):* the abstract
    reports **effect sizes**, a standardised measure, not per cent change. "Similar *relative* rate"
    and "in proportion" above are this file's gloss, not the paper's; the abstract uses "relative"
    only for upper-body strength. For a per-cent size comparison use Hubal 2005's conclusion (R2.12:
    men had "only a slight advantage in relative size gains").
- **Interest.** Nuckols (R5.10) is a co-author.

### R5.16 "No pain, no gain" and soreness as a sign of growth: Damas et al. 2016

- Damas F, Phillips SM, Libardi CA, et al. Resistance training-induced changes in integrated myofibrillar
  protein synthesis are related to hypertrophy only after attenuation of muscle damage. *J Physiol.*
  2016;594(18):5209-22. doi:10.1113/JP272472. PMID 27219125. Abstract only (open access, PMC5023708).
- **Design.** 10 young men (27 years, mean), biopsies of vastus lateralis before and 24 h and 48 h after
  a session at week 1, week 3 and week 10 of training. Measured muscle damage (Z-band streaming),
  day-to-day myofibrillar protein synthesis (deuterium oxide), and fibre cross-sectional area.
- **Result.** Damage was highest in week 1, lower at week 3, minimal at week 10. Fibre growth appeared
  only at week 10. Protein synthesis at week 1, when damage was highest, was not related to growth;
  at weeks 3 and 10 it correlated strongly (r ≈ 0.9). "When muscle damage was the highest, so was the
  integrated MyoPS (at T1), but neither were related to hypertrophy" (checked).
- **What it tests.** The belief that damage (and the soreness that goes with it) is what makes muscle
  grow. In these ten men, growth tracked protein synthesis *after* damage had faded. **Caveat:** ten
  men, one muscle; soreness itself was not the measured variable (damage was). Kenney ch. 6 (R5.3) has a
  "Muscle Soreness" section that would be the textbook source; it was not opened.

### R5.17 "No pain, no gain" and training to failure: Grgic et al. 2022

- Grgic J, Schoenfeld BJ, Orazem J, Sabol F. Effects of resistance training performed to repetition
  failure or non-failure on muscular strength and hypertrophy: a systematic review and meta-analysis.
  *J Sport Health Sci.* 2022;11(2):202-211. doi:10.1016/j.jshs.2021.01.007. PMID 33497853. Abstract only.
- **Design.** 15 studies, all in young adults.
- **Result.** No significant difference for strength (ES = -0.09, 95% CI -0.22 to 0.05) or hypertrophy
  (ES = 0.22, 95% CI -0.11 to 0.55). In resistance-trained individuals, a small significant effect
  favouring failure for hypertrophy (ES = 0.15). "Training to muscle failure does not seem to be required
  for gains in strength and muscle size. However, training in this manner does not seem to have
  detrimental effects on these adaptations, either" (checked). The authors ask for more studies "among
  older adults and highly trained individuals" (checked).
- **Classification.** Established that failure is not *required*; whether it adds a little for trained
  people is open.

### R5.18 "Muscle confusion" (constantly changing exercises): Kassiano et al. 2022; Fonseca et al. 2014

- Kassiano W, Nunes JP, Costa B, Ribeiro AS, Schoenfeld BJ, Cyrino ES. Does varying resistance exercises
  promote superior muscle hypertrophy and strength gains? A systematic review. *J Strength Cond Res.*
  2022;36(6):1753-1762. doi:10.1519/JSC.0000000000004258. PMID 35438660. Abstract only.
  8 studies, N = 241, "comprising all young men" (checked).
  Conclusion: "Some degree of systematic variation seems to enhance regional hypertrophic adaptations and
  maximize dynamic strength, whereas excessive, random variation may compromise muscular gains"
  (checked).
- Fonseca RM, Roschel H, Tricoli V, et al. Changes in exercises are more effective than in loading
  schemes to improve muscle strength. *J Strength Cond Res.* 2014;28(11):3085-92.
  doi:10.1519/JSC.0000000000000539. PMID 24832974. Abstract only. 49 physically active individuals,
  12 weeks, twice a week, four combinations of constant or varied intensity and exercise plus a control;
  outcomes squat 1RM and quadriceps cross-sectional area. Whole-quadriceps growth in all training groups
  (about 9 to 12%); groups varying the exercise grew all four quadriceps heads.
  *Added 2026-09-24 (lesson 10's review, F12, from the PubMed abstract, PMID 24832974):* the fixed
  groups missed heads: "the CICE and VICE groups did not present hypertrophy in the vastus medialis and
  rectus femoris (RF), and in the RF muscles, respectively". Strength: "The CIVE group had greater
  strength increments than the other training groups" (CIVE = constant intensity, varied exercise).
  The authors' conclusions, verbatim: "(a) CIVE is more efficient to produce strength gains for
  physically active individuals; (b) as long as the training intensity reaches an alleged threshold,
  muscle hypertrophy is similar regardless of the training intensity and exercise variation."
- **What they test.** The marketing claim that muscles must be "confused" by constant novelty. The
  evidence found supports *some planned* variation and warns against random, frequent change. The
  2026 ACSM stand, read in full (R2.2), addresses varied exercise directly: "There were insufficient
  data to determine if strength was affected by ... varied exercise selection ( 115 )". *(Corrected
  2026-09-24 at lesson 10's review, F7: this line had cited the abstract's "exercise complexity"
  (R5.26) for muscle confusion. Complexity, simple against complex movements, is not variation, and
  the stand's own sentence on variation says "insufficient data", not "did not affect".)* **No
  proponent statement of "muscle confusion" was read.**

### R5.19 The "anabolic window": Schoenfeld, Aragon and Krieger 2013

- Schoenfeld BJ, Aragon AA, Krieger JW. The effect of protein timing on muscle strength and hypertrophy:
  a meta-analysis. *J Int Soc Sports Nutr.* 2013;10(1):53. doi:10.1186/1550-2783-10-53. PMID 24299050.
  Abstract only (open access, PMC3879660). A comment was published in 2016 (not read).
- **Design.** Multi-level meta-regression of RCTs. Strength: 478 subjects, 20 studies. Hypertrophy: 525
  subjects, 23 studies.
- **Result.** Unadjusted, timing showed "a small to moderate effect on muscle hypertrophy with no
  significant effect found on muscle strength" (checked). Adjusted for covariates, no significant
  differences for either. "With respect to hypertrophy, total protein intake was the strongest predictor
  of ES magnitude" (checked). Conclusion: "These results refute the commonly held belief that the timing
  of protein intake in and around a training session is critical to muscular adaptations" (checked).
- **Classification and boundary.** Established that timing is not *critical*; total intake matters more.
  This belongs to the Nutrition course for detail; Strength and Fitness should say one sentence and
  point there.

### R5.20 The "fat-burning zone": Achten et al. 2002; Schoenfeld et al. 2014; Wewege et al. 2017

- **The real phenomenon.** Achten J, Gleeson M, Jeukendrup AE. Determination of the exercise intensity
  that elicits maximal fat oxidation. *Med Sci Sports Exerc.* 2002;34(1):92-7.
  doi:10.1097/00005768-200201000-00015. PMID 11782653. Abstract only. 18 moderately trained cyclists,
  graded tests. Fat oxidation peaked ("Fatmax") at 64 ± 4% VO2max (74 ± 3% HRmax); the zone within 10% of
  peak lay between 55 ± 3 and 72 ± 4% VO2max; fat oxidation became negligible above 89 ± 3% VO2max.
  "Fat oxidation rates are high over a large range of intensities; however, at exercise intensities
  above Fat(max), fat oxidation rates drop markedly" (checked).
  *Added 2026-09-24 (lesson 10's review, F5, from the PubMed abstract, PMID 11782653):* "graded tests"
  above was a trim. Each cyclist did a graded test to exhaustion, and "In addition, four to six
  continuous prolonged exercise tests (CE) at constant work rates, corresponding to the work rates of
  the GE test, were performed on separate days." Seven other subjects did three graded tests. Nobody
  trained. The study's stated aim: "to develop a test protocol to determine the exercise intensity at
  which fat oxidation rate is maximal (Fat(max))." **Nothing read traces how gym machines or watches
  set their "fat burn" band, or links that band to Fatmax**; a lesson that sets the two side by side
  is making its own comparison and says so (F4, P3).
- **What the claim gets wrong.** The "zone" is about the *fuel mix during the session*, measured in
  trained cyclists. The popular claim is that training in it loses more body fat over weeks. Two tests
  of the longer-run outcome:
  - Wewege M, van den Berg R, Ward RE, Keech A. HIIT vs moderate-intensity continuous training on body
    composition in overweight and obese adults: systematic review and meta-analysis. *Obes Rev.*
    2017;18(6):635-646. doi:10.1111/obr.12532. PMID 28401638. Abstract only. 13 trials, ages 18 to 45,
    about 10 weeks, 3 sessions a week. "There were no significant differences between HIIT and MICT for
    any body composition measure, but HIIT required ~40% less training time commitment" (checked). The
    abstract also reports running reduced whole-body fat mass but "cycling training did not induce fat
    loss" (checked), which is a surprising detail to handle carefully.
  - Schoenfeld BJ, Aragon AA, Wilborn CD, Krieger JW, Sonmez GT. Body composition changes associated
    with fasted versus non-fasted aerobic exercise. *J Int Soc Sports Nutr.* 2014;11(1):54.
    doi:10.1186/s12970-014-0054-7. PMID 25429252. Abstract only. 20 young women on a hypocaloric diet,
    4 weeks, 1 hour of steady cardio 3 days a week, fasted or fed. Both lost weight and fat; "no
    significant between-group differences were noted in any outcome measure" (checked). This tests the
    cousin claim that burning more fat *during* exercise (fasted) means losing more body fat.
- **Classification.** Fatmax is established physiology. That it predicts better fat loss is not
  supported by the trials found; intensity comparisons show similar body-composition change.

### R5.21 "You must stretch before exercise": Behm et al. 2016; Simic et al. 2013

- Behm DG, Blazevich AJ, Kay AD, McHugh M. Acute effects of muscle stretching on physical performance,
  range of motion, and injury incidence in healthy active individuals: a systematic review. *Appl
  Physiol Nutr Metab.* 2016;41(1):1-11. doi:10.1139/apnm-2015-0235. PMID 26642915. Abstract only.
  Static stretching (SS) -3.7%, dynamic (DS) +1.3%, PNF -4.4% performance change immediately after;
  deficits larger with ≥60 s per muscle group (-4.6%) than <60 s (-1.1%). "SS and PNF stretching had no
  clear effect on all-cause or overuse injuries; no data are available for DS" (checked). Their
  recommendation keeps a place for stretching: "stretching within a warm-up that includes additional
  poststretching dynamic activity is recommended for reducing muscle injuries and increasing joint ROM
  with inconsequential effects on subsequent athletic performance" (checked). Note the tension inside
  the abstract (no clear injury effect, yet recommended for reducing muscle injuries); a lesson should
  present both sentences.
- Simic L, Sarabon N, Markovic G. Does pre-exercise static stretching inhibit maximal muscular
  performance? A meta-analytical review. *Scand J Med Sci Sports.* 2013;23(2):131-48.
  doi:10.1111/j.1600-0838.2012.01444.x. PMID 22316148. Abstract only. 104 studies. Strength -5.4%, power
  -1.9% (CI crossing zero), explosive performance -2.0%; smallest effects with stretches of ≤45 s. "We
  conclude that the usage of SS as the sole activity during warm-up routine should generally be avoided"
  (checked).
- **What this tests.** The rule "always stretch before exercise". The evidence says: static stretching
  on its own before strength or power work slightly lowers performance, short stretches matter little,
  and a warm-up built on movement is preferred. It does not say stretching is harmful in general.
- NSCA 5th ed. ch. 15 and the ACSM 12th ed. flexibility changes (R5.1, R5.2) are where the professional
  bodies set this out; neither was opened.

### R5.22 What prevents injury: Lauersen et al. 2014

- Lauersen JB, Bertelsen DM, Andersen LB. The effectiveness of exercise interventions to prevent sports
  injuries: a systematic review and meta-analysis of randomised controlled trials. *Br J Sports Med.*
  2014;48(11):871-7. doi:10.1136/bjsports-2013-092538. PMID 24100287. Abstract only.
- 25 trials, 26,610 participants, 3,464 injuries. Stretching RR 0.963 (0.846-1.095); proprioception RR
  0.550; strength training RR 0.315 (0.207-0.480). "Strength training reduced sports injuries to less
  than 1/3 and overuse injuries could be almost halved" (checked).
- **Caveat.** Sports participants, not general adults. Heterogeneous overall estimate. A 2018 item by
  the same first author exists in PubMed (PMID 30131332), not read; check whether it is a correction or
  reply before citing the figure.

### R5.23 "Cardio kills gains": Wilson et al. 2012; Schumann et al. 2022

- Wilson JM, Marin PJ, Rhea MR, et al. Concurrent training: a meta-analysis examining interference of
  aerobic and resistance exercises. *J Strength Cond Res.* 2012;26(8):2293-307.
  doi:10.1519/JSC.0b013e31823a3e2d. PMID 22002517. Abstract only. 21 studies. Mean ES for hypertrophy:
  strength training 1.23, concurrent 0.85, endurance 0.27. Running, not cycling, alongside resistance
  training produced decrements; "interference effects of endurance training are a factor of the
  modality, frequency, and duration of the endurance training selected" (checked).
- Schumann M, Feuerbacher JF, Sünkeler M, et al. Compatibility of concurrent aerobic and strength
  training for skeletal muscle size and function: an updated systematic review and meta-analysis.
  *Sports Med.* 2022;52(3):601-612. doi:10.1007/s40279-021-01587-7. PMID 34757594. Abstract only (open
  access). 43 studies, healthy adults, at least 4 weeks. SMD maximal strength -0.06 (CI -0.20 to 0.09);
  explosive strength -0.28 (CI -0.48 to -0.08); hypertrophy -0.01. "Concurrent aerobic and strength
  training does not compromise muscle hypertrophy and maximal strength development. However, explosive
  strength gains may be attenuated, especially when aerobic and strength training are performed in the
  same session" (checked). Authors declare no conflicts.
- **Classification.** Contested in 2012, largely resolved for general adults by 2022: for size and
  maximal strength, adding cardio does not measurably cost gains; for explosive strength, a small cost,
  mostly same-session. The two meta-analyses disagree on running versus cycling; Schumann found no
  modality effect.

### R5.24 Machines versus free weights: Haugen et al. 2023

- Haugen ME, Vårvik FT, Larsen S, et al. Effect of free-weight vs. machine-based strength training on
  maximal strength, hypertrophy and jump performance: a systematic review and meta-analysis. *BMC Sports
  Sci Med Rehabil.* 2023;15(1):103. doi:10.1186/s13102-023-00713-4. PMID 37582807. Abstract only (open
  access, PMC10426227).
- 13 studies, 1016 participants (789 men, 219 women), adults 18 to 60, at least 6 weeks. Free-weight
  training improved free-weight tests more (SMD -0.210); machine training tended to improve machine tests
  more (SMD 0.291, p = 0.064). Direct comparisons: no differences in dynamic strength, isometric strength,
  jump, or hypertrophy. "Current body of evidence indicates that strength changes are specific to the
  training modality, and the choice between free-weights and machines are down to individual preferences
  and goals" (checked).
- **Tests.** Both halves of the folk argument ("free weights are better", "machines are for beginners").
  Neither is supported for size or general strength; what you train is what you get better at. **Safety**
  (machines being safer) was not an outcome here; no source in this section tests it.

### R5.25 "Sweat means calories burned": ACSM 2007 fluid replacement position stand

- American College of Sports Medicine; Sawka MN, Burke LM, Eichner ER, et al. Exercise and fluid
  replacement. *Med Sci Sports Exerc.* 2007;39(2):377-90. doi:10.1249/mss.0b013e31802ca597. PMID 17277604.
  Abstract only.
- "Because there is considerable variability in sweating rates and sweat electrolyte content between
  individuals, customized fluid replacement programs are recommended. Individual sweat rates can be
  estimated by measuring body weight before and after exercise" (checked).
- **What it supports, weakly.** The weight lost during a sweaty session is treated by the ACSM as water,
  to be replaced; sweat rate varies between people. That undercuts "more sweat, more fat burned" but does
  not test it directly. The ACSM 12th edition added "New Box 7.1 Calculating Sweat Rate" (crosswalk,
  checked). **A lesson should present this as reasoning from the position stand, not as a tested
  refutation.** No study that directly tests the sweat-calorie belief was found.

### R5.26 Cross-reference: ACSM 2026 position stand on resistance training (overview of reviews)

- Currier BS, D'Souza AC, Singh MAF, et al.; Phillips SM. American College of Sports Medicine Position
  Stand. Resistance training prescription for muscle function, hypertrophy, and physical performance in
  healthy adults: an overview of reviews. *Med Sci Sports Exerc.* 2026;58(4):851-872.
  doi:10.1249/MSS.0000000000003897. PMID 41843416. Abstract only (open access, PMC12965823). It updates
  the 2009 ACSM progression-models stand. **This is probably another researcher's core source**; it is
  recorded here because it bears on four of the claims above.
- 137 systematic reviews, more than 30,000 participants, randomised trials of at least 6 weeks in healthy
  adults. "Training to momentary muscle fatigue, equipment type, exercise complexity, set structure, time
  under tension, blood flow restriction, and periodization did not consistently impact training outcomes"
  (checked). Hypertrophy "was enhanced by higher volumes (≥10 sets/wk) and eccentric overload" (checked).
- Bears on: failure (R5.17), machines versus free weights (R5.24), muscle confusion (R5.18), the volume
  dispute (R5.10).

## Part E. Exercise and weight loss (boundary with the Nutrition course)

### R5.27 ACSM 2009 position stand: Donnelly et al.

- Donnelly JE, Blair SN, Jakicic JM, Manore MM, Rankin JW, Smith BK; American College of Sports Medicine.
  Appropriate physical activity intervention strategies for weight loss and prevention of weight regain
  for adults. *Med Sci Sports Exerc.* 2009;41(2):459-71. doi:10.1249/MSS.0b013e3181949333. PMID 19127177.
  Erratum 2009;41(7):1532 (not read). Read level: abstract only.
- **Recommendations, verbatim (checked).** "Evidence supports moderate-intensity PA between 150 and 250
  min wk(-1) to be effective to prevent weight gain. Moderate-intensity PA between 150 and 250 min wk(-1)
  will provide only modest weight loss. Greater amounts of PA (>250 min wk(-1)) have been associated with
  clinically significant weight loss." And on resistance training: "Resistance training does not enhance
  weight loss but may increase fat-free mass and increase loss of fat mass and is associated with
  reductions in health risk" (checked). The stand is candid about gaps: "no evidence from well-designed
  randomized controlled trials exists to judge the effectiveness of PA for prevention of weight regain
  after weight loss" (checked).
- **Use.** One lesson paragraph that sets expectations: exercise at guideline amounts alone produces
  modest weight loss; its bigger case is health, fitness and body composition. Detail belongs to the
  Nutrition course. The stand is from 2009; check whether ACSM has replaced it before citing it as
  current.

## Part F. Free resources a lesson can link

### R5.28 NHS (England) Live Well exercise pages

All fetched in full on 2026-09-24 (`raw/r5/nhs_*.txt`). Note: the old URL
`/live-well/exercise/strength-and-flexibility-exercises/` now redirects to the exercise hub, and
`/live-well/exercise/get-active/` returns 404.

- **"How to improve your strength and flexibility"**, https://www.nhs.uk/live-well/exercise/how-to-improve-strength-flexibility/
  (page last reviewed 21 July 2026). A plain-language definition: "A strength exercise is any activity
  that makes your muscles work harder than usual" (checked), and a usable test of effort: "For an
  activity to be muscle strengthening, it needs to work your muscles to the point where you may need a
  short rest before continuing" (checked). Lists everyday examples (heavy gardening, stairs, dance, yoga).
- **"Strength exercises"**, https://www.nhs.uk/live-well/exercise/strength-exercises/ (sit-to-stand,
  mini-squats, calf raises and more, chair-supported, "at least twice a week").
- **"Strength and Flex exercise plan: How-to videos"**, https://www.nhs.uk/live-well/exercise/strength-and-flex-exercise-plan-how-to-videos/
  (last reviewed 19 August 2026): 15 short videos (warm-up, posture, standing press-up, squat, sit to
  stand, wide squat, pull-up, bench dip, stretches), hosted on the NHS site (Brightcove), each with a
  standard safety note.
- **"How to warm up before exercising"**, https://www.nhs.uk/live-well/exercise/how-to-warm-up-before-exercising/
  (last reviewed 24 June 2026). A 6-minute routine (marching, heel digs, knee lifts, shoulder rolls, knee
  bends): "This warm-up routine should take at least 6 minutes" (checked). No static stretching in it.
- **Couch to 5K**, https://www.nhs.uk/better-health/get-active/get-running-with-couch-to-5k/ : nine weeks,
  three runs a week; week 1 alternates 1-minute runs with 90-second walks; "by the end you'll be running
  for 30 minutes without stopping" (checked).
- **Adult guidelines page**, https://www.nhs.uk/live-well/exercise/physical-activity-guidelines-for-adults-aged-19-to-64/
  (fetched; guideline content is likely another researcher's section).

### R5.29 CDC, "Adult Activity: An Overview"

https://www.cdc.gov/physical-activity-basics/guidelines/adults.html , dated Dec. 20, 2023. Captured via
browser (`raw/r5/cdc_adults_browser.txt`); plain download was refused. States the US guideline in lay
terms with three worked examples, and defines the strength half: "Muscle-strengthening activities on 2
or more days a week that work all major muscle groups (legs, hips, back, abdomen, chest, shoulders, and
arms)" (checked).

### R5.30 ExRx.net

https://exrx.net/ ; About page https://exrx.net/People/Contact (captured via browser,
`raw/r5/exrx_about_browser.txt`). What it is, in its own words: "ExRx.net began in 1999 as a resource for
exercise professionals, coaches, and fitness enthusiasts" (checked), with an exercise library of "over
2000 exercises" and calculators. Since 2022 it has had paid premium content and an app; most content
remains free, and it "relies on advertisement income" (checked). It lists recommendations in older ACSM
resource manuals (5th to 7th editions). **Use as an exercise-demonstration and anatomy reference, not as
evidence.** It is a commercial reference site with a long professional following.

### R5.31 NSCA free articles, and one free NSCA teaching article

- NSCA free articles hub: https://www.nsca.com/education/education-overview/free-articles/ (link captured
  from https://www.nsca.com/education/articles/ via browser). The NSCA describes itself as "a recognized
  501(c)(3) educational nonprofit" (checked, `raw/r5/nsca_articles_browser.txt`).
- **Lincoln M, Ill K, Ibrahim M. "Progressive Strategies for Teaching Fundamental Resistance Training
  Movement Patterns." *NSCA Personal Training Quarterly* 10.2, 2023.** Free PDF:
  https://www.nsca.com/contentassets/3d09f06f0b4c4f6fbd8cc382ed1f3d4a/ptq-10.2.1-progressive-strategies-for-teaching-fundamental-resistance-training-movement-patterns.pdf
  Read level: full text downloaded (`raw/r5/nsca_ptq_10_2.txt`); first two pages read closely.
  *(Updated 2026-09-24 at Stage 3: lesson 6's drafter read the whole downloaded text, including the
  figure captions and cueing pages, so the read level is now the full text. Only the passages quoted
  below were string-checked; anything else a lesson takes from the article is paraphrase.)* This is
  **the best single source found for a technique lesson**. It names six patterns: squat
  ("knee-dominant"), hip hinge ("hip-dominant"), horizontal push, horizontal pull, vertical push, vertical
  pull, with sample progressions for each. It is candid that there is no agreed method:
  "Although assessments and criteria for fundamental resistance training movements have been proposed,
  no consensus exists for selection or sequencing of exercises to best teach them" (checked against a
  reading-order extraction of the PDF, `nsca_ptq_10_2_raw.txt`, since the layout extraction interleaves
  the two columns). On beginners: "For novice clients,
  low-complexity exercises are favored for teaching fundamental movements of resistance training"
  (checked). Its injury claim is hedged: proficiency "may reduce injury risk, as poor technique is
  considered a risk factor for training injuries" (checked). Keep the "may" and "considered".
  Its Takeaway section draws the line between a technique fault and something else (added
  2026-09-24 at lesson 6's review, F1, checked against `nsca_ptq_10_2_raw.txt`): "The presence of
  pain, range of motion deficits, or overt weakness may negatively affect movement performance" and
  "If underlying pathology is suggested, referral to a physical therapist or physician is
  indicated." Any lesson that teaches a movement pattern should carry it with a stop-for-pain line.

### R5.32 ACSM consumer brochure, "Resistance Training for Health and Fitness"

- PDF (2 pages), hosted at https://www.prescriptiontogetactive.com/static/pdfs/resistance-training-ACSM.pdf ;
  the ACSM chapters-site copy (https://chapters.acsm.org/docs/default-source/files-for-resource-library/resistance-training-for-health.pdf)
  returned an HTML page, not the PDF. Read level: full text, `raw/r5/acsm_rt_fitness.txt`.
- **Dated.** "Copyright © 2013 American College of Sports Medicine" (checked), by Michael R. Esco, from
  ACSM's Consumer Information Committee; it cites ACSM recommendations "updated in 2011". It predates the
  2026 position stand (R5.26). It gives the classic beginner prescription (at least two non-consecutive
  days, one set of 8 to 12 repetitions for healthy adults or 10 to 15 for older and frail individuals, 8 to
  10 exercises for the major muscle groups) and a 2 to 10% load increase rule. These are paraphrases: the
  two-column layout breaks the sentences in the text extraction. **Link only with its date stated**, or
  prefer the NHS and CDC pages.

### R5.33 Stronger By Science free guides

https://www.strongerbyscience.com/guides/ (`raw/r5/sbs_guides.txt`). Free long-form guides including
"The Complete Strength Training Guide", "How to Squat", "How to Bench", "How to Deadlift"; free resources
include a "Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis Master List" and a program bundle. Also a free PDF, "More
Training, More Gaining" (the volume guide), at
https://www.strongerbyscience.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/06/More-Training-More-Gaining-PDF.pdf (resolves;
not read). Commercial interest as in R5.10. Suitable as "further reading" for motivated learners; the
guides are practitioner writing with citations, not peer-reviewed.

watch page (length, publish date). Output: `raw/r5/yt_check1.txt`, `yt_check2.txt`.

| V2 | Hip hinge / deadlift | https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CdZgCZtz1QQ | Bridge: Deadlift Variations \| NSCA.com | NSCA | 3:46 | NSCA channel; the NSCA site says it demonstrates techniques from the *Strength and Conditioning Journal* article "Exploring the Deadlift" (search-result level). |

| V4 | Push-up (beginner version) | https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NL8IRKjLPf0 | NHS Strength and flexibility: standing press-up | NHS | 0:42 | The NHS's own channel; the same series as the NHS Strength and Flex page (R5.28). Uploaded 2012. |
| V5 | Push-up (knee version) | https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WcHtt6zT3Go | Wellness Wednesday: Give yourself 10 knee push-ups | Mayo Clinic | 0:46 | Mayo Clinic's own channel (2017). |
| V6 | Warm-up | https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WOmVkf4yTLc | Mayo Clinic Mindful Study Breaks – Warm-Up & Squats | Mayo Clinic | 3:07 | Mayo Clinic's own channel (2019); a gentle warm-up plus bodyweight squat. |
| V7 | Idea: why muscles grow | https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2tM1LFFxeKg | What makes muscles grow? - Jeffrey Siegel | TED-Ed | 4:20 | TED-Ed animated lesson (2015), very widely used; covers fibres, damage and repair, sleep and nutrition. **Check it against R5.16 before linking**: a lesson should not endorse a damage-centred account uncritically. |

**Also checked, with notes.**
- NHS "Basic warm-up - 5 minutes | NHS", https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=oLu9kwDzBdg , 7:32 on the watch
  page (451 to 452 seconds). The page is playable (`"status":"OK"`) but **embedding is disabled**
  (`"playableInEmbed":false`), which is why oEmbed returned 401. Link, do not embed.
- Starting Strength, "Learning to Deadlift | The Starting Strength Method",
  https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=p2OPUi4xGrM , 4:22, 2020. Practitioner teaching the deadlift in his
  own method (commercial interest as R5.8). Clear, very widely viewed; usable as "one coach's method"
  in a lesson that names it as such.
- NSCA, "The Science of Squatting, with Brad Schoenfeld | NSCA.com", https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CjkzqsO3qhY ,
  48:59. A conference lecture; too long for a Foundation link but a good source for a teacher.
- Rejected: several popular fitness channels (e.g., ScottHermanFitness, Live Lean TV, ATHLEAN-X) because a
  credible institutional alternative existed; not a judgement on their content.

## Part H. Wikimedia Commons images

Metadata from the Commons API `imageinfo`/`extmetadata`, which reproduces the file page's information
template (`raw/r5/commons_meta.json`). The direct URLs are given without the API's tracking query.

1. **"Structure of skeletal muscle" (Commons title), which is in fact a single sarcomere.**
   File page: https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:202107_Structure_of_skeletal_muscle.svg
   Direct: https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/5/54/202107_Structure_of_skeletal_muscle.svg
   Author: DataBase Center for Life Science (DBCLS). Source: https://doi.org/10.7875/togopic.2021.083
   Licence: CC BY 4.0. Dated 2021-07-01.
   **Corrected 2026-09-24 (lesson 4's review, P1), after downloading the file and Commons' 960 px
   render:** the drawing shows one sarcomere (two Z discs, an M line, myosin and actin filaments, and
   two call-outs of a myosin molecule and an actin strand). Its only labels are "Z", "M", "Z". It has
   no whole muscle, fascicle, fibre or myofibril, which this entry had claimed from the title alone.
   The file is also 28.7 MB (28,728,277 bytes), too heavy to hotlink. **Do not use it for muscle
   structure**; for that, use item 3. It could illustrate a sarcomere, with a thumbnail URL.
2. **Skeletal muscle fibre (Blausen).**
   File page: https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Blausen_0801_SkeletalMuscle.png
   Direct: https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/6/6f/Blausen_0801_SkeletalMuscle.png
   Author: BruceBlaus; the page asks that it be cited as Blausen.com staff (2014). "Medical gallery of
   Blausen Medical 2014". WikiJournal of Medicine 1 (2). DOI:10.15347/wjm/2014.010. Licence: CC BY 3.0.
   2000 x 1823 px, labelled.
3. **Muscle structure (US government, public domain).**
   File page: https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Illu_muscle_structure.jpg
   Direct: https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/8/89/Illu_muscle_structure.jpg
   Source: US National Cancer Institute SEER Training Modules (training.seer.cancer.gov). Licence: Public
   domain. Small (520 x 286). Opened 2026-09-24 (46 KB): titled "Structure of a Skeletal Muscle", its
   labels are Bone, Tendon, Epimysium, Perimysium, Blood vessel, Muscle fiber, Fascicle, Endomysium.
   Commons description: "Numerous skeletal muscle fibers bundle together to form muscle fascicles."
   Used in lesson 4.
4. **Sarcomere diagram.**
   File page: https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Sarcomere_diagram.svg
   Direct: https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/4/4c/Sarcomere_diagram.svg
   Author: SlothMcCarty. Licence: CC BY-SA 3.0 (share-alike: a derivative must carry the same licence).
5. **Sarcomere shortened (actin and myosin).**
   File page: https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Sarcomere_shortened.png
   Direct: https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/4/47/Sarcomere_shortened.png
   Author: MatthewLFish. Licence: CC BY-SA 4.0. A companion "Sarcomere lengthened.png" exists (metadata
   not fetched); the pair would show contraction.

No figure from an open-access paper was selected. The spot-reduction meta-analysis (R5.13) is open access
but its licence was not stated in the downloaded text, so its figures should not be reused without
checking.
