## What this section found that should shape the course

- **The textbooks teach physiology first and application last; the course should invert that and borrow
  the dependencies.** Kenney (R5.3) and the syllabus built on it (R5.6) go muscle, energy, heart and
  lungs, training, health. The OpenLearn courses (R5.7) and the NSCA chapter 18 sequence (R5.1) show the
  applied order: define the quality, explain what limits it, then how to train it.
- **The "who, how long, measured how" spine is visible in almost every claim here.** Volume data are from
  trained 18 to 35 year olds over 6 to 8 weeks (R5.10); the failure meta-analysis is all young adults
  (R5.17); exercise variation is all young men (R5.18); the Fatmax curve is trained cyclists (R5.20); the
  sex-differences finding may be "an artifact of the short duration of studies" (R5.15). A lesson can
  make this the learner's habit.
- **Several popular claims have clean tests, and the tests are good teaching.** Spot reduction has a
  one-leg-trained, other-leg-control design (R5.12, R5.13) that anyone can understand.
- **The professional bodies have moved.** ACSM's 12th edition dropped the 90-second flexibility total
  (R5.2); its 2026 position stand (R5.26) found equipment type, periodization and training to failure did
  not consistently matter. Older consumer material (the 2013 ACSM brochure, R5.32) should be dated if
  linked.
- **Practitioners agree on more than their arguments suggest.** Rippetoe's novice effect, RP's volume
  landmarks and Nuckols's diminishing returns all describe the same shape: beginners respond to little,
  more work helps up to a point, recovery sets the ceiling. They disagree on how to exploit it. The
  course can teach the shape and present the methods as methods.
- **Technique teaching has a free, hedged professional source** (NSCA PTQ, R5.31) built on six movement
  patterns, which is a natural spine for the "doing it" lessons, with NSCA, NHS and Mayo Clinic videos
  for each pattern (Part G).
- **Weight loss is a boundary to draw on purpose.** The ACSM 2009 stand (R5.27) says guideline amounts of
  activity give "only modest weight loss" and that resistance training "does not enhance weight loss".
  One paragraph here, the rest in Nutrition.
- **Soreness is the trap in both folk wisdom and one practitioner system.** "No pain, no gain" and RP's
  MEV checklist both use soreness as a signal; the Damas biopsy study (R5.16) suggests early damage is
  not what tracks growth.

## Misconceptions this section can correct

| Misconception | Source that tests it | Strength |
|---|---|---|
| Ab exercises burn belly fat (spot reduction) | R5.11 Vispute 2011; R5.12 Ramírez-Campillo 2013; R5.13 meta-analysis 2022 | Strong within 2 to 20 weeks |
| Light weights "tone", heavy weights "bulk" | R5.14 Schoenfeld 2017 (hypertrophy similar across loads, to failure) | Moderate; all sets to failure |
| Lifting makes women bulky | R5.15 Roberts 2020 (similar effect sizes for hypertrophy; per cents are Hubal, R2.12) | Tests muscle growth, not appearance |
| No pain, no gain; soreness means growth | R5.16 Damas 2016; R5.17 Grgic 2022 (failure not required) | Moderate (Damas n = 10) |
| Muscles must be "confused" with constant change | R5.18 Kassiano 2022; Fonseca 2014; R5.26 | Moderate; all young men |
| You must eat protein within the "anabolic window" | R5.19 Schoenfeld 2013 | Moderate; total intake matters more |
| Train in the "fat-burning zone" to lose more fat | R5.20 Achten 2002 (what Fatmax is); Wewege 2017; Schoenfeld 2014 | Moderate |
| Always static-stretch before exercise | R5.21 Behm 2016; Simic 2013; R5.22 Lauersen 2014 | Strong for performance; injury effect unclear |
| Cardio kills gains | R5.23 Schumann 2022 (Wilson 2012 for the older view) | Strong for size and maximal strength |
| Free weights are better / machines are better | R5.24 Haugen 2023; R5.26 | Moderate; safety not tested |
| Sweat means calories burned | R5.25 ACSM 2007 (weight lost as sweat is water to replace) | Weak, indirect; no direct test found |
| Muscle turns into fat | **No source found that tests it.** Not recorded as a correctable claim. | None |

## Contested questions in this section

1. **How much volume is enough?** High-volume case: Nuckols's reading that 20+ weekly sets "will likely
   lead to a bit more muscle growth" for trained lifters, and the 2026 ACSM finding that ≥10 sets/wk
   enhanced hypertrophy (R5.10, R5.26). Low-volume case: known here **only through Nuckols's description**
   of a "junk volume" camp; no proponent was read. Classification: contested at the top end for trained
   lifters; not contested that beginners need little.
2. **Is a session-to-session linear progression the best way for a novice to start?** For: Rippetoe, in
   his words, that a linear increase "yields the greatest increase in strength in the shortest amount of
   time" (R5.8, PDF; his claim, anecdotal evidence). Against or qualifying: Nuckols (2014) on varying rep
   schemes, and the ACSM 2026 finding that periodization did not consistently matter. Classification:
   practitioner dispute; no trial read tests it.
3. **Stretching and injury.** Behm et al.'s own abstract finds "no clear effect" on injuries yet
   recommends stretching within a movement-based warm-up for "reducing muscle injuries" (R5.21);
   Lauersen finds no benefit (R5.22). Classification: contested; the performance findings are not.
4. **Does training to failure add anything?** Grgic (R5.17): not required, a small hypertrophy edge in
   trained people. ACSM 2026: not consistent. Classification: open at the margins.
5. **Running versus cycling interference.** Wilson 2012 found running interfered, cycling not; Schumann
   2022 found no modality effect (R5.23). Classification: contested; the headline (little interference for
   size and strength) is not.

## Unread or unreachable

- The four textbooks themselves (NSCA *Essentials* 4th and 5th; ACSM *Guidelines* 12th; Kenney 9th;
  McArdle 9th). Only publishers' descriptions and tables of contents were read. The NSCA 4th edition
  table of contents was seen at search-result level only.
- The ACSM 11th edition (superseded).
- Rippetoe, *Starting Strength: Basic Barbell Training* and *Practical Programming for Strength Training*
  (PPST): named in the article, not opened.
- Rhea and Alderman 2004 (the periodization meta-analysis quoted by Nuckols): not opened.
- Baz-Valle et al. (volume meta-analysis) and Schoenfeld et al. 2017 volume meta-regression: known only
  through Nuckols (R5.10). Pelland et al. (2024 preprint meta-regressions): known only through Nuckols.
- Full texts of all PubMed-listed studies in Part D (abstracts only), including the 2016 comment on the
  protein-timing meta-analysis and the 2009 erratum to Donnelly.
- Lauersen et al. PMID 30131332 (2018): not read; may be a correction or reply.
- Schoenfeld and Contreras, "Is postexercise muscle soreness a valid indicator of muscular adaptations?"
  (Strength Cond J, 2013): not found in PubMed; not read.
- UF APK 6176 graduate syllabus: the URL returned an HTML page, not the PDF.
- The ACSM chapters-site brochure "Resistance Training for Health": URL returned HTML, not the PDF.
- NSCA free articles list itself (only the hub page was captured); NSCA position statements page.
- Stronger By Science "More Training, More Gaining" PDF: resolves, not read.
- Sarcomere lengthened.png metadata: not fetched.
- Starting Strength live site and OpenLearn, CDC, ExRx and NSCA pages refuse plain downloads (Cloudflare or
  403); archived or browser-captured copies were used as stated at each entry.

# Synthesis for Stage 2

Each part above ends with its own "what this section found", misconceptions, contested questions and
unread list, which are the detailed record. This section pulls them into the template's headings so
the outline has one place to build from.

## What the field considers essential

In the order the ideas build, taken from the textbooks' and syllabi's own sequences (R5.1 to R5.7:
physiology first, then how to assess, then how to train each quality) and from what every guideline
body leads with (R1.1 to R1.4).

1. **The dose-response curve for activity**: the steepest gain is from none to some, there is no
   lower threshold, bouts of any length count, and every guideline includes muscle strengthening on
   two or more days (R1.1, R1.3, R1.4). What the numbers rest on (R1.1 methods, R1.9 to R1.11).
2. **Screening and warning signs**: who should ask a doctor first and why the modern answer is fewer
   people than it used to be (R4.1, R4.2, R4.3).
3. **Intensity, absolute and relative**: METs (R3.2), heart rate and its prediction error (R3.3a to
   c), perceived exertion and the talk test (R3.3d, e), repetitions in reserve (R2.10).
4. **Aerobic adaptation**: what VO2max is and what limits it (R3.1), how it responds to continuous and
   interval training (R3.4), individual response and dose (R3.5, R3.6).
5. **Strength adaptation**: neural then muscular (R2.4), specificity (R2.5), and what load, volume,
   frequency and effort each do (R2.6 to R2.10), with the evidence base's limits (R2.16).
6. **Progression and programming**: progressive overload, the novice's fast early gains and why they
   slow (R2.4, R5.8), periodisation and what it adds (R2.11), the 2026 stand's simplification (R2.2),
   detraining and regain (R2.17).
7. **Technique**: the movement patterns (R5.31, the NSCA teaching article), and what the technique
   rules each rest on (R4.8 to R4.11).
8. **Warm-up, flexibility, soreness, recovery**: R4.12 to R4.19, R3.10.
9. **Across a life**: women (R2.14, R5.15), older adults and falls (R2.14, R4.21), pregnancy (R4.20),
   children (R4.22), common conditions as safety pointers (R4.23 to R4.26).
10. **Reading a claim about training**: the popular claims and their tests (R5.11 to R5.25), zone 2
    (R3.7), wearables (R3.9), practitioners' frameworks (R5.8 to R5.10).

## Common misconceptions

Consolidated from the five parts; each has its source there.

| Misconception | What the sources read say | Where |
|---|---|---|
| You need 10,000 steps a day | A 1965 pedometer's name, "likely"; curves level at about 6,000 to 8,000 (60+) and 8,000 to 10,000 (under 60), observational | R1.7 |
| Exercise under 10 minutes doesn't count | Removed from WHO 2020 and PAG 2018 | R1.1, R1.4 |
| If you can't do 150 minutes there's no point | No lower threshold; none to some is the biggest gain | R1.1, R1.3, R1.4 |
| Trials prove exercise makes you live longer | The one general-population trial was null on mortality; evidence is mostly observational | R1.9 |
| It's all confounding | Not what Gen 100's authors, Lee 2021 or the MR study conclude | R1.9, R1.11 |
| Everyone must see a doctor before starting | Not if asymptomatic and starting light to moderate | R4.1, R4.3 |
| Lifting is dangerous | 2 to 4 injuries per 1,000 hours in competitors; strength work prevents sports injuries | R4.5, R4.7 |
| You must lift heavy to build muscle | Growth similar across loads when sets are hard; heavy matters for 1RM | R2.6 |
| Every set must go to failure | Not needed in the trials; near failure is enough | R2.10 |
| More is always better | Diminishing returns, especially for strength | R2.8 |
| Early gains mean your muscles grew | Early gains are largely neural | R2.4 |
| Women get bulky / gain less | Effect sizes similar, upper body larger in women, in short studies (Roberts); relative strength gains greater in women (Hubal) | R2.14, R5.15, R2.12 |
| Older people can't gain strength | Large gains at 65+, 75+ and 80+ | R2.14 |
| Cardio kills gains | Not for maximal strength or size; possibly for explosive power | R2.15, R5.23 |
| Spot reduction works | Three tests and a meta-analysis say no | R5.11 to R5.13 |
| Max heart rate is 220 minus age | Never derived from a study; any formula misses a person by about ±20 | R3.3a to c |
| My watch knows my calories | Errors 27% to 93%; heart rate is mostly acceptable | R3.9 |
| Lactic acid causes next-day soreness | DOMS is attributed to muscle damage from unaccustomed or eccentric work | R3.10, R4.16 |
| If you're not sore it didn't work | Soreness tracks novelty, not growth (by inference; labelled as such) | R4.16, R5.16 |
| Stretch before exercise to prevent injury | Not shown; long static holds cost a few per cent of performance | R4.12, R4.13 |
| Lifting makes you stiff | Resistance training improves range of motion about as much as stretching | R4.14 |
| Never let knees pass the toes | Moves load from knee to hip; seven men | R4.8 |
| Never increase running by more than 10% a week | The trial found no difference | R3.8d |
| Ice baths help you grow | Regular cold water after lifting reduced gains in one trial | R4.17 |
| Children shouldn't lift | Supervised youth resistance training is supported | R4.22 |
| People with back pain should rest | Exercise therapy helps chronic low back pain | R4.23 |
| Pregnant women shouldn't lift | ACOG and the Canadian guideline encourage it absent contraindications | R4.20 |

## Contested questions and the positions on each

Each part gives each side in its own proponents' words; the classification under standards 3.1 is
given there. The ones a lesson will meet:

1. **Does activity cause longer life?** Contested empirical (R1, contested 1). Only one sceptic's own
   statement read (Karvinen, R1.10); a lesson says so.
2. **How much strength training is enough, and is there a ceiling?** Contested, low certainty (R1,
   contested 2; R2.8).
3. **Training closer to failure for muscle** (R2, contested 1).
4. **Periodisation for strength** (R2, contested 2).
5. **Non-responders**, resistance (R2, contested 3) and aerobic (R3, contested 2).
6. **Does muscle growth cause strength gain?** (R2, contested 4; R2.19). A researchers' dispute; a
   lesson may name it and should not resolve it.
7. **HIIT for the public** (R3, contested 1). Efficacy agreed; effectiveness and priority contested.
8. **Zone 2** (R3, contested 3). **No proponent statement was read**; a lesson that mentions it says so.
9. **Lumbar flexion when lifting** (R4, contested 1). The caution view read at abstract level only.
10. **Static stretching before training** (R4, contested 2). Largely settled in the middle.
11. **Lifting belts** (R4, contested 3). No gym evidence read; a lesson says so or leaves belts out.
12. **Optimise or simplify?** (R2, contested 6). Partly a value question about public-health
    priorities; ACSM 2026 states its view "from our perspective", and a lesson labels it as a view.

## Best existing free resources (to link, not duplicate)

R5 Part F (NHS, CDC, ExRx, NSCA, ACSM brochure dated 2013, Stronger By Science), R5 Part G (seven
YouTube videos checked through oEmbed: NSCA squat, deadlift and inverted row; NHS standing press-up;
Mayo Clinic knee push-ups and warm-up; TED-Ed on muscle growth with a caveat; the NHS five-minute
warm-up cannot be embedded, so link it), R5 Part H (five Wikimedia Commons images with licences), the
PAR-Q+ (R4.2: link it, never reproduce it, because its terms forbid modification), and NHS Couch to 5K
(R3.8a).

## Safety-critical guidance

**The callout.** Every lesson carries the same callout, directly after the opening, word for word,
enforced by `scripts/build.mjs` in the form Mental Fitness and Nutrition use. It is built only from
the wording recorded in R4.1 (ACSM's signs and symptoms and its disease categories), R4.3 (AHA 2023,
ADA 2016, NIA, NHS), R4.4 (the exertional rhabdomyolysis signs) and R4.20 (ACOG's clinical evaluation
before an exercise programme in pregnancy):

> :::callout Before you train
> This course is education, not advice about your own body. If you have heart, kidney or metabolic disease such as diabetes, are pregnant, or have symptoms like chest discomfort, fainting or unusual breathlessness, talk to a doctor before you start or step up training. Stop and get medical help straight away for pain or pressure in the chest, neck, jaw or arms, dizziness, palpitations, or breathlessness out of all proportion to the effort, and call your local emergency number for chest pain. Cola-coloured urine, or muscle pain and swelling far worse than the session explains, needs a doctor the same day.
> :::

**Other safety lines a competent instructor would give, each with its source:**
- **Novelty plus volume plus eccentric work** is the rhabdomyolysis pattern: a first hard class,
  hundreds of unaccustomed repetitions (R4.4). The beginner lessons say "start below what you could
  do".
- **Breath-holding under load raises blood pressure sharply** (R4.10); people with hypertension get
  the guidance recorded there.
- **Pregnancy**: ACOG's Box 3 warning signs to stop (R4.20), and no lying flat on the back if it
  makes you unwell.
- **Low bone mass**: LIFTMOR's heavy lifting was supervised, in otherwise healthy women; AHA advises
  low to moderate loads in severe osteoporosis (R4.21). Keep the scope attached.
- **Children**: supervision is the condition attached to the permission (R4.22).
- **Diabetes**: hypoglycaemia with glucose-lowering medicines, and silent ischaemia (R4.3, R4.24).
- **The PAR-Q+**: a "yes" means a conversation with a doctor or qualified exercise professional
  before becoming much more active (R4.2).

## Open concerns about scope

- **This is a practical course and it will teach people to train.** Unlike Nutrition's "no
  prescriptions" rule, a strength course that will not show a beginner how to squat or how to write
  a first programme fails its own row in the taxonomy ("Programming, technique, progression"). The
  resolution for Stage 2: the course teaches the general-population guidance of named bodies (WHO,
  ACSM 2026, the NHS) and shows how to build and progress a plan within it, labels every choice with
  what it rests on, and never prescribes for a medical condition, a pregnancy or a child. Those get the
  callout and a pointer.
- **Most trials were read at abstract level**, because Springer, BJSM, LWW and Wiley refused scripted
  downloads. Lessons state the read level at the point of use; where a lesson wants a detail below
  the abstract, it says the course did not read that part.
- **ACSM 2009 and Garber 2011 were read at abstract level only.** No lesson prints %HRR intensity bands
  from Garber (R3, unread list). The 2026 ACSM stand was read in full and is the one to teach.
- **Epley and Brzycki** 1RM formulas come from a preprint's review (R2.18), not from the originals;
  a lesson that uses one says it is an estimate and where the formula reached the course from.
- **The creatine stand has declared industry conflicts** (R2.20), and Nutrition's lesson 10 owns
  supplements. This course names creatine at most once, with the conflict stated, or leaves it out.
- **Technique cannot be learned from text**, which is why the videos matter; the lessons describe
  what to feel for and what each cue rests on, and send the reader to a demonstration.
- **Textbooks were read at table-of-contents level only** (R5.1 to R5.4). They set the sequence; no
  lesson cites one for a claim.
